Totally convex functions, L 2 L^{2} -Optimal transport for laws of random measures, and solution to the Monge problem
Alessandro Pinzi, Giuseppe Savaré

Abstract
We study the Optimal Transport problem for laws of random measures in the Kantorovich-Wasserstein space 𝒫 2 ​ ( 𝒫 2 ​ ( H ) ) {\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})) , associated with a Hilbert space H {\mathrm{H}} (with finite or infinite dimension) and for the corresponding quadratic cost induced by the squared Wasserstein metric in 𝒫 2 ​ ( H ) . {\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}). Despite the lack of smoothness of the cost, the fact that the space 𝒫 2 ​ ( H ) {\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}) is not Hilbertian, and the curvature distortion induced by the underlying Wasserstein metric, we will show how to recover at the level of random measures in 𝒫 2 ​ ( 𝒫 2 ​ ( H ) ) {\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})) the same deep and powerful results linking Euclidean Optimal Transport problems in 𝒫 2 ​ ( H ) {\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}) and convex analysis. Our approach relies on the notion of totally convex functionals, on their total subdifferentials, and their Lagrangian liftings in the space square integrable H {\mathrm{H}} -valued maps L 2 ​ ( Q , 𝕄 , H ) . L^{2}({\mathrm{Q}},\mathbb{M};{\mathrm{H}}). With these tools, we identify a natural class of regular measures in 𝒫 2 ​ ( 𝒫 2 ​ ( H ) ) {\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})) for which the Monge formulation of the OT problem has a unique solution and we will show that this class includes relevant examples of measures with full support in 𝒫 2 ​ ( H ) {\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}) arising from the push-forward transformation of nondegenerate Gaussian measures in L 2 ​ ( Q , 𝕄 , H ) . L^{2}({\mathrm{Q}},\mathbb{M};{\mathrm{H}}).



1
Introduction

One of the most elegant and fascinating aspects of Optimal Transport Theory

[
RR98
,
Vil09
]

for the classical quadratic cost
{\mathsf{c}}(x,y):=\frac{1}{2}|x-y|^{2}
in

\mathbb{R}^{d}
is its link with convex analysis,
which has been thoroughly exploited
by Knott-Smith, Rachev-Rüschendorf, and Brenier

[
KS84
,
RR90
,
Bre91
]

(see also

[
CM89
]
).
If
\mu,\nu
belong to the space

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
of probability measures with finite quadratic moment

{\mathsf{m}}_{2}^{2}(\mu):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x),

(1.1)

is in fact possible to prove that
a coupling
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})

with marginals
\mu,\nu

(we say that
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in\Gamma(\mu,\nu)
) is optimal for the
L^{2}
-Kantorovich-Wasserstein metric

{\mathsf{w}}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu):=\min\Big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x-y|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}:{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in\Gamma(\mu,\nu)\Big\}=\min\Big\{\mathbb{E}\big[|X-Y|^{2}\big]:X\sim\mu,\ Y\sim\nu\Big\}

(1.2)

if and only if its support
S:=\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits({\boldsymbol{\gamma}})\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}

is cyclically monotone, i.e.

\begin{gathered}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\langle y_{n},x_{n}-x_{\sigma(n)}\rangle\geq 0\quad\text{for every $N\in\mathbb{N}$, every choice of }(x_{n},y_{n})\in S\\
\text{ and every permutation $\sigma\in{\mathrm{S}}_{N}$}.\end{gathered}

(1.3)

The dual formulation of (
1.2
)
and the fact that every cyclically monotone subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
is
contained in the graph of the convex subdifferential

\partial\varphi

of a convex and lower semicontinuous function

\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}

implies that we can find optimal conjugate Kantorovich potentials

\varphi,\varphi^{{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{$\ast$}}}}}
such that
for all optimal couplings
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\rm opt}

solving (
1.2
)
the support of

{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\rm opt}
is contained in the graph of
\partial\phi
and

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}x\cdot y\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\rm opt}(x,y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi^{{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{$\ast$}}}}}(y)\,{\mathrm{d}}\nu(y).

(1.4)

The simple but crucial link between
the first integral in (
1.4
)
and the Optimal Transport problem
(
1.2
)
is guaranteed by the specific property of the Euclidean norm in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, for which

{\mathsf{w}}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)={\mathsf{m}}_{2}^{2}(\mu)+{\mathsf{m}}_{2}^{2}(\nu)-2[\mu,\nu],\quad[\mu,\nu]:=\max_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in\Gamma(\mu,\nu)}\int x\cdot y\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}},

(1.5)

so that the minimum problem (
1.2
)
and the maximum problem (
1.5
)
defining
[\mu,\nu]
share the same class of
optimizers.

Since the subdifferential of a convex function
\varphi

is a singleton at every differentiability point of
\varphi

and the set of non-Gateux-differentiability points of a convex Lipschitz function can be covered by a countable union of
d.c. hypersurfaces
[
Zaj79
]
(a result which holds even in infinite dimensional Hilbert spaces),
one can prove that when
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

does not give mass to
d.c. hypersurfaces
(we say that
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

is
regular
)
there exists a unique optimal coupling
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\rm opt}

which is moreover concentrated on the graph of a Borel map
f
, thus satisfying
f_{\sharp}\mu=\nu
.
The class of regular measures
{\mathcal{P}}^{r}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
coincide with
the class of atomless measures when
d=1

and contains all the measures absolutely continuous with respect to the
d
-dimensional Lebesgue measure
{\mathcal{L}}^{d}
for every dimension
d
.

This remarkable combination of analytic and geometric
arguments yields the solution of the Monge formulation of (
1.2
)-(
1.5
),
i.e. the existence of an optimal transport map
f
such that

f_{\sharp}\mu=\nu,\quad{\mathsf{w}}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|f(x)-x|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x),\quad[\mu,\nu]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)\cdot x\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x).

(1.6)

The convex theory for random measures

Whenever
({\mathrm{X}},{\mathsf{d}}_{\mathrm{X}})
is
a (complete, separable) metric space,
the construction of the
L^{2}
-Kantorovich-Wasserstein metric can be naturally extended to

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{X}})
, the space of
probability measures on
{\mathrm{X}}
with finite quadratic moment;
the resulting (squared) metric

{\mathsf{W}}_{2,{\mathsf{d}}_{\mathrm{X}}}^{2}(\mu,\nu):=\min\Big\{\int_{{\mathrm{X}}\times{\mathrm{X}}}{\mathsf{d}}_{\mathrm{X}}^{2}(x,y)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}:{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in\Gamma(\mu,\nu)\Big\}=\min\Big\{\mathbb{E}\big[{\mathsf{d}}^{2}_{\mathrm{X}}(X,Y)\big]:X\sim\mu,\ Y\sim\nu\Big\}

(1.7)

inherits relevant geometric properties from the underlying space
{\mathrm{X}}
and the problem still admits a dual
formulation involving Kantorovich potentials.

Since
({\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),{\mathsf{w}}_{2})

is a complete and separable metric space,
a nice example of applications of the
metric perspective is
provided by
the possibility to lift
Optimal Transport problems
at the level of the laws of the so-called
random measures, i.e. probability measures
in the space

{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}):={\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))

endowed with
the Kantorovich-Wasserstein metric

{\mathsf{W}}_{2}:={\mathsf{W}}_{2,{\mathsf{w}}_{2}}

\displaystyle{\mathsf{W}}_{2}^{2}({\mathtt{M}},{\mathtt{N}}):={}

\displaystyle\min\Big\{\int_{{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}{\mathsf{w}}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)\,{\mathrm{d}}\text{${\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}$}(\mu,\nu):\text{${\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}$}\in\Gamma({\mathtt{M}},{\mathtt{N}})\Big\}

(1.8)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\min\Big\{\mathbb{E}\big[{\mathsf{w}}_{2}^{2}(M,N)\big]:M\sim{\mathtt{M}},\ N\sim{\mathtt{N}}\Big\},\quad{\mathtt{M}},{\mathtt{N}}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(1.9)

A similar class of problems,
starting however from a
smooth and compact Riemannian manifold instead of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, have recently been studied by

[
EP25
]
.
Here we want to focus on the Euclidean case
(also including infinite dimensional separable Hilbert spaces), which shows
distinguished and remarkable features
and has recently attracted
a lot of attention in view
of many interesting applications

[
BVK25
,
FHS23
,
Acc+25
,
PS25
,
CL24
]
.

It is well known that in general metric spaces
({\mathrm{X}},{\mathsf{d}}_{\mathrm{X}})

(as, in particular,
({\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),{\mathsf{w}}_{2})
)
the link between Optimal Transport problems and convex analysis
is typically lost, mainly due to the possible lack of a linear structure in
{\mathrm{X}}
;
even when

{\mathrm{X}}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
but the
metric cost is
induced by a non-Hilbertian norm
\|\cdot\|
,
convexity and Legendre duality of Kantorovich potentials do not hold, since the optimality condition
and the structure of optimal transport maps involve the
nonlinear
differential associated with
\frac{1}{2}\|\cdot\|^{2}.

Even if
({\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),{\mathsf{w}}_{2})
is a genuine
metric space which is positively curved in the sense of Alexandrov

[
AGS08
]
,
the aim of the present paper is to show that

a large part of the convexity landscape of the Euclidean case remarkably holds also
for the Optimal Transport problem
(
1.8
)
for laws of random measures in
{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

if we use the appropriate notion
of
total displacement
convexity in

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

i.e. convexity
along interpolation curves induced by arbitrary couplings.

Such a nice and somehow unexpected
result relies on two important properties.
First of all,
as for (
1.5
),
the lifted Wasserstein metric
{\mathsf{W}}_{2}

given by (
1.8
)
still satisfies a similar identity

\begin{gathered}{\mathsf{W}}_{2}^{2}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})={\mathsf{M}}_{2}^{2}({\mathtt{M}}_{1})+{\mathsf{M}}_{2}^{2}({\mathtt{M}}_{2})-2[\kern-2.0pt[{\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2}]\kern-2.0pt],\\
{\mathsf{M}}_{2}^{2}({\mathtt{M}})=\int{\mathsf{m}}_{2}^{2}(\mu)d{\mathtt{M}}(\mu),\quad[\kern-2.0pt[{\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2}]\kern-2.0pt]=\max_{{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}\in\Gamma({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})}\int[\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]\,{\mathrm{d}}{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2}),\end{gathered}

(1.10)

so that we can study
the equivalent formulation in terms of
the maximization of the function
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\mapsto[\mu_{1},\mu_{2}].

Even if
[\cdot,\cdot]
is not bilinear,
it exhibits many analogies with
a scalar product, in particular
along displacement interpolation of measures,
i.e. curves obtained by
general couplings
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in{\mathcal{P}}(R^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})

via the dynamic push forward

\mu_{t}=(\pi^{1\shortrightarrow 2}_{t})_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\quad\pi^{1\shortrightarrow 2}_{t}(x_{1},x_{2}):=(1-t)x_{1}+tx_{2},\quad t\in[0,1],\quad{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}).

(1.11)

When
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
is an optimal coupling
between
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}

for the Wasserstein metric (
1.2
)
the curve
t\mapsto\mu_{t}
is in fact
a minimal, constant speed geodesic
in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(which we call

optimal displacement interpolation
)
and plays a crucial
geometric role
in the Optimal Transport setting,
since the pioneering paper of McCann

[
McC97
]
.
In particular a function
\phi:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
is called
geodesically (or displacement) convex
if for every
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

there exists a
geodesic
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}

connecting
\mu_{0}
to
\mu_{1}
such that

t\mapsto\phi(\mu_{t})
is convex in
[0,1].

A more restricted class of functions
are in fact convex along

any
displacement interpolation, induced by arbitrary couplings

{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})

as in (
1.11
),
thus satisfying

\phi\big((\pi^{1\shortrightarrow 2}_{t})_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\big)\leq(1-t)\phi\big(\mu_{0})+t\phi\big(\mu_{1}\big)\quad\text{for every }\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\ {\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}).

(1.12)

Such a class of
totally displacement convex
functionals, thoroughly studied in
[
CSS23
]
, enjoys better properties.
Starting from the fact
that
(see also the inspiring notes by
Brenier
[
Bre20
]
)

\mu\mapsto[\mu,\nu]\quad\text{is totally convex for every $\nu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$},

(1.13)

we can introduce the
Kantorovich-Legendre-Fenchel transform

\phi^{\star}(\nu):=\sup_{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}[\nu,\mu]-\phi(\mu)

(1.14)

and prove that
proper, totally convex, and lower semicontinuous functions are characterized by
the identity
\phi=\phi^{\star\star}

as for convex functions in Euclidean spaces
(see Section
3.1
).

The Rockafellar type
transformation
(
1.14
)
is a typical technique
in Optimal Transport (where it is applied
to the concave version of the potentials
and it is called
{\mathsf{c}}
-transform).
What distinguishes the Euclidean
and the current random-Euclidean setting
is the possibility to characterize

{\mathsf{c}}
-transforms and self-biconjugate functions in a simple
way using convexity.

This remarkable property
allows us to retrace the same strategy as
in the finite-dimensional Euclidean theory and has
relevant applications:

1.

it provides an intrinsic
characterization for the optimal Kantorovich potentials
associated with
the Wasserstein metric
(
1.8
)
(Section
3.2
):
they coincide with the class of totally convex functionals;

2.

it allows for a deeper understanding
of the Wasserstein (total) subdifferential
of
\phi

[
AGS08
,
CSS23
]
, interpreted as
a

Multivalued Probability Vector Field
(Section
4
);

3.

it clarifies the structure of optimal couplings
and minimal geodesics
(Sections
5.2
,

5.3
)

4.

it suggests a general lifting strategy to the
L^{2}
-space of Lagrangian maps, importing a Hilbertian perspective for regularity
of laws of random measures
(Section
6.2
)
which plays a crucial role in
proving uniqueness for solution
to the Monge formulation.

Let us briefly summarize the main points:
combining Kantorovich duality and
total displacement convexity, we will prove that for every
{\mathtt{M}},{\mathtt{N}}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

there exists a pair of conjugate totally convex function
\phi,\phi^{\star}:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}

such that

\int\phi(\mu)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathtt{M}}(\mu)+\int\phi^{\star}(\nu)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathtt{N}}(\nu)=[\kern-2.0pt[{\mathtt{M}},{\mathtt{N}}]\kern-2.0pt].

(1.15)

We will then show that
there is a natural correspondence between
optimal couplings
{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}\in\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}},{\mathtt{N}})

minimizing
(
1.8
)
and random coupling laws

{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})

which can be used to characterize
{\mathsf{W}}_{2}
as

{\mathsf{W}}_{2}^{2}({\mathtt{M}},{\mathtt{N}})=\min\bigg\{\int\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x-y|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(x,y)\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{{\mathtt{P}}}({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}):(\pi^{1}_{\sharp})_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}={\mathtt{M}},\ (\pi^{2}_{\sharp})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}}={\mathtt{N}}\bigg\}.

(1.16)

Random couplings are optimal
if and only if their support is contained
in the so-called total subdifferential

\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi

of the optimal potential

\phi
in (
1.15
), a closed subset of

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Optimality can also be characterized by
total cyclical monotonicity and in particular
implies that optimal random couplings are concentrated
on the subset
{\mathcal{P}}_{2,o}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})

of the usual optimal couplings in
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}.

As for the classic subdifferentials of convex functions, among all the elements of
the total subdifferential

\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi
there is a minimal distinguished one (called the minimal section
and denoted by
\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}^{\circ}\phi
)
which can be represented by
a nonlocal deterministic field

{\boldsymbol{f}}^{\circ}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}:

{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}^{\circ}\phi(\mu)\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=({\boldsymbol{i}}\times{\boldsymbol{f}}^{\circ}(\cdot,\mu)_{\sharp}\mu.

(1.17)

Total cyclical monotonicity
can be more easily understood in the case of
{\boldsymbol{f}}^{\circ}
, where it reads as

\begin{gathered}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\int{\langle}{\boldsymbol{f}}^{\circ}(x_{n},\mu_{n}),x_{n}-x_{\sigma(n)}{\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N})\geq 0\\
\text{for every }\mu_{i}\in D(\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi),\ {\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\Gamma(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\cdots,\mu_{N}),\ \sigma\in{\mathrm{S}}_{N}=\operatorname{Sym}(\{1,\cdots,N\}).\end{gathered}

(1.18)

As a byproduct, when
\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi[\mu]
reduces to a singleton for
{\mathtt{M}}
-a.e.
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
the OT problem (
1.8
)
has a unique solution
{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}_{o}
which
is also concentrated on a Monge map

\mathscr{F}:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}=(\mathrm{Id}\times\mathscr{F})_{\sharp}{\mathtt{M}}
.
Moreover,

\mathscr{F}
can be expressed
as a push-forward via
{\boldsymbol{f}}^{\circ}
:

\mathscr{F}(\mu)={\boldsymbol{f}}^{\circ}(\cdot,\mu)_{\sharp}\mu,\quad{\mathsf{W}}_{2}^{2}({\mathtt{M}},{\mathtt{N}})=\int{\mathsf{w}}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\mathscr{F}(\mu))\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathtt{M}}(\mu)=\int\bigg(\int|{\boldsymbol{f}}^{\circ}(x,\mu)-x|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\bigg)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathtt{M}}(\mu).

(1.19)

It turns out that
{\boldsymbol{f}}^{\circ}
solves
the strict Monge formulation
of (
1.8
)

\inf\bigg\{\int\bigg(\int|{\boldsymbol{f}}(x,\mu)-x|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\bigg)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathtt{M}}(\mu):{\boldsymbol{f}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d},\ {\mathtt{N}}=\int\delta_{{\boldsymbol{f}}(\cdot,\mu)_{\sharp}\mu}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathtt{M}}(\mu)\bigg\}.

(1.20)

Lagrangian parametrizations, Hilbertian liftings, and laws of Gaussian-generated random measures

The above discussion raises the crucial question to find general condition on
{\mathtt{M}}
ensuring that
\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi
is concentrated on a singleton
{\mathtt{M}}
-a.e.
A similar problem has also been considered in

[
EP25
]
by assuming
suitable regularity properties on the Dirichlet form associated with
{\mathtt{M}}
in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(M)
, in particular the Rademacher property studied in
[
Del20
]
.

Here we adopt a different perspective,
inspired by the crucial fact that
Optimal Kantorovich potentials are totally convex on

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, i.e. convex along arbitrary couplings as in (
1.12
).
It is then possible to apply a natural
Lions-Lagrangian lifting technique
that has been systematically studied in

[
CSS23
]
in the context of convex analysis and evolution problems
(but see also the relevant notions
of
{\mathrm{L}}
-convexity,
{\mathrm{L}}
-monotonicity

[
Car13
,
CD18
]
,
Fréchet differentiability

[
GT19
]
,
and the discussion
of
[
CSS23
, Remark 5.4]
).

The main idea is to introduce a standard Borel space

({\mathrm{Q}},{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathrm{Q}})
endowed with
a nonatomic probability measure
\mathbb{M}

which can represent every
measure of
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

as the law
X_{\sharp}\mathbb{M}

of a map
X

in the Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}:=L^{2}({\mathrm{Q}},\mathbb{M};\mathbb{R}^{d}).

The law map
\iota=\iota_{\mathbb{M}}:X\mapsto X_{\sharp}\mathbb{M}

is a
1
-Lipschitz surjection
from
{\mathcal{H}}
to
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

and total convexity of
\phi
in

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is equivalent to
the usual convexity of
\hat{\phi}:=\phi\circ\iota
in the Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}.
Such a correspondence also holds at the levels of the respective subdifferentials,
so that the measures
\mu
where
\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi[\mu]
reduces to a singleton
correspond to maps
X\in{\mathcal{H}}
where

\hat{\phi}
is Gateux-differentiable.

Since every measure
{\mathtt{M}}
on

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
can be obtained as the push-forward
\iota_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{m}}

of a measure
{\mathfrak{m}}
on
{\mathcal{H}}
,
it is tempting to lift the problem of
{\mathtt{M}}
-a.e. differentiability in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

to the problem of
{\mathfrak{m}}
-a.e. differentiability of
\hat{\phi}
in
{\mathcal{H}}
,
for which many powerful result are known.
In particular,
d.c. hypersurfaces in a Hilbert space are
Gaussian null

[
Aro76
,
Phe78
,
Bog84
,
Csö99
]
), i.e. are negligible
w.r.t. every nondegenerate Gaussian measure.

We will systematically pursue this direction, showing that the strict Monge problem in
{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
has a unique solution
if
{\mathtt{M}}
satisfies two conditions:

(R1)

it is concentrated on
the set of

regular
measures
{\mathcal{P}}^{r}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, i.e.
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
{\mathtt{M}}
-a.e.
\mu
;

(R2)

{\mathtt{M}}(B)=0
on
every Borel set
B\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

such that
\iota^{-1}(B)

is contained in a d.c. hypersurface of the Hilbert space

{\mathcal{H}}=L^{2}({\mathrm{Q}},\mathbb{M};\mathbb{R}^{d}).

We call
{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r\kern-1.0ptr}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the
set of
super-regular measures
satisfying the two conditions above; it is worth noticing that
the first condition has also been assumed by

[
EP25
]
, whereas the second one
is strongly related to the lifting procedure.
We will show that
those conditions are nearly optimal if
we look for measures
{\mathtt{M}}
for which
the usual Monge formulation has
at least one solution for every target measure
{\mathtt{N}}
. Both conditions are stable if we replace
{\mathtt{M}}
by
{\mathtt{M}}^{\prime}\ll{\mathtt{M}}
.

A simple way to construct measures
satisfying (R2) is to
start from a regular measure
{\mathfrak{m}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathcal{H}})
and
taking its push forward
{\mathtt{M}}=\iota_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{m}}
.
We will focus on the relevant case
of Laws of Gaussian-Generated Random Measures (LGGRM), i.e. measures
in
{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
of the form

{\mathtt{G}}=\iota_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{g}}

where
{\mathfrak{g}}
is a non-degenerate Gaussian measure in
{\mathcal{H}}

[
Bog98
]
.
They have full support in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(R^{d})

and will satisfy condition (R2).
We will show that
every
LGGRM
in dimension
d=1
is super-regular
and we will exhibit a large class
of super-regular
LGGRM in every dimension.
As a byproduct, we obtain that the class
of super-regular measures is dense in

{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

We have developed our analysis in the
case of the “Euclidean”
2
-Wasserstein metric, since we believe that its distinguished features deserve a separate analysis.
Since (finite) dimension play a role only in the final discussion of super-regular measures,
we decided to develop our theory in an arbitrary separable Hilbert space
{\mathrm{H}}
,
even if all the results are new also in finite dimension.
In addition, we think that many tools we have introduced in this paper may be useful to study
the more general case of the
L^{p}
-Wasserstein metric induced by a smooth norm in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We are also confident that
the class of LGGRM measures may reveal
interesting features from the viewpoint of
the induced Dirichlet form in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(see also
[
FSS23
]
), and we plan to address both questions in a forthcoming paper.

Plan of the paper

After a quick recap of the main notions and notation in Section
2
,
we will deal with totally convex function
and their link with optimal Kantorovich potentials in Section
3
.
The related notions of multivalued probability vector fields and total subdifferentials are developed in Section

4
.

Section

5
is devoted
to applications of these tools to the Optimal Transport problem in
{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
The (strict) Monge formulation, its solution
for super-regular measures, and the discussion of the relevant examples
is presented in the last section

6
.

2
Notation and preliminary results

f

;

,

in

,

, the lifting

, Def.

,

;

,

[\kern-2.0pt[\cdot,\cdot]\kern-2.0pt]

induced by the maximal correlation pairing
[\cdot,\cdot]
,

{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r\kern-1.0ptr}({\mathrm{H}})

{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
, Def.
6.6
;

If
{\mathrm{X}}
is a Polish topological space (i.e. it is separable and its
topology is induced by a complete metric)
we denote by
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathrm{X}})
the space of Borel probability measures on

{\mathrm{X}}
endowed with the weak (Polish) topology in duality with continuous and
bounded real functions.
Given a Borel map
between Polish spaces

f:{\mathrm{X}}\to{\mathrm{Y}}
and
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathrm{X}})

we denote by
f_{\sharp}\mu
the image measure
\mu\circ f^{-1}
given by

f_{\sharp}\mu(B):=\mu(f^{-1}(B))
for every Borel subset
B
of
{\mathrm{Y}}
.
In the case of a cartesian product
of Polish spaces

\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{X}}}:=\Pi_{i=1}^{N}{\mathrm{X}}^{i}

we will denote by
\pi^{i}:\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{X}}}\to{\mathrm{X}}^{i}
the map

\pi^{i}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}):=x_{i}
, and similarly

\pi^{ij}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}):=(x_{i},x_{j})
.

A probability kernel is a Borel map
\kappa:{\mathrm{X}}\ni x\mapsto\kappa_{x}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathrm{Y}})
; if

\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathrm{X}})
there exists a unique Borel probability measure

\mu\otimes\kappa=\int_{{\mathrm{X}}\times{\mathrm{Y}}}\delta_{x}\otimes\kappa_{x}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathrm{X}}\times{\mathrm{Y}})
which satisfies

\int_{{\mathrm{X}}\times{\mathrm{Y}}}f(x,y)\,{\mathrm{d}}(\mu\otimes\kappa)(x,y):=\int_{{\mathrm{X}}}\Big(\int_{\mathrm{Y}}f(x,y)\,{\mathrm{d}}\kappa_{x}(y)\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)

(2.1)

for every bounded (or nonnegative) real Borel function
f
defined in

{\mathrm{X}}\times{\mathrm{Y}}
.

Elements of
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathrm{X}}\times{\mathrm{Y}})
are also called

couplings

or
transport plans
.
The universal disintegration Theorem
[
Kal17
, Corollary 1.26]

says that
there exists a Borel map

{\mathcal{K}}:{\mathrm{X}}\times{\mathcal{P}}({\mathrm{X}}\times{\mathrm{Y}})\to{\mathcal{P}}({\mathrm{Y}})

such that

\kappa_{x}:={\mathcal{K}}(x,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})
provides a disintegration of
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}

with respect to its first marginal, i.e.

{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\mu\otimes\kappa,\quad\mu:=\pi^{1}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}},\quad\kappa_{x}:={\mathcal{K}}(x,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})\quad x\in{\mathrm{X}},\quad\text{for every }{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathrm{X}}\times{\mathrm{Y}}).

(2.2)

We will denote by
{{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm det}}({\mathrm{X}}\times{\mathrm{Y}})
the set of
deterministic couplings:

{{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm det}}({\mathrm{X}}\times{\mathrm{Y}}):=\Big\{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathrm{X}}\times{\mathrm{Y}}):\ \exists f:{\mathrm{X}}\to{\mathrm{Y}}\text{ Borel, such that }{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=(\mathrm{Id}\times f)_{\sharp}\mu,\ \mu=\pi^{1}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\Big\}.

(2.3)

Since disintegrations are uniquely defined almost everywhere
with respect to the marginal, we also have

\begin{gathered}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in{{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm det}}({\mathrm{X}}\times{\mathrm{Y}})\quad\text{if and only
if}\quad{\mathcal{K}}(\cdot,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})\in{\mathcal{P}}_{\delta}({\mathrm{Y}})\text{
$\pi^{1}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}$-a.e.}\\
\text{where}\qquad{\mathcal{P}}_{\delta}({\mathrm{Y}}):=\Big\{\delta_{y}:y\in{\mathrm{Y}}\Big\}.\end{gathered}

(2.4)

It is possible to prove

[
LS25
]
that
{{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm det}}({\mathrm{X}}\times{\mathrm{Y}})

is a
G_{\delta}
(thus Borel) subset of
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathrm{X}}\times{\mathrm{Y}})
.

Given
\mu_{i}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathrm{X}}^{i})
we will denote by

\Gamma(\mu_{1},\cdots,\mu_{N})

the subset of
{\mathcal{P}}(\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{X}}})
whose elements
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
satisfy

\pi^{i}_{\sharp}({\boldsymbol{\mu}})=\mu_{i}
,
i=1,\cdots,N
.

We will often use the following consequence of Von Neumann selection
Theorem
[
Sch73
, Theorem 13, pag. 127]
:

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
{\mathrm{X}},{\mathrm{Y}}
be Polish spaces,

\nu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathrm{Y}})
concentrated on the Borel set
B\subset{\mathrm{Y}}

and let
f:{\mathrm{X}}\to{\mathrm{Y}}
be a Borel map.
If
f({\mathrm{X}})\supset B
then there exists a measure

\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathrm{X}})

such that

\text{$\mu$ is concentrated on $f^{-1}(B)$,}\quad f_{\sharp}\mu=\nu.

(2.5)

2.1
The
L^{2}
-Wasserstein space

Let
({\mathrm{X}},{\mathsf{d}}_{\mathrm{X}})
be a complete and separable metric space and
let
x_{o}
a point of
{\mathrm{X}}
.
We denote by
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{X}})
the space of Borel probability measures on

{\mathrm{X}}

with finite quadratic moment

\int_{{\mathrm{X}}}{\mathsf{d}}_{\mathrm{X}}^{2}(x,x_{o})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)<\infty.

(2.6)

It is easy to check that the definition is independent of the choice
of the reference point
x_{o}
.
The space
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{X}})
can be
endowed with the
L^{2}
-Kantorovich-Wasserstein metric

{\mathsf{W}}_{2,{\mathsf{d}}_{\mathrm{X}}}

{\mathsf{W}}^{2}_{2,{\mathsf{d}}_{\mathrm{X}}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\min\Big\{\int{\mathsf{d}}_{\mathrm{X}}^{2}(x_{0},x_{1})\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(x_{0},x_{1}):{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\Gamma(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\Big\};

(2.7)

we will denote by
\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})

the (compact, non-empty) subset of
\Gamma(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
where
the minimum is attained.
We will also denote by
{\mathcal{P}}_{2,o}({\mathrm{X}}\times{\mathrm{X}})

the set of couplings
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{X}}\times{\mathrm{X}})
such that

{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\Gamma_{o}(\pi^{1}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\pi^{2}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mu}})
.

({\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{X}}),{\mathsf{W}}_{2,{\mathsf{d}}_{\mathrm{X}}})
is a complete and separable
metric space as well

[
AGS08
, Chap. 7]
.
In this paper we will mainly consider three important cases,
when
{\mathrm{X}}={\mathrm{H}}
is a Hilbert space, when
{\mathrm{X}}=L^{2}({\mathrm{Q}},\mathbb{M};{\mathrm{H}})

is the space of
{\mathrm{H}}
-valued
(Bochner) square-integrable Lagrangian maps defined in some probability space
({\mathrm{Q}},\mathbb{M})
, and
when
{\mathrm{X}}={\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
is the Wasserstein space itself.

The Hilbertian case
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
and the maximal correlation pairing.

In this paper we will denote by

{\mathrm{H}}

a separable Hilbert space with
scalar product

{\langle}\cdot,\cdot{\rangle}
, norm
|\cdot|
,
and induced metric
{\mathsf{d}}_{\mathrm{H}}
.
The finite dimensional case
{\mathrm{H}}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
provides an important example
covered by the theory.

The previous construction
applied to
({\mathrm{H}},{\mathsf{d}}_{\mathrm{H}})

yields the space
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
;
the quadratic moment of
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
is

{\mathsf{m}}^{2}_{2}(\mu):=\int_{\mathrm{H}}|x|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)=\int_{\mathrm{H}}{\mathsf{d}}_{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(x,0)d\mu(x),\quad\text{corresponding to the choice of }x_{o}:=0.

(2.8)

To simplify notation, we will simply denote the Wasserstein metric

{\mathsf{W}}_{2,{\mathsf{d}}_{\mathrm{H}}}
by

{\mathsf{w}}_{2}
:

{\mathsf{w}}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\min\Big\{\int_{{\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}}}|x_{0}-x_{1}|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(x_{0},x_{1}):{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\Gamma(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\Big\}.

(2.9)

The Euclidean structure of
{\mathsf{d}}_{\mathrm{H}}
allows for a useful
decomposition of
{\mathsf{w}}_{2}
.

Definition 2.2

(The maximal correlation pairing)
.

For every
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

we set

[\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]:=\max\Big\{\int_{{\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}}}{\langle}x_{0},x_{1}{\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}:{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\Gamma(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\Big\}.

(2.10)

It is clear that
[\cdot,\cdot]
is finite in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

and satisfies

\displaystyle\Big|[\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]\Big|\leq{\mathsf{m}}_{2}(\mu_{1})\,{\mathsf{m}}_{2}(\mu_{2}),

(2.11)

\displaystyle\quad{\mathsf{w}}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})={\mathsf{m}}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{1})+{\mathsf{m}}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{2})-2[\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]\quad\text{for every }\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}).

(2.12)

In particular, a coupling
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
belongs to
\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
if
and only if it attains the maximum in (
2.10
).

The space
{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})={\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}))

Since
({\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}),{\mathsf{w}}_{2})
is a complete and separable metric space,
we can iterate the Wasserstein construction and consider the space

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}))
, which we will also denote by
{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
;
its elements (also called laws of random measures) will be denoted by capital letters
{\mathtt{M}},{\mathtt{N}},\cdots
.

Using
\delta_{0}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
as a reference measure and observing that

{\mathsf{w}}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\delta_{0})={\mathsf{m}}_{2}^{2}(\mu)
,
the quadratic moment in
{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
is

{\mathsf{M}}_{2}^{2}({\mathtt{M}}):=\int_{{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})}{\mathsf{m}}_{2}^{2}(\mu)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathtt{M}}(\mu)=\int_{{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})}\int_{\mathrm{H}}|x|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathtt{M}}(\mu).

(2.13)

The corresponding Wasserstein
metric
{\mathsf{W}}_{2,{\mathsf{w}}_{2}}

will be denoted by
{\mathsf{W}}_{2}
:

{\mathsf{W}}_{2}^{2}({\mathtt{M}}_{0},{\mathtt{M}}_{1}):=\min\Big\{\int_{{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})}{\mathsf{w}}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\,{\mathrm{d}}{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}\in\Gamma({\mathtt{M}}_{0},{\mathtt{M}}_{1})\Big\}.

(2.14)

2.2
Regularity of measures and Monge formulation in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

A particularly relevant case when the optimal coupling
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}

solving (
2.9
) or
(
2.10
) is
unique and deterministic according to (
2.3
), is related to the regularity of (at least one of) the
marginals
\mu_{i}
.
The most refined description of such a regularity is characterized by the vanishing of
the marginal
\mu_{i}

on all the
so-called d.c. (or
\delta
-convex or cc) hypersurfaces, i.e. graphs of difference of convex Lipschitz functions in a suitable coordinate system.
More precisely, we recall that a subset

S\subset{\mathrm{H}}
is a d.c. hypersurface
if we can find a decomposition of
{\mathrm{H}}

in a orthogonal sum

{\mathrm{H}}={\mathrm{E}}\oplus v\mathbb{R}
, where

v\in{\mathrm{H}}
and
{\mathrm{E}}
is the hyperplane of
{\mathrm{H}}
orthogonal to
v
, and two Lipschitz convex functions

f,g:{\mathrm{E}}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

S=\Big\{x+(f(x)-g(x))v:x\in{\mathrm{E}}\Big\}.

(2.15)

When
{\mathrm{H}}=\mathbb{R}
, d.c. hypersurfaces just
reduce to a point.
We say that

B\subset{\mathrm{H}}
is

\sigma
-d.c. hypersurface

if it can be covered by a countable union of d.c. hypersurfaces.

(2.16)

A larger class of
negligible subsets is provided by the so-called
Gaussian-null sets introduced by Phelps in

[
Phe78
]
:
a Borel subset
B
of
{\mathrm{H}}
is Gaussian-null, if

{\mathfrak{g}}(B)=0
for every non-degenerate Gaussian measure
{\mathfrak{g}}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathrm{H}})

(see Section

2.4
below).
Thanks to

[
Csö99
]

the class of Gaussian null Borel sets
coincides with
the
\sigma
-ideals of
cube null Borel sets and
of Aronsajn null Borel sets

[
Aro76
]
(see also

[
Bog84
]
and
the other comparisons and extensions discussed in
[
Bog18
]
).
Since every d.c. hypersurface is Aronsajn null, it
is immediate to check that

\sigma
-d.c. hypersurfaces sets
are Gaussian-null.

Definition 2.3

(Atomless, regular , and G-regular measures)
.

We denote by
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{al}({\mathrm{H}})

the collection of
atomless
probability measures in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
,
thus satisfying

\mu(\{x\})=0
for every
x\in{\mathrm{H}}
.

We denote by
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathrm{H}})

the class of
regular
probability measures that vanish on all d.c. hypersurfaces (and therefore on all

\sigma
-d.c. hypersurfaces).

We denote by
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{gr}({\mathrm{H}})

the class of
G-regular

probability measures, that vanish on
all Gaussian null Borel subsets of

{\mathrm{H}}
.

Remark 2.4
.

It is clear that
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{gr}({\mathrm{H}})\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{al}({\mathrm{H}}).

It is useful to
recall other important relations
and some particular cases
covered by the above definitions.

1.

When
{\mathrm{H}}=\mathbb{R}
is one-dimensional,
the class of atomless and regular measures coincide, i.e.
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}(\mathbb{R})={\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{al}(\mathbb{R})
.

2.

When
{\mathrm{H}}
has finite dimension
d
,

\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{gr}({\mathrm{H}})

if and only if it is absolutely
continuous
with respect to the
d
-dimensional Lebesgue measure.

3.

Since d.c. hypersurfaces are Gaussian null,
it is immediate to check that
the class of G-regular measures

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{gr}({\mathrm{H}})

is included in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathrm{H}}).

Therefore we have

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{gr}({\mathrm{H}})\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathrm{H}})\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{al}({\mathrm{H}}),\qquad{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}(\mathbb{R})={\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{al}(\mathbb{R}).

(2.17)

The crucial property of
regular measures
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathrm{H}})

is that every
convex and Lipschitz function

\varphi:{\mathrm{H}}\to\mathbb{R}
is
Gateaux-differentiable

\mu
-almost everywhere.
This follows by a nice result of
Zajíček
[
Zaj79
]

(see also
[
BL00
, Theorem 4.20]
)
showing that
the set of points where
\varphi:{\mathrm{H}}\to\mathbb{R}

is not Gateaux-differentiable
is contained in an
\sigma
-d.c. hypersurface according to (
2.16
).

Using this property and the structure of optimal couplings,
it is possible to prove the celebrated Brénier Theorem.

Theorem 2.5
.

If
\mu_{1}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathrm{H}}),\mu_{2}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
then

\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
contains a unique element
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}

which is deterministic, i.e. concentrated on the graph of a Borel
map

f\in L^{2}({\mathrm{H}},\mu_{1};{\mathrm{H}})
with
f_{\sharp}\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}
.

We will also be interested to measurability properties
of the above sets. We collect them in the following statement, in the case when
{\mathrm{H}}
has finite dimension.

Proposition 2.6

(Measurability of

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathrm{H}})
and
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{gr}({\mathrm{H}})
)
.

Let us assume that

{\mathrm{H}}
has finite dimension
d.

1.

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathrm{H}})
is a
G_{\delta}
(thus Borel) subset of
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}).

2.

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{gr}({\mathrm{H}})
is a Borel subset of

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}).

Proof.

Claim 1
(
d=1
,
{\mathrm{H}}=\mathbb{R}
).
It is sufficient to note that

\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}(\mathbb{R})
if and only if

\mu\times\mu(D)=0
,
where

D:=\{(x,x):x\in\mathbb{R}\}
is the
(closed) diagonal of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Since the map
\mu\mapsto\mu\times\mu(D)

is upper semicontinuous,
we have

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}(\mathbb{R})=\bigcap_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}\big\{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}):\mu\times\mu(D)>1/k\big\}.

Claim 1
(
{\mathrm{H}}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
d>1
).
Let
{\mathcal{B}}

be the collection of all
the compact boxes

B=\Pi_{k=1}^{d-1}[a_{k},b_{k}]\subset\mathbb{R}^{d-1}

with rational endpoints

a_{k}<b_{k}
, let

{\mathcal{R}}

a dense countable set of rotations
R
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
For every
L\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
B\in{\mathcal{B}}

we consider the set

{\mathrm{C}}(B,L):=\{f:B\to\mathbb{R}:f\text{ convex, $L$-Lipschitz, $\sup_{B}|f|\leq L$}\}.

{\mathrm{C}}(B,L)
is a compact set of the
Polish space
{\mathrm{C}}(B).

For every
g,h\in{\mathrm{C}}(B,L)
and
R\in{\mathcal{R}}

we consider the compact graph

{\mathrm{S}}_{B,R}(g,h):=\Big\{R(y,g(y)-h(y)):y\in B\Big\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Since every
d.c.-hypersurface can be covered by
a countable collections of
sets of the
form

{\mathrm{S}}_{B,R}(g,h)

for
g,h
in some
{\mathrm{C}}(B,L)
,
we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})={}

\displaystyle\bigcap\Big\{G(B,R,L):B\in{\mathcal{B}},\ R\in{\mathcal{R}},L\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\Big\},

\displaystyle G(B,R,L):={}

\displaystyle\Big\{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}):\mu({\mathrm{S}}_{B,R}(g,h))=0\text{ for every }g,h\in{\mathrm{C}}(B,L)\Big\}.

It is then sufficient to show that
each set
G(B,R,L)
is a
G_{\delta}
.

It is not difficult to check that
the map

(\mu,g,h)\mapsto\mu({\mathrm{S}}_{B,R}(g,h))

is jointly upper semicontinuous
in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times({\mathrm{C}}(B,L))^{2}

(uniform convergence in

({\mathrm{C}}(B,L))^{2}
implies
Hausdorff convergence of the compact graphs

{\mathrm{S}}_{B,R}(\cdot,\cdot)
) so that
the function

\mu\mapsto U(\mu):=\sup_{g,h\in{\mathrm{C}}(B,L)}\mu({\mathrm{S}}_{B,R}(g,h)

is upper semicontinuous as well, thanks to
the compactness of
{\mathrm{C}}(B,L).

On the other hand

G(B,R,L)=\bigcap_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}\Big\{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}):U(\mu)<1/k\Big\}

so it is the intersection of
a countable collection of open sets.

Claim 2.

Let
{\mathfrak{g}}
denote the
standard Gaussian measure in
\mathbb{R}^{d}

and let
C
be
the
F_{\sigma}
(thus Borel) set

C:=\Big\{f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},{\mathfrak{g}}):f\geq 0,\ \int f(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathfrak{g}}(x)=1,\ \int|x|^{2}f(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathfrak{g}}(x)<\infty\Big\}

of the Polish space
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d};{\mathfrak{g}}_{d}).

The map

J:C\to{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

defined by

J:f\mapsto f{\mathfrak{g}}
,
is continuous and injective
so that its image

J(C)={\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a Borel subset of

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by Lusin Theorem.
∎

2.3
Lagrangian representations.

Let us consider a standard Borel space
({\mathrm{Q}},{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathrm{Q}})

endowed with a reference
nonatomic Borel probability measure
\mathbb{M}
.
We denote by

{\mathcal{H}}
the Hilbert space

L^{2}({\mathrm{Q}},{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathrm{Q}},\mathbb{M};{\mathrm{H}})
endowed with the scalar product and norm

{\langle}X,Y{\rangle}_{\mathcal{H}}:=\int{\langle}X(q),Y(q){\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{M}(q),\quad\|X\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}:={\langle}X,X{\rangle}_{\mathcal{H}}.

(2.18)

A map
{\mathsf{g}}:{\mathrm{Q}}\to{\mathrm{Q}}
is a
measure preserving
isomorphism
(m.p.i.) if it is
{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathrm{Q}}-{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathrm{Q}}
-measurable, there exists a set of full measure

{\mathrm{Q}}_{0}\subset{\mathrm{Q}}
such that
{\mathsf{g}}\lower 3.0pt\hbox{$|_{{\mathrm{Q}}}$}_{0}
is
injective and
{\mathsf{g}}_{\sharp}\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}
. We denote by
{\mathrm{G}}({\mathrm{Q}})
the
group of measure-preserving isomorphisms.
A m.p.i.
{\mathsf{g}}\in{\mathrm{G}}({\mathrm{Q}})
induces a linear isometry
{\mathsf{g}}^{*}:{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{H}}
of
{\mathcal{H}}
by
the map

{\mathsf{g}}^{*}X:=X\circ{\mathsf{g}}.

(2.19)

There is a natural

1
-Lipschitz surjective map

\iota:{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

given by

\iota(X):=X_{\sharp}\mathbb{M}\quad\text{for every }X\in{\mathcal{H}},

(2.20)

and satisfying

{\mathsf{m}}_{2}(\iota(X))=\|X\|_{\mathcal{H}},\quad{\mathsf{w}}_{2}(\iota(X_{1}),\iota(X_{2}))\leq\|X_{1}-X_{2}\|_{{\mathcal{H}}},\quad{\langle}X_{1},X_{2}{\rangle}_{\mathcal{H}}\leq[\iota(X_{1}),\iota(X_{2})].

(2.21)

Notice that

{\mathsf{g}}\in{\mathrm{G}}({\mathrm{Q}}),\quad Y=X\circ{\mathsf{g}}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\iota(Y)=\iota(X),

(2.22)

i.e.
\iota\circ{\mathsf{g}}^{*}=\iota
for
every
{\mathsf{g}}\in{\mathrm{G}}({\mathrm{Q}})
.
The next Lemma shows that we can considerably refine the previous
inequalities.
We refer to
[
CSS25
, Sect. 3]
for the proof.

Lemma 2.7
.

For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
the map
\iota^{k}:{\mathcal{H}}^{k}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}^{k})
defined by

\iota^{k}(X_{1},X_{2},\cdots,X_{k}):=(X_{1},X_{2},\cdots,X_{k})_{\sharp}\mathbb{M}

(2.23)

is surjective. In particular, for every

{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2,o}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})

there exists a pair
(X_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}},1},X_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}},2})\in{\mathcal{H}}\times{\mathcal{H}}
such that

\iota^{2}(X_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}},1},X_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}},2})={\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
and
for every
(X_{1},X_{2})\in{\mathcal{H}}\times{\mathcal{H}}
we have

\iota^{2}(X_{1},X_{2})\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2,o}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})\ \Leftrightarrow\ \|X_{1}-X_{2}\|_{{\mathcal{H}}}={\mathsf{w}}_{2}(\iota(X_{1}),\iota(X_{2}))\ \Leftrightarrow\ {\langle}X_{1},X_{2}{\rangle}_{\mathcal{H}}=[\iota(X_{1}),\iota(X_{2})].

(2.24)

Moreover, for every
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
and
X_{1},X_{2}\in{\mathcal{H}}

with
\iota(X_{i})=\mu_{i}
we have

[\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]=\sup_{{\mathsf{g}}\in{\mathrm{G}}({\mathrm{Q}})}{\langle}X_{1},{\mathsf{g}}^{*}X_{2}{\rangle},\quad{\mathsf{w}}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\inf_{{\mathsf{g}}\in{\mathrm{G}}({\mathrm{Q}})}\|X_{1}-{\mathsf{g}}^{*}X_{2}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}.

(2.25)

Finally, if
\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}
(i.e.
\iota(X_{1})=\iota(X_{2})
) then there
exists a sequence
{\mathsf{g}}_{n}\in{\mathrm{G}}({\mathrm{Q}})
such that

{\mathsf{g}}_{n}^{*}X_{1}\to X_{2}
strongly in
{\mathcal{H}}
.

Every function
\phi:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
induces a function

\hat{\phi}:=\phi\circ\iota
on
{\mathcal{H}}
which satisfies the obvious
law-invariance property

\iota(X)=\iota(Y)\quad\Rightarrow\quad\hat{\phi}(X)=\hat{\phi}(Y),

(2.26)

and, in particular, is invariant by the action of
measure-preserving isomorphisms:

\hat{\phi}({\mathsf{g}}^{*}X)=\hat{\phi}(X)\quad\text{for every }{\mathsf{g}}\in{\mathrm{G}}({\mathrm{Q}}).

(2.27)

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
\phi:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
and let
\hat{\phi}:=\phi\circ\iota:{\mathcal{H}}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
. We have

1.

\phi
is proper if and only if
\hat{\phi}
is proper.

2.

\phi
is l.s.c. if and only if
\hat{\phi}
is l.s.c.

3.

\phi
is continuous if and only if
\hat{\phi}
is continuous.

4.

\phi
is
L
-Lipschitz if and only if
\hat{\phi}
is
L
-Lipschitz.

Moreover, suppose that
\Phi:{\mathcal{H}}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
is
proper, l.s.c. and invariant by measure preserving isomorphisms,
i.e.
\Phi\circ{\mathsf{g}}^{*}=\Phi
.
Then
\Phi
is law-invariant and
\Phi=\phi\circ\iota
for a
(unique) proper, l.s.c. function
\phi:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
.

Proof.

The first claim and all the left-to-right implications of Claims
2,3,4 are trivial, thanks to (
2.21
). We thus consider only the right-to-left implications.

Concerning Claim 2, let us suppose that
\hat{\phi}
is lower semicontinuous and let

(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converging to
\mu
in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

with
\phi(\mu_{n})\leq c
. By (
2.25
)
there exists a sequence
X_{n}
converging to
X
in
{\mathcal{H}}
such that

\mu_{n}=\iota(X_{n})
and
\mu=\iota(X)
. Since
\hat{\phi}
is lower
semicontinuous we deduce that
\phi(\mu)=\hat{\phi}(X)\leq c
as well.

Claim 3 immediately follows by Claim 2 applied to
\phi
and

-\phi
.
Claim 4 follows from (
2.24
).

Let now
\Phi
be as in the last statement of the Lemma
and let
X_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
with
\iota(X_{1})=\iota(X_{2})
.
By Lemma
2.7
we can find a sequence
{\mathsf{g}}_{n}\in{\mathrm{G}}({\mathrm{Q}})
such that

{\mathsf{g}}_{n}^{*}X_{1}\to X_{2}
as
n\to\infty
so that

\Phi(X_{2})\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\Phi({\mathsf{g}}_{n}^{*}X_{1})=\liminf_{n\to\infty}\Phi(X_{1})=\Phi(X_{1}).

Inverting the role of
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
we deduce that
\Phi(X_{1})=\Phi(X_{2}).

∎

2.4
Lagrangian representation of the laws of random measures of
{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

The
1
-Lipschitz and surjective law map
\iota:{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

provides a natural way
to construct measures
in
{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

(the space of laws of random measures in

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
) starting
from measures in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{H}})

(the space of laws of random Lagrangian maps in
{\mathcal{H}}
).
In fact, the corresponding push-forward transform
\iota_{\sharp}
is
a surjective map from
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{H}})

to
{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
so that

for every
{\mathfrak{m}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{H}})

the push-forward

{\mathtt{M}}=\iota_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{m}}
belongs to

{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}).

(2.28)

In this way, we will use (maps from)
({\mathrm{Q}},\mathbb{M})
as
a sort of “labelling” space for measures
in
{\mathrm{H}}

and not as a source of randomness.
The latter can be introduced by using a further measure
{\mathfrak{m}}
, not in
{\mathrm{Q}}
but in the Hilbert space
L^{2}({\mathrm{Q}},\mathbb{M};{\mathrm{H}})
;
in turn
{\mathfrak{m}}
can be represented
as the law of a random vector
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}

defined in another
(standard Borel)
probability space

(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},\mathbb{P})
.
Thus in many situations
it will be useful to
deal with the pair of spaces

({\mathrm{Q}},{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathrm{Q}},\mathbb{M})
and
(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},\mathbb{P})
.
Such a construction has been used, with different aims, in various contexts,
see e.g.
[
KR24
]
,

[
RS09
,
Stu11
]
.

Let us briefly describe this simple construction: we can assume that

\begin{gathered}\text{the measure ${\mathfrak{m}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{H}})$
is the law of a ${\mathcal{H}}$-valued random vector ${\boldsymbol{\xi}}$ defined
in }(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},\mathbb{P}):\\
\text{${\mathfrak{m}}={\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}$
\quad and
\quad${\mathtt{M}}=(\iota\circ{\boldsymbol{\xi}})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}$}\text{ is the law of the random measure }\mu_{\cdot}={\boldsymbol{\xi}}[\cdot]_{\sharp}\mathbb{M}.\end{gathered}

(2.29)

We first represent
{\mathfrak{m}}
as
the distribution of a
{\mathcal{F}}\otimes{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathrm{Q}}
-measurable stochastic process.

Lemma 2.9
.

If
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}:\Omega\to{\mathcal{H}}

is a Borel vector field satisfying (
2.29
)
then there exists a

{\mathcal{F}}\otimes{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathrm{Q}}
-measurable stochastic process

\Xi:\Omega\times{\mathrm{Q}}\to{\mathrm{H}}

such that

\text{for $\mathbb{P}$-a.e.~$\omega\in\Omega$}\quad\Xi(\omega,\cdot)={\boldsymbol{\xi}}[\omega]\quad\text{$\mathbb{M}$-a.e.~in ${\mathrm{Q}}.$}

(2.30)

Proof.

We select an orthonormal basis

({\mathsf{h}}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
of

{\mathrm{H}}

and set
\xi_{k}:=\langle{\boldsymbol{\xi}},{\mathsf{h}}_{k}\rangle
.

\xi_{k}
is a Borel map from

\Omega
to
L^{2}({\mathrm{Q}},\mathbb{M})

satisfying

\sum_{k}\int\|\xi_{k}[\omega]\|_{L^{2}({\mathrm{Q}},\mathbb{M})}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega)=\int\|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}[\omega]\|_{{\mathcal{H}}}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega)=\int\|X\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathfrak{m}}(X)<\infty.

(2.31)

We can then set
\nu_{k}(\omega,B):=\int\xi_{k}[\omega]{\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{B}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{M}

for every
\omega\in\Omega
and

B\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathrm{Q}}

obtaining a family of signed measures

\nu_{k}(\omega,\cdot)
on
{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathrm{Q}}

depending on
\omega
in a

{\mathcal{F}}
-measurable way, with

|\nu_{k}|(\omega,B)\leq\|\xi_{k}[\omega]\|_{L^{2}({\mathrm{Q}},\mathbb{M})}\mathbb{M}(B)

By the Doob’s measurable Radon-Nykodim theorem

[
DM82
, Thm. 58]

(see also

[
Bog07
, Ex. 6.10.72]
)
we can
find a

{\mathcal{F}}\times{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathrm{Q}}
-measurable density

f_{k}:\Omega\times{\mathrm{Q}}\to\mathbb{R}

such that

\nu_{k}(\omega,\cdot)=f_{k}(\omega,\cdot)\mathbb{M}

so that

\xi_{k}[\omega]=f_{k}(\omega,\cdot)\quad\text{$\mathbb{M}$-a.e.~and}\quad\int f_{k}^{2}(\omega,q)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{M}(q)=\|\xi_{k}[\omega]\|_{L^{2}({\mathrm{Q}},\mathbb{M})}^{2}\quad\text{for
$\mathbb{P}$-a.e.~$\omega\in\Omega.$}

Setting

\Xi_{n}(\omega,q):=\sum_{k=1}^{n}f_{k}(\omega,q){\mathsf{h}}_{k}

and using (
2.31
)
it is not difficult to check that

\Xi_{n}
converges pointwise

\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{M}
-a.e. to
an element
\Xi\in L^{2}(\Omega\times{\mathrm{Q}},\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{M};{\mathrm{H}})

which satisfies (
2.30
).
∎

We can use
\Xi
to respresent
{\mathtt{M}}

and its
k
-projections:
for every
\omega\in\Omega

the measure
\mu_{\omega}=\iota({\boldsymbol{\xi}}(\omega))\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathrm{H}})

satisfies

\int_{\mathrm{H}}\zeta(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\omega}(x)=\int_{\mathrm{Q}}\zeta(\Xi(\omega,q))\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{M}(q)

(2.32)

and we can recover

{\mathtt{M}}
as the law with respect to
\mathbb{P}

of the random measure
\mu_{\omega}
:

{\mathtt{M}}=\int_{\Omega}\delta_{\mu_{\omega}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega)=\int_{\Omega}\delta_{\Xi(\omega,\cdot)_{\sharp}\mathbb{M}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega).

(2.33)

By using
\Xi
we can easily express

k
-projections of
{\mathtt{M}}
.
For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
{\mathtt{M}}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
let us define

\operatorname{pr}^{k}[{\mathtt{M}}]:=\int\mu^{\otimes k}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathtt{M}}(\mu)\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}^{k}),

(2.34)

which satisfies

\int_{{\mathrm{H}}^{k}}\zeta\,{\mathrm{d}}\operatorname{pr}^{k}[{\mathtt{M}}]=\int\Big(\int_{{\mathrm{H}}^{k}}\zeta\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{\otimes k}\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathtt{M}}(\mu)\quad\text{for every bounded Borel }\zeta:{\mathrm{H}}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}.

(2.35)

We then have

\int_{{\mathrm{H}}^{k}}\zeta\,{\mathrm{d}}\operatorname{pr}^{k}[{\mathtt{M}}]=\int_{\Omega}\Big(\int_{{\mathrm{Q}}}\zeta(\Xi(\omega,q_{1}),\cdots,\Xi(\omega,q_{k}))\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{M}^{\otimes k}(q_{1},\cdots,q_{k})\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega),

(2.36)

i.e.

\operatorname{pr}^{k}[{\mathtt{M}}]=\Xi^{k}_{\sharp}(\mathbb{P}\otimes\mathbb{M}^{\otimes k})\quad\text{where}\quad\Xi^{k}(\omega,q_{1},\cdots,q_{k}):=(\Xi(\omega,q_{1}),\cdots,\Xi(\omega,q_{k})).

(2.37)

Example 2.10

(Laws of Gaussian generated random measures (LGRRM))
.

Let us focus on the particular case
of a nondegenerate centered Gaussian measure

{\mathfrak{g}}\sim N(0,K)
in
{\mathcal{H}}

with covariance operator
K
.
By Karhunen-Loève expansion,
we can find

1.

an orthonormal basis
({\mathsf{E}}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
of
{\mathcal{H}}
,
given by the eigenvectors of
K
;

2.

the corresponding sequence of
nonnegative eigenvalues
of
K

(\lambda^{2}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}

with

K{\mathsf{E}}_{n}=\lambda^{2}_{n}{\mathsf{E}}_{n}
and

\Lambda:=\sum_{n}\lambda_{n}^{2}<\infty

3.

a sequence of
independent Gaussian random variables

(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}

defined in
\Omega

with
\xi_{n}\sim N(0,\lambda_{n}^{2})

such that

{\mathfrak{g}}={\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{\sharp}\mathbb{P},\quad{\boldsymbol{\xi}}:=\sum_{n}\xi_{n}{\mathsf{E}}_{n}.

(2.38)

Since

\Xi_{n}(\omega,q):=\xi_{n}(\omega){\mathsf{E}}_{n}(q)

is an orthogonal system in

L^{2}(\Omega\times{\mathrm{Q}},\mathbb{P}\otimes\mathbb{M})

the series

\Xi(\omega,q):=\sum_{n}\Xi_{n}(\omega,q)=\sum_{n}\xi_{n}(\omega){\mathsf{E}}_{n}(q),\quad\|\Xi_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega\times{\mathrm{Q}},\mathbb{P}\otimes\mathbb{M})}=\lambda_{n}

(2.39)

converges in

L^{2}(\Omega\times{\mathrm{Q}},\mathbb{P}\otimes\mathbb{M})

and provides a measurable version
satisfying (
2.30
).

Example 2.11
.

We can also proceed in a slightly different way:
assuming that
{\mathrm{Q}}
is a compact metrizable space, we can start from a
{\mathrm{H}}
-valued
measurable process
\Xi_{q}=\Xi(\cdot,q)

indexed by
q\in{\mathrm{Q}}

with continuous paths, i.e.
q\mapsto\Xi(\omega,q)\in{\mathrm{C}}^{0}({\mathrm{Q}})

for
\mathbb{P}
-a.e.
\omega
.
In this way the map
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}:\omega\to\Xi(\omega,\cdot)

can be considered as a measurable map from

\Omega
to the Banach space

{\mathcal{B}}:={\mathrm{C}}^{0}({\mathrm{Q}};{\mathrm{H}})
;
assuming that

{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in L^{2}(\Omega,\mathbb{P};{\mathcal{B}})
, the distribution
of the process
{\mathfrak{m}}={\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}

is a Radon measure in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{B}})
.
Any choice of diffuse measure

\mathbb{M}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathrm{Q}})

induces a push-forward map

\iota_{\mathbb{M}}(X):=X_{\sharp}\mathbb{M}

and a measure
{\mathtt{M}}=(\iota_{\mathbb{M}})_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{m}}
as in (
2.33
).
If in particular
\Xi
is a Gaussian process,
then
{\mathfrak{m}}
is a Gaussian measure in
{\mathcal{B}}

and thus also in
{\mathcal{H}}
.

3
Totally convex functionals

We first recall the definition of totally convex functionals on

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

[
CSS23
, Sec. 5]

Definition 3.1

(Totally convex functionals)
.

A functional
\phi:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
is
totally
convex

if for every coupling
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})

and
t\in[0,1]

\phi(\mu_{t})\leq(1-t)\phi(\mu_{0})+\phi(\mu_{1})\quad t\in[0,1],\quad\mu_{t}=(\pi^{1\shortrightarrow 2}_{t})_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\quad\pi^{1\shortrightarrow 2}_{t}(x_{1},x_{2}):=(1-t)x_{1}+tx_{2}.

(3.1)

Equivalently, the lifted functional
\hat{\phi}:=\phi\circ\iota
is
convex in
{\mathcal{H}}
.

We say that
\phi
is totally
\lambda
-convex if

\phi-\frac{\lambda}{2}{\mathsf{m}}_{2}^{2}
is totally convex
(equivalently
\hat{\phi}
is
\lambda
-convex in
{\mathcal{H}}
).

Notice that the lifting
\phi\to\hat{\phi}
inherits also properness
and lower semicontinuity.
Conversely, if
\Phi:{\mathcal{H}}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
is convex, lower semicontinuous and
invariant by m.p.i. then there exists a unique totally convex
functional
\phi
such that
\Phi=\hat{\phi}=\phi\circ\iota
.

If we restrict the convexity inequality (
3.1
) to displacement
interpolation induced by
optimal couplings

{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
, we obtain a
larger class of functionals, which are called
geodesically (or
displacement)
convex, according to
[
McC97
]
.
However if
\dim({\mathrm{H}})\geq 2
, every
continuous
geodesically convex functionals

\phi
is also totally convex
[
CSS23
, Thm. 5.5]
.
We quote here a few important examples:

Example 3.2

(Potential energy)
.

If
f:{\mathrm{H}}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
is
\lambda
-convex (proper, l.s.c.), then

V_{f}:\mu\mapsto\int_{\mathrm{H}}f(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)

is totally
\lambda
-convex (proper, l.s.c.) in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
.
In particular,
{\mathsf{m}}_{2}^{2}
(corresponding to
f(x)=|x|^{2}
) is totally

1
-convex.

Example 3.3

(Multiple interaction energy)
.

If
g:{\mathrm{H}}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
is proper, convex, l.s.c., and invariant
respect to arbitrary permutations of its entries, then

W_{g}:\mu\mapsto\int g(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{k})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{\otimes k}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{k})

is totally convex (proper and
l.s.c.) in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
.

Example 3.4
.

For every
\nu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
we
define the maximal pairing functional

\mathsf{k}_{\nu}:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

\mathsf{k}_{\nu}(\mu):=[\mu,\nu]\quad\text{for every }\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}).

(3.2)

Proposition 3.5
.

For every
\nu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

the function

\mathsf{k}_{\nu}

is
{\mathsf{m}}_{2}(\nu)
-Lipschitz in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
and
totally convex.

Proof.

We give two proofs. The first one is direct: let us fix
\nu,\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

and let

{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{12}\in\Gamma(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
.
Let us set

\pi^{1\shortrightarrow 2}_{t}:{\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}}\to{\mathrm{H}},\quad\pi^{1\shortrightarrow 2}_{t}(x_{1},x_{2}):=(1-t)x_{1}+tx_{2},\quad t\in[0,1],

(3.3)

and
\mu^{1\shortrightarrow 2}_{t}:=(\pi^{1\shortrightarrow 2}_{t})_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}.

We have to prove that

\mathsf{k}_{\nu}(\mu^{1\shortrightarrow 2}_{t})\leq(1-t)\mathsf{k}_{\nu}(\mu_{1})+t\mathsf{k}_{\nu}(\mu_{2})\quad\text{for every }t\in[0,1].

(3.4)

We select
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{t}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{t},\nu)

and we apply

[
AGS08
, Prop. 7.3.1]

to find
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\Gamma(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\nu)\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})

such that

\pi^{12}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}={\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{12}
and

(\pi^{1\shortrightarrow 2}_{t},\pi^{3})_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}={\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{t}
.
It follows that

\displaystyle\mathsf{k}_{\nu}(\mu^{1\shortrightarrow 2}_{t})

\displaystyle=\int{\langle}x,z{\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{t}(x,z)=\int{\langle}\pi^{1\shortrightarrow 2}_{t}(x_{1},x_{2}),x_{3}{\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})

\displaystyle=\int{\langle}(1-t)x_{1}+tx_{2},x_{3}{\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})

\displaystyle=(1-t)\int{\langle}x_{1},x_{3}{\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})+t\int{\langle}x_{2},x_{3}{\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})

\displaystyle\leq(1-t)\mathsf{k}_{\nu}(\mu_{1})+t\mathsf{k}_{\nu}(\mu_{2}).

The second argument uses the representation result (
2.25
);
we fix
Y\in{\mathcal{H}}
such that
\iota(Y)=\nu
and observe that

\hat{\mathsf{k}}_{\nu}(X)={\mathsf{k}}_{\nu}(\iota(X))=\sup\Big\{{\langle}X,Y\circ{\mathsf{g}}{\rangle}:{\mathsf{g}}\in{\mathrm{G}}({\mathrm{Q}})\Big\},

(3.5)

so that
\hat{\mathsf{k}}_{\nu}
is the supremum of a family of

L
-Lipschitz (with
L=\|Y\|_{\mathcal{H}}={\mathsf{m}}_{2}(\nu)
) and
linear functionals: therefore, it is convex and
L
-Lipschitz as well.
∎

3.1
A Kantorovich version of the Legendre-Fenchel transform
in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
.

Recall that the Legendre-Fenchel transform of a function
\Phi:{\mathcal{H}}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
is defined as

\Phi^{{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{$\ast$}}}}}(Y):=\sup_{X\in{\mathcal{H}}}{\langle}Y,X{\rangle}_{\mathcal{H}}-\Phi(X).

(3.6)

Inspired by this formula we define a corresponding transformation
for functionals on
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
.

Definition 3.6

(Kantorovich-Legendre-Fenchel transformation in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
)
.

Let
\phi:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to(-\infty,+\infty]

be a proper function.
We call Kantorovich-Legendre-Fenchel conjugate of

\phi

the function
\phi^{\star}:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to(-\infty,+\infty]
defined by

\phi^{\star}(\nu):=\sup_{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})}[\nu,\mu]-\phi(\mu).

(3.7)

It is clear that
\phi^{\star}
is totally convex and lower
semicontinuous, as the supremum of totally convex and continuous functions. It is also proper (i.e. not identically
=\infty
)
if
\phi
satisfies the lower bound

\phi(\mu)\geq-a+[\mu,\nu]\quad\text{for every }\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

(3.8)

for some
a\in\mathbb{R}
and
\nu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
.

Every geodesically convex function

\phi:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}

is
linearly bounded from below
[
MS20
]
, in the sense
that
there exist constants
a,b\geq 0
such that

\phi(\mu)\geq-a-b\,{\mathsf{m}}_{2}(\mu)\quad

(3.9)

When
\phi
is totally convex, we immediately get
a refined lower bound.

Lemma 3.7
.

If
\phi:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
is totally convex and lower semicontinuous then
there exist
a\in\mathbb{R}
and
\nu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
such that
(
3.8
) holds.
In particular
\phi^{\star}(\nu)\leq a
so that
\phi^{\star}
is proper.

Proof.

It is sufficient to observe that
\hat{\phi}:=\phi\circ\iota
is
convex and lower semicontinuous in
{\mathcal{H}}
so that
there exist
a\geq 0
and
Y\in{\mathcal{H}}
such that

\hat{\phi}(X)\geq-a-{\langle}X,Y{\rangle}\quad\text{for every }X\in{\mathcal{H}}.

Setting
\nu:=\iota(Y)\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
and
using the fact that
\hat{\phi}
is invariant
w.r.t. measure-preserving isomorphisms, we get
for every
\mu=\iota(X)\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

and
{\mathsf{g}}\in{\mathrm{G}}({\mathrm{H}})

\phi(\mu)=\hat{\phi}(X)=\hat{\phi}(X\circ{\mathsf{g}}^{-1})\geq-a+{\langle}X\circ{\mathsf{g}}^{-1},Y{\rangle}=-a+{\langle}X,Y\circ{\mathsf{g}}{\rangle}.

Taking the supremum w.r.t.
{\mathsf{g}}\in{\mathrm{G}}({\mathrm{H}})

and recalling
(
2.21
) and (
2.25
)
we get

\phi(\mu)\geq-a+\sup_{{\mathsf{g}}\in{\mathrm{G}}({\mathrm{H}})}{\langle}X,Y\circ{\mathsf{g}}{\rangle}=-a+[\nu,\mu].

∎

We collect a few simple but relevant properties of the
Kantorovich-Legendre-Fenchel transform in the following Theorem.

Theorem 3.8
.

Let
\phi:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
be a proper function satisfying
(
3.8
) for some
a\in\mathbb{R}
and
\nu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
.

1.

The function
\phi^{\star}
is proper,
totally convex and lower semicontinuous.

2.

\phi^{\star}
satisfies the commutation property

(\phi^{\star})\circ\iota=(\phi\circ\iota)^{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{$\ast$}}}}

(3.10)

and it is the unique function satisfying (
3.10
).

3.

The function
\phi^{\star\star}=(\phi^{\star})^{\star}
is the
largest totally convex and lower semicontinuous function
dominated by
\phi
.

Proof.

Claim 1 is a direct consequence of Lemma
3.7

(for properness) and Proposition
3.5
(for
convexity and lower semicontinuity).

In order to check (
3.10
) of Claim 2, we consider
Y\in{\mathcal{H}}
and

\nu=\iota(Y)
; (
2.25
) yields

\displaystyle\phi^{\star}(\nu)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\mu\in{\mathcal{H}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})}[\nu,\mu]-\phi(\mu)=\sup_{X\in{\mathcal{H}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})}[\nu,\iota(X)]-\phi(\iota(X))=\sup_{{\mathsf{g}}\in{\mathrm{G}}({\mathrm{Q}})}\sup_{X\in{\mathcal{H}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})}{\langle}Y,X\circ g{\rangle}-\phi(\iota(X))

\displaystyle=\sup_{{\mathsf{g}}\in{\mathrm{G}}({\mathrm{Q}})}\sup_{X\in{\mathcal{H}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})}{\langle}Y,X\circ{\mathsf{g}}{\rangle}-\phi(\iota(X\circ{\mathsf{g}}))=\sup_{X^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})}{\langle}Y,X^{\prime}{\rangle}-\phi(\iota(X^{\prime}))=(\phi\circ\iota)^{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{$\ast$}}}}(Y).

The uniqueness follows by the law invariance of
(\phi\circ\iota)^{*}
.
Claim 3 is then an obvious consequence of Claim 2 and the corresponding property
for the Legendre-Fenchel transformation in
{\mathcal{H}}
.
∎

We can easily derive natural properties from the Hilbertian theory.

Corollary 3.9

(Kantorovich-Fenchel inequality)
.

For every
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in\Gamma(\mu,\nu)
we have

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathrm{H}}^{2}}{\langle}x,y{\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(x,y)\leq[\mu,\nu]\leq\phi(\mu)+\phi^{\star}(\nu)

(3.11)

\displaystyle\phi^{\star}(\nu)=\sup\Big\{\int_{{\mathrm{H}}^{2}}{\langle}x,y{\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(x,y)-\phi(\pi^{1}_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}):{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}}),\ \pi^{2}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\nu\Big\}.

(3.12)

Moreover

\int_{{\mathrm{H}}^{2}}{\langle}x,y{\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(x,y)=\phi(\mu)+\phi^{\star}(\nu)\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\begin{cases}\phi(\mu)+\phi^{\star}(\nu)=[\mu,\nu],\\
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu,\nu).\end{cases}

(3.13)

Corollary 3.10
.

Let
\phi:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}

be a proper function.
The following properties are equivalent:

1.

\phi
is totally convex and lower
semicontinuous;

2.

\phi\circ\iota
is convex and lower semicontinuous in
{\mathcal{H}}
;

3.

\phi=\phi^{\star\star}
.

4.

\phi

is
[\cdot,\cdot]
-convex, i.e. there exists a set

G\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\times\mathbb{R}
such that

\phi(\mu)=\sup_{(\nu,a)\in G}[\mu,\nu]-a.

(3.14)

Proof.

The implication 1
\Leftrightarrow
2 is a consequence of the
definition of total convexity and Lemma
2.8
.

1
\Leftrightarrow
3 is a consequence of Claim 3 of Theorem
3.8
.
3
\Rightarrow
4 is also immediate by the definition of

(\phi^{\star})^{\star}
; the converse implication
is a consequence of the fact that the map
\mu\mapsto[\mu,\nu]
is
totally convex and continuous.
∎

Thanks to the commutation identity
(
3.10
),
it is possible to
derive interesting calculus rules
for
\phi^{\star}

from the corresponding
formulae for
\hat{\phi}^{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{$\ast$}}}}
. We
present two simple examples that will turn to be useful in the following.
Let us first introduce the
dilation maps
and the Moreau-Yosida regularizations of
\phi.

Definition 3.11
.

For every
a>0
we set

{\mathfrak{d}}_{a}:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

{\mathfrak{d}}_{a}[\mu]:=(a{\boldsymbol{i}})_{\sharp}\mu,\quad{\mathfrak{d}}_{a}[\mu](B)=\mu(a^{-1}B)\quad\text{for every Borel set }B\subset{\mathrm{H}}.

(3.15)

For every
\tau>0

and every proper
l.s.c. and totally convex function

\phi:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}

the
\tau
-Moreau-Yosida regularization of

\phi
is defined by

\phi_{\tau}(\mu):=\min_{\nu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})}\frac{1}{2\tau}{\mathsf{w}}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)+\phi(\nu).

(3.16)

Dilations are clearly related to
scalar multiplication of Lagrangian maps via the formula

\mu=\iota(X)\quad\Rightarrow\quad{\mathfrak{d}}_{a}[\mu]=\iota(a\,X).

(3.17)

The Yosida regularization (
3.16
)
plays a crucial role in evolution problems via the JKO-Minimizing Movement scheme

[
JKO98
,
AGS08
]
.
Existence of a minimizer of
(
3.16
) for an arbitrary geodesically convex functional follows by the results of
[
NS21
]
; in the present case, we can invoke the following important commutation property

\widehat{\phi_{\tau}}=\hat{\phi}_{\tau}

(3.18)

where
\hat{\phi}_{\tau}
is
the Yosida regularization of
\hat{\phi}

in
{\mathcal{H}}
defined by

\hat{\phi}_{\tau}(X)=\min_{Y\in{\mathcal{H}}}\frac{1}{2\tau}\|X-Y\|^{2}_{{\mathcal{H}}}+\hat{\phi}(Y),

(3.19)

which also shows that
\phi_{\tau}
is
totally convex as well.
In order to check (
3.18
)
we first oberve that (
2.21
) yields

\mu=\iota(X)\quad\Rightarrow\quad\hat{\phi}_{\tau}(X)\geq\phi_{\tau}(\mu);

the converse inequality follows by the
law invariance of
\hat{\phi}
and
(
2.25
).

Corollary 3.12
.

Let

\phi:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
be a proper
l.s.c. and totally convex function.
For every
a,b>0

we have

(a\phi)^{\star}=a\phi^{\star}\circ{\mathfrak{d}}_{a^{-1}},

(3.20)

\Big(a\phi+\frac{b}{2}{\mathsf{m}}_{2}^{2}\Big)^{\star}=a(\phi^{\star})_{b/a}\circ{\mathfrak{d}}_{a^{-1}}

(3.21)

Proof.

(
3.20
) follows from the
corresponding identity for

\hat{\phi}
:

(a\hat{\phi})^{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{$\ast$}}}}(X)=a\hat{\phi}^{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{$\ast$}}}}(a^{-1}X).

(
3.21
) follows by the
fact that

\Big(a\phi+\frac{b}{2}{\mathsf{m}}_{2}^{2}\Big)^{\wedge}=a\hat{\phi}+\frac{b}{2}\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

and by the corresponding formula
for the Legendre transform
of a quadratic perturbation
of
\hat{\phi}
in
{\mathcal{H}}
(which is a
particular case of infimal convolution):

\Big(a\hat{\phi}+\frac{b}{2}\tau\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\Big)^{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{$\ast$}}}}=a(\hat{\phi}^{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{$\ast$}}}})_{b/a}(\cdot/a).

(3.22)

∎

3.2
{\mathsf{c}}
-concave functions and their
{\mathsf{c}}
-super
differentials

As in the case of functions defined in a Hilbert space,
we can connect the notion of totally convex functionals and
Kantorovich-Legendre-Fenchel transform
in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

with the corresponding notion of
{\mathsf{c}}
-concavity and
{\mathsf{c}}
-transform
for the cost
{\mathsf{c}}:=\frac{1}{2}{\mathsf{w}}_{2}^{2}
.

Let us first recall the main definition for a continuous
and symmetric cost function
{\mathsf{c}}:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to\mathbb{R}
:
in our case we will mainly use the choice
{\mathsf{c}}:=\frac{1}{2}{\mathsf{w}}_{2}^{2}
.

Definition 3.13

(Concave
{\mathsf{c}}
-transform,
{\mathsf{c}}
-concavity and

{\mathsf{c}}
-superdifferential)
.

Let
f:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
be a proper function.
Its concave
{\mathsf{c}}
-transform

f^{\mathsf{c}}:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
is defined by

f^{\mathsf{c}}(\nu):=\inf_{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})}{\mathsf{c}}(\mu,\nu)-f(\mu).

(3.23)

f
is
{\mathsf{c}}
-concave if

\exists\,A\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\times\mathbb{R}:\quad f(\mu)=\inf_{(\nu,a)\in A}{\mathsf{c}}(\mu,\nu)-a.

(3.24)

If
\mu,\nu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
with
f(\mu)\in\mathbb{R}
, we say
that
\nu
belongs to the
{\mathsf{c}}
-superdifferential of
f
at
\mu
,
denoted by

\partial_{{\mathsf{c}}}^{+}f(\mu)
, if

f(\mu^{\prime})-f(\mu)\leq{\mathsf{c}}(\mu^{\prime},\nu)-{\mathsf{c}}(\mu,\nu)\quad\text{for every
}\mu^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}).

(3.25)

We collect a series of well known properties of the above notion.

Theorem 3.14
.

Let
f:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
be a proper function.

1.

f
is
{\mathsf{c}}
-concave iff
f=g^{\mathsf{c}}
for some function

g:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
,

2.

f
is
{\mathsf{c}}
-concave if and only if

f=f^{{\mathsf{c}}{\mathsf{c}}}=(f^{\mathsf{c}})^{\mathsf{c}}
.

3.

for every
\mu,\nu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
,
f(\mu)+f^{{\mathsf{c}}}(\nu)\leq{\mathsf{c}}(\mu,\nu)

4.

\nu\in\partial_{\mathsf{c}}^{+}f(\mu)
if and only if

f(\mu)+f^{{\mathsf{c}}}(\nu)={\mathsf{c}}(\mu,\nu)
.

We could introduce the analogous concepts of
{\mathsf{c}}
-convexity,
{\mathsf{c}}
-convex transform and

{\mathsf{c}}
-subdifferential: since in this context we will only use the
pseudo-scalar cost
[\cdot,\cdot]
,

[\cdot,\cdot]
-convexity is equivalent to total convexity by
Corollary (
3.10
), so that
we will keep the notation
\phi^{\star}

of Definition
3.6
for the
[\cdot,\cdot]
-conjugate.
We will just introduce the notion
of
[\cdot,\cdot]
-subdifferential: for every
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
with
\phi(\mu)\in\mathbb{R}
, we also set

\partial^{-}\phi(\mu):=\Big\{\nu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}):\phi(\mu^{\prime})-\phi(\mu)\geq[\mu^{\prime},\nu]-[\mu,\nu]\quad\text{for every }\mu^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\Big\}.

(3.26)

As for property 4 of Theorem
3.14
above, we easily get

\nu\in\partial^{-}\phi(\mu)\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad[\mu,\nu]=\phi(\mu)+\phi^{\star}(\nu).

(3.27)

As in the case of the
L^{2}
-Wasserstein metric in
{\mathrm{H}}
, we have the
following simple but crucial properties.

Corollary 3.15
.

Let us consider the cost function
{\mathsf{c}}:=\frac{1}{2}{\mathsf{w}}_{2}^{2}
, let

\phi,\psi:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}

and
{\mathcal{U}}
,
{\mathcal{V}}
be defined as

{\mathcal{U}}:=\frac{1}{2}{\mathsf{m}}_{2}^{2}-\phi,\quad{\mathcal{V}}:=\frac{1}{2}{\mathsf{m}}_{2}^{2}-\psi.

(3.28)

The following hold true:

1.

\psi=\phi^{\star}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad{\mathcal{V}}={\mathcal{U}}^{\mathsf{c}}

(3.29)

2.

\phi
is totally convex and lower semicontinuous
if and only if

{\mathcal{U}}
is
{\mathsf{c}}
-concave (and thus upper semicontinuous).

3.

For every
\mu,\nu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
with
\phi(\mu)\in\mathbb{R}

(and thus
{\mathcal{U}}(\mu)\in\mathbb{R}
as well)

\nu\in\partial^{-}\phi(\mu)\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\nu\in\partial_{\mathsf{c}}^{+}{\mathcal{U}}(\mu).

(3.30)

Proof.

We repeatedly use the identity in (
2.12
).

Claim 1.
For
every
\nu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

(
3.28
) yields

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}^{\mathsf{c}}(\nu)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})}\frac{1}{2}{\mathsf{w}}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)-{\mathcal{U}}(\mu)=\inf_{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})}\frac{1}{2}{\mathsf{w}}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)-\Big({\mathsf{m}}_{2}^{2}(\mu)-\phi(\mu)\Big)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})}\frac{1}{2}{\mathsf{m}}_{2}^{2}(\nu)-[\mu,\nu]+\phi(\mu)=\frac{1}{2}{\mathsf{m}}_{2}^{2}(\nu)+\inf_{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})}\Big(-[\mu,\nu]+\phi(\mu)\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}{\mathsf{m}}_{2}^{2}(\nu)-\sup_{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})}\Big([\mu,\nu]-\phi(\mu)\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}{\mathsf{m}}_{2}^{2}(\nu)-\phi^{\star}(\nu).

The second claim immediately follows
by the first one, Claim 3 of Corollary
3.10
and Claim 2
of Theorem
3.14
.

Claim 3 then follows by (
3.27
) and
Claim 4 of Theorem
3.14
.
∎

In the next section we will discuss a more refined representation of

\partial^{-}\phi
, where we
replace
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
by
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})
.

4
Multivalued probability
fields

We recall the notion of multivalued probability vector field (MPVF)

\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}
, introduced in a different context by

[
Pic19
]
and in full generality by
[
CSS23a
]
:
they are a natural extension of the usual notion of vector field for studying
the evolution of probability measures.

The simplest way to define a MPVF
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}
is just to consider it as a nonempty
subset of
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})
.
We set
D(\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}):=\Big\{\pi^{1}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}:{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}\Big\}

and for every
\mu\in D(\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}})
we consider the sections
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]:=\Big\{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}:\pi^{1}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\mu\Big\}
.
Sections define a multivaled map
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}[\cdot]:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to 2^{{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})}
with the property

{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]\quad\Rightarrow\quad\pi^{1}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\mu.

When
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}[\cdot]
is single valued we say that
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}
is a

probability vector field (PVF).
A particular case, that play a
crucial role, is given by

deterministic
PVFs:
they are characterized by the property

\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}\subset{{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm det}_{2}}({\mathrm{H}}^{2})\quad\text{so that }\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]=({\boldsymbol{i}}\times{\boldsymbol{f}})_{\sharp}\mu\quad\text{for a (unique) ${\mathrm{H}}$-valued map }{\boldsymbol{f}}\in L^{2}({\mathrm{H}},\mu;{\mathrm{H}}),

(4.1)

where we adopted the obvious notation

{{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm det}_{2}}({\mathrm{H}}^{2}):={\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}^{2})\cap{{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm det}}({\mathrm{H}}^{2})
.
If
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}
is a deterministic PVF, for every
\mu\in D(\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}})
we can
thus define
a nonlocal vectorfield

{\boldsymbol{f}}[\mu]={\boldsymbol{f}}(\cdot,\mu)\in L^{2}({\mathrm{H}},\mu;{\mathrm{H}})\quad\text{such that
\eqref{eq:70} holds;}

(4.2)

notice that

\pi^{2}_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu]={\boldsymbol{f}}[\mu]_{\sharp}\mu.

(4.3)

Every MPVF
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}

admits a Lagrangian representation (or lifting)

\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}\subset{\mathcal{H}}\times{\mathcal{H}}
defined by

(X,Y)\in\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\iota^{2}(X,Y)=(X,Y)_{\sharp}\mathbb{M}\in\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}.

(4.4)

It is immediate to check that
\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}
is law invariant

(X,Y)\in\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}},\ \iota^{2}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})=\iota^{2}(X,Y)\quad\Rightarrow\quad(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})\in\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}
