(4.5)

and thus invariant with respect to the action of measure-preserving
isomorphisms:

(X,Y)\in\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}\quad\Rightarrow\quad({\mathsf{g}}^{*}X,{\mathsf{g}}^{*}Y)\in\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}\quad\text{for every }{\mathsf{g}}\in{\mathrm{G}}({\mathrm{Q}}).

(4.6)

Conversely, if a subset
{\boldsymbol{B}}\subset{\mathcal{H}}\times{\mathcal{H}}
is
invariant by m.p.i. and
closed
, then it is also law invariant
and it is the Lagrangian representation of a unique MPVF
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}

[
CSS25
]
.

4.1
Totally monotone and cyclically monotone MPVF

Inspired by the the definition of totally monotone MPVF (introduced in

[
CSS23
]
) we
introduce here the
corresponding notion of totally cyclically monotone MPVF. We will adopt the
notation

{\boldsymbol{\pi}}^{n}:({\mathrm{H}}^{2})^{N}\to{\mathrm{H}}^{2},\quad{\boldsymbol{\pi}}^{n}((x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{2},y_{2}),\cdots,(x_{N},y_{N}))=(x_{n},y_{n}),\quad n=1,\cdots,N.

(4.7)

Definition 4.1

(Totally monotone and cyclically monotone MPVF)
.

A multivalued probability vector field (MPVF)
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}^{2})

is totally monotone if
for every
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}^{2}\times{\mathrm{H}}^{2})

with
{\boldsymbol{\pi}}^{1}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}},\ {\boldsymbol{\pi}}^{2}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}
we have

\int{\langle}y_{2}-y_{1},x_{2}-x_{1}{\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(x_{1},y_{1};x_{2},y_{2})\geq 0.

(4.8)

\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}
is a maximal totally monotone MPVF if
it is totally monotone and
the inclusion
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{G}}}
,
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{G}}}

totally monotone, yields
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}=\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{G}}}
.

\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}

is totally cyclically monotone if
for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(({\mathrm{H}}^{2})^{N})
,
with
{\boldsymbol{\pi}}^{n}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}
,
n=1,\cdots,N
,
and
\sigma\in{\mathrm{S}}_{N}=\operatorname{Sym}(\{1,\cdots,N\})
permutation

\sum_{n=1}^{N}\int{\langle}y_{n},x_{n}-x_{\sigma(n)}{\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(x_{1},y_{1};x_{2},y_{2};\cdots;x_{N},y_{N})\geq 0.

(4.9)

In the case of a deterministic PVF
induced by a nonlocal vector field

{\boldsymbol{f}}

as in (
4.1
),(
4.2
), the total monotonicity condition
(
4.9
) reads as

\int{\langle}{\boldsymbol{f}}(x_{2},\mu_{2})-{\boldsymbol{f}}(x_{1},\mu_{1}),x_{2}-x_{1}{\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(x_{1},x_{2})\geq 0\quad\text{for every }\mu_{i}\in D(\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}),\ {\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\Gamma(\mu_{1},\mu_{2});

(4.10)

Similarly, (
4.8
) reads as

\sum_{n=1}^{N}\int{\langle}{\boldsymbol{f}}(x_{n},\mu_{n}),x_{n}-x_{\sigma(n)}{\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\geq 0\quad\text{for every }\mu_{i}\in D(\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}),\ {\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\Gamma(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\cdots,\mu_{N}).

(4.11)

Let
\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}
be the Lagrangian representation of
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}
; it is easy to check that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}\text{ is totally monotone}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}\text{ is
monotone in }{\mathcal{H}}\times{\mathcal{H}},

(4.12)

and, according to the main result of
[
CSS25
]
, we also have

\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}\text{ is maximal totally monotone}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}\text{ is
maximal monotone in }{\mathcal{H}}\times{\mathcal{H}}.

(4.13)

A maximal totally monotone MPVF has many important properties, see

[
CSS23
]
.
We quote here the most relevant ones for our discussion; the first one involves the
(Borel) barycentric map

{\boldsymbol{b}}:{\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})\to{\mathrm{H}}

defined by using the universal disintegration kernel (
2.2
):

{\boldsymbol{b}}(x,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}):=\int y\,{\mathrm{d}}({\mathcal{K}}(x,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})(y))=\int y\,{\mathrm{d}}\kappa_{x}(y),\quad

(4.14)

where
\kappa_{x}={\mathcal{K}}(x,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})

is the disintegration of
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
w.r.t. its first marginal.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}
be a maximal totally monotone MPVF.

1.

If
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}
and

\mu=\pi^{1}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in D(\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}})
,
then the barycentric projection

{\boldsymbol{b}}(\cdot,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})
of
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}

satisfies

({\boldsymbol{i}}\times{\boldsymbol{b}}(\cdot,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}))_{\sharp}\mu\in\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu].

(4.15)

2.

There exists a unique minimal section
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}^{\circ}\subset\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}\cap{{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm det}_{2}}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})

and a Borel nonlocal vector field

{\boldsymbol{f}}^{\circ}={\boldsymbol{b}}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}^{\circ}):{\mathrm{H}}\times D(\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}})\to{\mathrm{H}}
such that
for every
\mu\in D(\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}})

we have

\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}^{\circ}[\mu]=({\boldsymbol{i}}\times{\boldsymbol{f}}^{\circ}(\cdot,\mu))_{\sharp}\mu
and

\int_{\mathrm{H}}\big|{\boldsymbol{f}}^{\circ}(x,\mu)\big|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\leq\int_{\mathrm{H}}\big|{\boldsymbol{b}}(x,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})\big|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\leq\int_{\mathrm{H}}\big|y\big|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(x,y)\quad\text{for every }{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}[\mu].

(4.16)

3.

If
\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}
is the Lagrangian representation of
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}
, then

D(\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}})=(\iota_{\sharp})^{-1}(D(\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}))
and the minimal section

\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}{}^{\circ}

of
\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}

satisfies

Y=\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}{}^{\circ}(X)\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad Y(q)={\boldsymbol{f}}^{\circ}(X(q),\mu)={\boldsymbol{f}}^{\circ}[\mu](X(q))\quad\text{for $\mathbb{M}$-a.e.~$q$},\ \mu=\iota(X).

(4.17)

Let us focus now on totally cyclically monotone MPVFs: first of all, we have a simple lifting result.

Proposition 4.3

(Lifting of totally cyclically monotone MPVF)
.

A MPVF
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}
is totally cyclically monotone
if and only if
\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}
is
cyclically monotone in
{\mathcal{H}}\times{\mathcal{H}}.

Proof.

It is sufficient to observe that
for every
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(({\mathrm{H}}^{2})^{N})

with
{\boldsymbol{\pi}}^{n}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}
,
n=1,\cdots,N
,
we can find a Borel map

Z=((X_{1},Y_{1}),\cdots,(X_{N},Y_{N})):{\mathrm{Q}}\to({\mathrm{H}}^{2})^{N}

such that

Z_{\sharp}\mathbb{M}={\boldsymbol{\theta}}
, so that

(X_{n},Y_{n})\in\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}
for every
n\in\{1,\cdots,N\}.

Conversely, if

(X_{n},Y_{n})\in\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}
then

{\boldsymbol{\theta}}=Z_{\sharp}\mathbb{M}

belongs to

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(({\mathrm{H}}^{2})^{N})
and

{\boldsymbol{\pi}}^{n}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}
.

For every permutation
\sigma\in{\mathrm{S}}_{N}

we can then use the identity

\sum_{n=1}^{N}\int{\langle}y_{n},x_{n}-x_{\sigma(n)}{\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\theta}}=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\int{\langle}Y_{n},X_{n}-X_{\sigma(n)}{\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{M}=\sum_{n=1}^{N}{\langle}Y_{n},X_{n}-X_{\sigma(n)}{\rangle}_{\mathcal{H}}

which shows the equivalence between condition
(
4.9
)
and the corresponding condition expressing the cyclical monotonicity of
\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}
in
{\mathcal{H}}\times{\mathcal{H}}
.
∎

As in the Hilbertian framework, we will show that totally cyclically monotone MPVFs
are strictly linked with the notion of

total subdifferential
.
Let us first recall the definition
(we refer to

[
CSS23
]
for a detailed comparison with other notion of subdifferentiability, in particular the ones introduced in

[
AGS08
]
, from a metric perspective).

Definition 4.4

(Total subdifferential)
.

The total subdifferential of

\phi:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}

is the set
\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})
characterized by the following property:
a plan
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}^{2})
belongs to
\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi
if and only if

\mu=\pi^{1}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in D(\phi)
and for every
\nu\in D(\phi)

and
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in\Gamma({\boldsymbol{\gamma}},\nu)
we have

\phi(\nu)-\phi(\mu)\geq\int_{{\mathrm{H}}^{2}\times{\mathrm{H}}}{\langle}y_{1},x_{2}-x_{1}{\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(x_{1},y_{1};x_{2}).

(4.18)

Notice that
if
\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi
is not empty then

\phi
satisfies
(
3.8
):
if
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi[\mu]
,

\mu^{\prime}:=\pi^{2}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
,

{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{o}(\nu,\mu^{\prime})

we can apply the glueing Lemma to select

{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}^{3})

with
\pi^{1\,2}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\theta}}={\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
,

\pi^{2\,3}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\theta}}={\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{\prime}

so that
(
4.18
) yields

\phi(\nu)\geq\phi(\mu)-\int_{{\mathcal{H}}^{2}}{\langle}y,x{\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(x,y)+\int_{{\mathcal{H}}^{2}}{\langle}y,z{\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{\prime}(y,z)=-a+[\nu,\mu^{\prime}]

where
a:=\int_{{\mathcal{H}}^{2}}{\langle}y,x{\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(x,y)-\phi(\mu)
.

Applying

[
CSS23
, Proposition 5.2]

we can show that there is a strong relation between the total subdifferential
of
\phi
and the
(convex) subdifferential of its Lagrangian lifting
\hat{\phi}=\phi\circ\iota
in
{\mathcal{H}}
.

Theorem 4.5

(Lifting of total subdifferentials)
.

Let
\phi:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
be a proper lower semicontinuous function
with Lagrangian lift
\hat{\phi}:=\phi\circ\iota
.
The (possible empty) convex subdifferential
\partial\hat{\phi}\subset{\mathcal{H}}\times{\mathcal{H}}

is law invariant and coincides with the Lagrangian representation of
\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi
, i.e.

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi}=\partial\hat{\phi}.

(4.19)

In particular

\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi
is totally cyclically monotone and

\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi\subset\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}(\phi^{\star\star}),\qquad\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi[\mu]\neq\emptyset\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\phi(\mu)=\phi^{\star\star}(\mu),\ \boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi[\mu]=\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi^{\star\star}[\mu]\neq\emptyset.

(4.20)

If moreover
\phi
is also totally convex then
\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi

is maximal totally
monotone and its minimal section
\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}^{\circ}\phi

is associated with the minimal section
\partial^{\circ}\hat{\phi}
through (
4.17
).

Proof.

Since
\hat{\phi}
is l.s.c.,

\partial\hat{\phi}
is a closed
subset of
{\mathcal{H}}\times{\mathcal{H}}
.
By Claim 1 of
[
CSS23
, Proposition 5.2]

we deduce that
\partial\hat{\phi}
is law invariant
and
we can then apply Claim 3 of
[
CSS23
, Proposition 5.2]

(which uses only the law invariance of
\partial\hat{\phi}
)
to deduce
that

\partial\hat{\phi}
is the
Lagrangian representation of

\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi
according to (
4.19
).
(
4.20
) then follow
by the corresponding well known properties
of
\partial\hat{\phi}
.

When
\phi
is also totally convex,
the same Proposition 5.2 shows that

\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi
is maximal totally monotone and (
4.12
) shows
that it is totally cyclically monotone (since
\partial\hat{\phi}
is
cyclically monotone in
{\mathcal{H}}\times{\mathcal{H}}
).
We conclude by applying Claim 3 of Proposition
4.2
.
∎

Thanks to the previous result, we can now extend the celebrated Rockafellar Theorem to totally
cyclically monotone MPVF.

Theorem 4.6

(Totally cyclically maximal monotone MPVFs are total subdifferentials)
.

If
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}
is a totally cyclically monotone MPVF in

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}^{2})
then there exists
a proper, totally convex and lower semicontinuous function

\phi:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}

such that
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi
.
In particular,
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}
has a maximal
totally monotone
extension which is cyclically monotone
and every totally cyclically
maximal monotone MPVF is the total
subdifferential
of a totally convex function.

Proof.

Let us denote by
\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}
the Lagrangian representation of
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}
in

{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
; we know that
\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}
is invariant by measure preserving
isomorphisms, i.e.

(X,Y)\in\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}\quad\Rightarrow\quad(X\circ{\mathsf{g}},Y\circ{\mathsf{g}})\in\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}\quad\text{for every }{\mathsf{g}}\in{\mathrm{G}}({\mathrm{Q}}).

(4.21)

We fix
(X_{0},Y_{0})\in\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}
and use Rockafellar construction

[
Roc66
]
to define

\Phi(X):=\sup\Big\{{\langle}X-X_{N},Y_{N}{\rangle}_{\mathcal{H}}+\sum_{n=1}^{N}{\langle}X_{n}-X_{n-1},Y_{n-1}{\rangle}_{\mathcal{H}}:N\in\mathbb{N},\ (X_{n},Y_{n})\in\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}\Big\}.

(4.22)

We know that
\Phi
is convex, proper, lower semicontinuous, and
\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}\subset\partial\Phi
.
Let us prove that
\Phi
is invariant by measure-preserving
isomorphisms:
we use the fact that for every
N
-tuple
(X_{n},Y_{n})\in\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}

we also have
(X_{n}\circ{\mathsf{g}},Y_{n}\circ{\mathsf{g}})\in\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}
so that

\displaystyle\Phi(X\circ{\mathsf{g}})

\displaystyle\geq{\langle}X\circ{\mathsf{g}}-X_{N}\circ{\mathsf{g}},Y_{N}\circ{\mathsf{g}}{\rangle}_{\mathcal{H}}+\sum_{n=1}^{N}{\langle}X_{n}\circ{\mathsf{g}}-X_{n-1}\circ{\mathsf{g}},Y_{n-1}\circ{\mathsf{g}}{\rangle}_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle={\langle}X-X_{N},Y_{N}{\rangle}_{\mathcal{H}}+\sum_{n=1}^{N}{\langle}X_{n}-X_{n-1},Y_{n-1}{\rangle}_{\mathcal{H}}

so that taking the supremum with respect to
(X_{n},Y_{n})\in\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}
and

N\in\mathbb{N}
we get

\Phi(X\circ{\mathsf{g}})\geq\Phi(X).

Applying the same inequality to
X\circ{\mathsf{g}}
with
{\mathsf{g}}
replaced by

{\mathsf{g}}^{-1}
we also obtain

\Phi(X\circ{\mathsf{g}}\circ{\mathsf{g}}^{-1})=\Phi(X)\geq\Phi(X\circ{\mathsf{g}})

so that
\Phi(X\circ{\mathsf{g}})=\Phi(X)
for every
X
and every
{\mathsf{g}}\in{\mathrm{G}}({\mathrm{Q}})
.
Since
\Phi
is lower semicontinuous and invariant by the action
of
{\mathrm{G}}({\mathrm{Q}})
, Lemma
2.8
shows that

\Phi=\phi\circ\iota
for a lower semicontinuous
function

\phi:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
, which is clearly totally convex and
satisfies
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi
by Proposition
4.5
.
∎

We briefly discuss the case when
\phi

is differentiable, in a suitable Wasserstein sense.
First of all
by using a (Borel) barycentric map
{\boldsymbol{b}}
as in
(
4.14
) we can define the
nonlocal deterministic field

\nabla_{W}\phi:{\mathrm{H}}\times D(\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi)\to{\mathrm{H}}

as in Proposition
4.2
:

\nabla_{W}\phi(x,\mu)=\nabla_{W}\phi[\mu](x):={\boldsymbol{b}}(x,\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}^{\circ}\phi[\mu]).

(4.23)

Recall that if a convex function
\psi:{\mathcal{H}}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
is Gateaux differentiable at

X\in D(\psi)
then

\partial\psi(X)
is a singleton
(thus coinciding with the minimal section

\partial^{\circ}\psi
). The converse is also true if in addition
\psi
is continuous at
X

[
ET76
, Chap. I, Prop. 5.3]
.
We can say that

\text{$\phi$ is
$W$-differentiable at $\mu$ if
$\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi[\mu]$
contains a unique element},

(4.24)

which in turn coincides with the minimal section, expressed through
\nabla_{W}\phi
.

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
\phi:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}

be a proper, l.s.c., and totally convex function.

1.

\phi
is
W
-differentiable
at
\mu
if and only if

\partial\hat{\phi}

is a singleton at every

X\in\iota^{-1}(\mu).

2.

If
\phi
is continuous
at
\mu
then
it is

W
-differentiable if and only if

\hat{\phi}
is Gateaux-differentiable
at every point of

\iota^{-1}(\mu)
.

3.

For every
\tau>0

\phi_{\tau}
is

W
-differentiable everywhere,
the map
(x,\mu)\mapsto\nabla_{W}\phi_{\tau}(x,\mu)

is everywhere defined and continuous
in

{\mathcal{S}}({\mathrm{H}}):=\Big\{(x,\mu)\in{\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}):x\in\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits(\mu)\Big\}

(4.25)

for every
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

the map
x\mapsto\nabla_{W}\phi_{\tau}(x,\mu)

is
\tau^{-1}
-Lipschitz in
\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits(\mu)
,
and the map

\mu\mapsto\nabla_{W}\phi_{\tau}(\cdot,\mu)_{\sharp}\mu
is
\tau^{-1}
-Lipschitz
in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}).

Proof.

To prove the first claim
it is sufficient to recall that

(X,Y)\in\partial\hat{\phi}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad(X,Y)_{\sharp}\mathbb{M}\in\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi

\boldsymbol{\partial}\phi[\mu]

contains just one element
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}

if and only if it is
reduced to the
minimal section, i.e.
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=({\boldsymbol{i}}\times\nabla_{W}\phi(\cdot,\mu))_{\sharp}\mu

so that

\iota^{2}(X,Y)={\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
implies
Y=\nabla_{W}(X,\mu)

and therefore
\partial\phi(X)

is reduced to a singleton for every

X\in\iota^{-1}(\mu).

The second claim then follows
since continuity of
\phi
at
\mu

implies continuity of
\hat{\phi}

at every
X\in\iota^{-1}(\mu).

Finally, the third claim
follows by
(
3.18
) and
the well known properties of the Moreau-Yosida regularization in the Hilbert space

{\mathcal{H}}
:

\hat{\phi}_{\tau}
is of class
{\mathrm{C}}^{1,1}
and its differential

{\mathrm{D}}\hat{\phi}_{\tau}
(which
provides the unique element
of
\partial\hat{\phi}_{\tau}
) is

\tau^{-1}
-Lipschitz.
We can then apply
the results of

[
CSS25
, Section 4]
and

[
CSS23
, Section 5]
to conclude.
∎

4.2
Marginal projections of MPVF

We notice that a MPVF
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})
induces a subset
{\mathfrak{F}}\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
by applying
marginal projections

{\mathfrak{F}}=(\pi^{1}_{\sharp},\pi^{2}_{\sharp})\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}},\quad\text{or, equivalently,}\quad(\mu,\nu)\in{\mathfrak{F}}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}\cap\Gamma(\mu,\nu)\neq\emptyset.

(4.26)

It is therefore natural to investigate the relations between
the total subdifferential
\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi
(a MPVF) and
the
[\cdot,\cdot]
-subdifferential of
\phi
(a subset of

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
).

We start with a nice characterization of the total subdifferential of
\phi

in terms of its Kantorovich-Legendre-Fenchel transform.

Theorem 4.8

(Total subdifferential and Kantorovich-Legendre-Fenchel transform)
.

A plan
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}^{2})

belongs to
\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi[\mu]

if and only if

\mu=\pi^{1}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
,

\nu=\pi^{2}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in D(\phi^{\star})
and

\int_{{\mathrm{H}}^{2}}{\langle}x,y{\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(x,y)=\phi(\mu)+\phi^{\star}(\nu).

(4.27)

In particular
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu,\nu)

and
\int_{{\mathrm{H}}^{2}}{\langle}x,y{\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(x,y)=[\mu,\nu]
.

Proof.

We have seen that
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi[\mu]
if and only if
for every
(X,Y)\in{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
with
\iota^{2}(X,Y)={\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
we have

(X,Y)\in\partial\hat{\phi}
. In turn, this is equivalent to

{\langle}X,Y{\rangle}_{\mathcal{H}}=\hat{\phi}(X)+(\hat{\phi})^{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{$\ast$}}}}(Y)=\phi(\mu)+\phi^{\star}(\nu).

Since
{\langle}X,Y{\rangle}_{\mathcal{H}}=\int{\langle}x,y{\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
we obtain (
4.27
).
∎

Combining Theorem
4.8
with (
3.27
) we can immediately link the total subdifferential

\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi
with the
[\cdot,\cdot]
subdifferential
\partial^{-}\phi
.

Corollary 4.9

(Total and
[\cdot,\cdot]
subdifferential)
.

Let
\phi:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
be a function satisfying the
lower bound (
3.8
),
\mu,\nu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

with
\phi(\mu)\in\mathbb{R}
.
Then

{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi[\mu]\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\nu\in\partial^{-}\phi(\mu),\quad{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu,\nu).

(4.28)

In particular

\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi

can be obtained as the image of the
of the graph of
\partial^{-}\phi
in

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
through the
multivalued map
\Gamma_{o}:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\rightrightarrows{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})
.

Conversely,
the graph of
\partial^{-}\phi
can be obtained as the image
of
\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
through the map

\pi^{1}_{\sharp}\times\pi^{2}_{\sharp}
.

It is worth highlighting
the following consequence of the previous result, which does not immediately appear from the definition of total subdifferential.

Corollary 4.10

(Total subdifferentials are optimal couplings)
.

For every function
\phi:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}

its total subdifferential

\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi
is contained in the set of optimal couplings

{\mathcal{P}}_{2,o}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})
.

The above corollary suggests a lifting procedure of a subset
{\mathfrak{F}}\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
which
defines
a MPVF
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}
by the formula

\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}:=\Big\{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}^{2}):{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in\Gamma_{o}(\mu,\nu)\quad\text{for some }(\mu,\nu)\in{\mathfrak{F}}\Big\}=(\pi^{1}_{\sharp},\pi^{2}_{\sharp})^{-1}({\mathfrak{F}})\cap{\mathcal{P}}_{2,o}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}}).

(4.29)

Theorem 4.11

(Total cyclical monotonicity and
{\mathsf{w}}_{2}^{2}
-cyclical monotonicity)
.

If the MPVF
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}
is totally cyclically monotone then
the set

{\mathfrak{F}}=(\pi^{1}_{\sharp},\pi^{2}_{\sharp})\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}

defined as in (
4.26
) is
{\mathsf{w}}_{2}^{2}
-cyclically monotone in

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

in the sense that for every

N\in\mathbb{N}
, every choice of pairs

(\mu_{1},\nu_{1}),\cdots,(\mu_{N},\nu_{N})\in{\mathfrak{F}}
and every permutation

\sigma\in{\mathrm{S}}_{N}

\sum_{n=1}^{N}{\mathsf{w}}^{2}(\mu_{n},\nu_{n})\leq\sum_{n=1}^{N}{\mathsf{w}}^{2}(\mu_{n},\nu_{\sigma(n)}).

(4.30)

Conversly, if

{\mathfrak{F}}
is
{\mathsf{w}}_{2}^{2}
-cyclically monotone in

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
according to (
4.30
)
then
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}
defined by (
4.29
) is totally
cyclically monotone.

Proof.

Let us first observe that for arbitrary choice of
(\mu_{n},\nu_{n})\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
the identity (
2.12
) yields

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{N}{\mathsf{w}}^{2}(\mu_{n},\nu_{\sigma(n)})-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{N}{\mathsf{w}}^{2}(\mu_{n},\nu_{n})=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Big([\mu_{n},\nu_{n}]-[\mu_{n},\nu_{\sigma(n)}]\Big).

Suppose that
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}
is totally cyclically monotone;
by Theorem
4.6
we can find a proper totally convex
l.s.c. function
\phi:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}

such that
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}\subset\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi
so that in particular
all the elements of
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}
are optimal couplings of

{\mathcal{P}}_{2,o}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})
.
For every pair
(\mu_{n},\nu_{n})\in{\mathfrak{F}}
,
n=1,\cdots,N

we thus get

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{N}[\mu_{n},\nu_{n}]-[\mu_{n},\nu_{\sigma(n)}]

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{n=1}^{N}\phi(\mu_{n})+\phi^{\star}(\nu_{n})-\Big(\phi(\mu_{n})+\phi^{\star}(\nu_{\sigma(n)})\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\phi^{\star}(\nu_{n})-\sum_{n=1}^{N}\phi^{\star}(\nu_{\sigma(n)})=0.

Conversely, if
{\mathfrak{F}}
is
{\mathsf{w}}_{2}^{2}
-cyclically monotone
and
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}
is defined as in (
4.29
)
we have for every
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(({\mathrm{H}}^{2})^{N})
with

\pi^{n}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\theta}}={\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{n}\in\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{N}\int{\langle}y_{n},x_{n}-x_{\sigma(n)}{\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\theta}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\int{\langle}y_{n},x_{n}{\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\sum_{n=1}^{N}\int{\langle}y_{n},x_{\sigma(n)}{\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\theta}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\int{\langle}y,x{\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{n}(x,y)-\sum_{n=1}^{N}\int{\langle}y_{n},x_{\sigma(n)}{\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\theta}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{N}[\mu_{n},\nu_{n}]-\sum_{n=1}^{N}\int{\langle}y_{n},x_{\sigma(n)}{\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\theta}}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{n=1}^{N}[\mu_{n},\nu_{n}]-\sum_{n=1}^{N}[\mu_{n},\nu_{\sigma(n)}]\geq 0

∎

5
L^{2}
-Random Optimal Transport

In this section we will apply the main results of Section

3
for totally convex functions
and of Section
4
for totally cyclically monotone MPVF
to optimal transport in
{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
.

5.1
Random couplings and couplings of random measures

Let us first observe that
to every random coupling law

{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})={\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}}))
we can associate a
coupling between (laws of) random measures

\mathit{\Pi}
by the formula

{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}=(\pi^{1}_{\sharp},\pi^{2}_{\sharp})_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}},\quad{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}=\int\delta_{\pi^{1}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\otimes\delta_{\pi^{2}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}).

(5.1)

If
{\mathtt{M}}_{i}=\pi^{i}_{\sharp}{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}
,
it is not difficult to check that

{\mathsf{W}}_{2}^{2}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})\leq\int{\mathsf{w}}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\,{\mathrm{d}}{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\leq\int\Big(\int|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(x_{1},x_{2})\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}).

(5.2)

Conversely,
using the fact that the continuous map

\pi^{1}_{\sharp}\times\pi^{2}_{\sharp}:{\mathcal{P}}_{2,o}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})\to{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\quad\text{is surjective,}

(5.3)

given a coupling
{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}))

we can find a
{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{P}}_{2,o}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}}))

(thus concentrated on optimal couplings)
such that

{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}=(\pi^{1}_{\sharp},\pi^{2}_{\sharp})_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}},\quad\int{\mathsf{w}}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\,{\mathrm{d}}{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\int\Big(\int|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(x_{1},x_{2})\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}).

(5.4)

We immediately deduce an equivalent characterization of
{\mathsf{W}}_{2}
in
terms of random coupling laws. We write

\pi^{i}_{\sharp\sharp}:=(\pi^{i}_{\sharp})_{\sharp}

and we call

\mathrm{R}\Gamma({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2}):=\Big\{{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}}),\pi^{i}_{\sharp\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}={\mathtt{M}}_{i}\Big\}.

(5.5)

Proposition 5.1

(Random couplings formulation of OT between random measures)
.

For every
{\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
we have

{\mathsf{W}}_{2}^{2}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})=\min\Big\{\int\Big(\int|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(x_{1},x_{2})\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}):{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}\in\mathrm{R}\Gamma({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})\Big\}.

(5.6)

Moreover,
{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}
is optimal for (
5.6
) if and only if

{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}
is concentrated on the optimal couplings of

{\mathcal{P}}_{2,o}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})
and

{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}=(\pi^{1}_{\sharp},\pi^{2}_{\sharp})_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}\in\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})
.

We denote by
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})
the set of optimal random couplings
for (
5.6
).

Proof.

By (
5.2
) it clear that the infimum of the quantities in the
right-hand side of
(
5.6
) is larger than
{\mathsf{W}}_{2}^{2}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})
and
that if
{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}
satisfies the optimal condition

{\mathsf{W}}_{2}^{2}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})=\int\Big(\int|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(x_{1},x_{2})\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}),

then
{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}=(\pi^{1}_{\sharp},\pi^{2}_{\sharp})_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}
is optimal thanks
to (
5.2
).

On the other hand, we can prove the equality by choosing an optimal coupling
{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}\in\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})

and a corresponding lift
{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}
as in (
5.4
).
∎

5.2
L^{2}
-Optimal Transport for laws of random measures

Let us first introduce the natural pairing in
{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

associated with
the maximal correlation pairing

[\cdot,\cdot]
.

Definition 5.2
.

For every
{\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
we set

[\kern-2.0pt[{\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2}]\kern-2.0pt]:=\max\Big\{\int_{{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})}[\mu,\nu]\,{\mathrm{d}}{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}(\mu,\nu):{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}\in\Gamma({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{{\mathtt{M}}_{2}})\Big\}.

(5.7)

Recalling the identity (
2.12
) we immediately have
the corresponding property at the level of
{\mathsf{W}}_{2}
:

Lemma 5.3
.

For every
{\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
we have

{\mathsf{W}}_{2}^{2}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{{\mathtt{M}}_{2}})={\mathsf{M}}_{2}^{2}({\mathtt{M}}_{1})+{\mathsf{M}}_{2}^{2}({{\mathtt{M}}_{2}})-2[\kern-2.0pt[{\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2}]\kern-2.0pt].

(5.8)

In particular, the class of optimal couplings for (
5.7
)
coincides with
the class
\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})
of optimal couplings for (
2.14
).
Moreover, we have the equivalent formulation

[\kern-2.0pt[{\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2}]\kern-2.0pt]=\max\Big\{\int\Big(\int\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(x_{1},x_{2})\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}):{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}\in\mathrm{R}\Gamma({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})\Big\}

(5.9)

whose solution is provided by the same class

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})
of optimal random couplings of (
5.6
).

The general duality result for Optimal Transport and
Corollary
3.15
then yield:

Theorem 5.4

(Optimal Kantorovich potentials for ROT)
.

For every proper lower semicontinuous
function
\zeta:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}

\displaystyle\int\zeta(\mu)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathtt{M}}_{1}(\mu)+\int\zeta^{\star}(\nu)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathtt{M}}_{2}(\nu)\geq[\kern-2.0pt[{\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2}]\kern-2.0pt]

(5.10)

and there exists a totally convex, lower
semicontinuous and proper function

\phi:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
such that

\int\phi(\mu)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathtt{M}}_{1}(\mu)+\int\phi^{\star}(\nu)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathtt{M}}_{2}(\nu)=[\kern-2.0pt[{\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2}]\kern-2.0pt].

(5.11)

The corresponding potential
{\mathcal{U}}=\frac{1}{2}{\mathsf{m}}_{2}^{2}-\phi
defined as in (
3.28
)
satisfy

{\mathcal{V}}={\mathcal{U}}^{\mathsf{c}}=\frac{1}{2}{\mathsf{m}}_{2}^{2}-\phi^{\star},\quad\int{\mathcal{U}}(\mu)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathtt{M}}_{1}(\mu)+\int{\mathcal{U}}^{\mathsf{c}}(\nu)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathtt{M}}_{2}(\nu)=\frac{1}{2}{\mathsf{W}}_{2}^{2}({{\mathtt{M}}_{1}},{{\mathtt{M}}_{2}}),

(5.12)

with respect to the cost

{\mathsf{c}}:=\frac{1}{2}{\mathsf{w}}_{2}^{2}

We collect now the main results concerning optimality and duality.

Theorem 5.5

(Optimality conditions)
.

Let
{\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
,

{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}\in\Gamma({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}))
,

{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})
supported in

{\mathcal{P}}_{2,o}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})

and associated with
\mathit{\Pi}
via
(\pi^{1}_{\sharp},\pi^{2}_{\sharp})_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}={\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}
as in (
5.4
), so that
in particular

(\pi^{1}_{\sharp},\pi^{2}_{\sharp})(\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}})\subset\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}\subset\overline{(\pi^{1}_{\sharp},\pi^{2}_{\sharp})(\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}})},\quad\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}\subset(\pi^{1}_{\sharp},\pi^{2}_{\sharp})^{-1}(\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}})\cap{\mathcal{P}}_{2,o}({\mathrm{H}}^{2}).

(5.13)

The following properties are equivalent:

1.

\mathit{\Pi}
is an optimal plan in
\Gamma_{o}({{\mathtt{M}}_{1}},{{\mathtt{M}}_{2}})

for
{\mathsf{W}}_{2}
or, equivalently, for
[\kern-2.0pt[\cdot,\cdot]\kern-2.0pt]
.

2.

\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits({\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}})
is
{\mathsf{w}}_{2}^{2}
-cyclically monotone (recall Theorem
4.11
).

3.

{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}
is an optimal random coupling law in
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})

for
{\mathsf{W}}_{2}
(according to
(
5.6
)) or, equivalently,
for
[\kern-2.0pt[\cdot,\cdot]\kern-2.0pt]
(according to
(
5.9
)).

4.

\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits({\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}})
is totally cyclically monotone.

5.

There exists a totally convex, lower
semicontinuous and proper function

\phi:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
such that

\phi(\mu)+\phi^{\star}(\nu)=[\mu,\nu]\quad\text{for ${\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}$-a.e.~$(\mu,\nu)\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})$},

(5.14)

i.e.
\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits({\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}})\subset\partial^{-}\phi
. Moreover
such a property holds for

every
pair of optimal Kantorovich potentials satisfying (
5.11
).

6.

There exists a totally convex, lower
semicontinuous and proper function

\phi:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
such that

\phi(\pi^{1}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})+\phi^{\star}(\pi^{2}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})=\int{\langle}x_{1},x_{2}{\rangle}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\quad\text{for ${\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}$-a.e.~${\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})$},

(5.15)

i.e.
\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits({\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}})\subset\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi.

Moreover, such a property holds for every pair of optimal Kantorovich potentials satisfying
(
5.11
).

Proof.

The equivalence 1
\Leftrightarrow
2 follows by the general theory of
optimal transport.

The equivalence 1
\Leftrightarrow
3 follows by
Proposition
5.1
.

Clearly 2
\Rightarrow
3
by the last inclusion of
(
5.13
)
and the second part of Theorem
4.11
.
On the other hand, if
\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits({\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}})
is totally cyclically monotone
the same Theorem

4.11
and
the inclusion

\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}\subset\overline{(\pi^{1}_{\sharp},\pi^{2}_{\sharp})\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}}

shows that

\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}
is
{\mathsf{w}}_{2}^{2}
-cyclically
monotone, and therefore
\mathit{\Pi}
is optimal.

In order to prove the implication
1
\Rightarrow
5 it is sufficient to select
an optimal totally convex proper and l.s.c. function
\phi

satisfying
(
5.11
).
The optimality of
\mathit{\Pi}
yields

\int\Big(\phi(\mu_{1})+\phi^{\star}(\mu_{2})-[\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=0

(5.16)

which implies (
5.14
) thanks to the Kantorovich-Fenchel
inequality (
3.11
).
On the other hand,
if
\mathit{\Pi}
satisfies (
5.14
) we get
(
5.16
) and the optimality of
\mathit{\Pi}
thanks to (
5.10
).

A similar argument shows the equivalence with Claim 6.
∎

As in the usual deterministic case,
when
{\mathtt{M}}_{2}
is concentrated on a set of measures with
uniformly bounded quadratic moment, we
can find a Lipschitz totally convex optimal Kantorovich potential.

Corollary 5.6
.

Let us suppose that there exists
R>0
such that

{\mathsf{m}}_{2}(\mu)\leq R\quad\text{for ${\mathtt{M}}_{2}$-a.e.~$\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})$}.

(5.17)

Then we can find a totally convex
R
-Lipschitz function

\phi_{R}:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying
(
5.11
).

Proof.

Let
\phi
as in Claim 5 of Theorem
5.5

and let us set

\psi_{R}(\nu):=\phi^{\star}(\nu)\quad\text{if }{\mathsf{m}}_{2}(\nu)\leq R,\quad\psi_{R}(\nu):=+\infty\text{ otherwise.}

Clearly
\psi_{R}
is proper, totally convex and lower semicontinuous;
the pair
(\phi,\psi_{R})
satisfies

\phi(\mu)+\psi_{R}(\nu)\geq[\mu,\nu],\quad\phi(\mu)+\psi_{R}(\nu)=[\mu,\nu]\quad\text{for ${\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}$-a.e.~$(\mu,\nu)\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})$}.

It is easy to check that
\phi_{R}:=(\psi_{R})^{\star}
still provides
an optimal totally convex Kantorovich potential
with

(\phi_{R})^{\star}=\psi_{R}
;
\phi_{R}
is also

R
-Lipschitz since

\phi_{R}(\mu)=\sup\Big\{[\mu,\nu]-\phi^{\star}(\nu):{\mathsf{m}}_{2}(\nu)\leq R\Big\}.

(5.18)

∎

5.3
The structure of minimal geodesics in
{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

We can apply
the previous results to identify
minimal geodesics
in
{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

and to characterize their
transport structure.
Let us first recall that
a minimal geodesic

({\mathtt{M}}_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}

connecting
{\mathtt{M}}_{0}
to
{\mathtt{M}}_{1}

is a curve in
{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

satisfying

{\mathsf{W}}_{2}({\mathtt{M}}_{s},{\mathtt{M}}_{t})=|t-s|{\mathsf{W}}_{2}({\mathtt{M}}_{0},{\mathtt{M}}_{1})\quad\text{for every $s,t\in[0,1]$.}

(5.19)

We also say that
\tilde{\mathtt{M}}
is a
t
-intermediate point between
{\mathtt{M}}_{0}
and
{\mathtt{M}}_{1}
,
t\in(0,1)
, if

{\mathsf{W}}_{2}({\mathtt{M}}_{0},\tilde{\mathtt{M}})=t{\mathsf{W}}_{2}({\mathtt{M}}_{0},{\mathtt{M}}_{1}),\quad{\mathsf{W}}_{2}(\tilde{\mathtt{M}},{\mathtt{M}}_{1})=(1-t){\mathsf{W}}_{2}({\mathtt{M}}_{0},{\mathtt{M}}_{1}).

(5.20)

We will use the interpolating maps

\pi^{1\shortrightarrow 2}_{t}
of
(
1.11
)
and we will consider Borel maps defined
in sets of the form
(recall (
4.25
))

{\mathcal{S}}({\mathrm{H}},D):=\Big\{(x,\mu)\in{\mathrm{H}}\times D:x\in\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits\mu\Big\},\quad D\text{ Borel subset of }{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}),\quad{\mathcal{S}}({\mathrm{H}})={\mathcal{S}}({\mathrm{H}},{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})),

(5.21)

which are Borel subsets of

{\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

[
CSS23
, (4.23)]
.

Theorem 5.7

(Structure of minimal geodesics in
{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
)
.

Let
{\mathtt{M}}_{0},{\mathtt{M}}_{1}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
,
let
\phi,\phi^{\star}

be a pair of optimal Kantorovich potentials for
{\mathtt{M}}_{i}
,
and let
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}=\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}}).

1.

For every optimal random coupling law

{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}\in\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}}_{0},{\mathtt{M}}_{1})

the curve

{\mathtt{M}}_{t}:=(\pi_{t}^{1\shortrightarrow 2})_{\sharp\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}},\ t\in[0,1]\quad\text{is a minimal geodesic.}

(5.22)

2.

For every
t\in(0,1)

the set

\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}_{t}:=(\pi^{1\shortrightarrow 2}_{t})_{\sharp}(\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}})

is closed in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

and there exists two uniquely characterized continuous maps

{\boldsymbol{f}}_{t,i}:{\mathcal{S}}({\mathrm{H}},\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}_{t})\to{\mathrm{H}}
,
i=0,1,

inverting
(\pi^{1\shortrightarrow 2}_{t})_{\sharp}
in the sense that

{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}},\quad\mu=(\pi^{1\shortrightarrow 2}_{t})_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\quad\Rightarrow\quad{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=({\boldsymbol{f}}_{t,0}(\cdot,\mu),{\boldsymbol{f}}_{t,1}(\cdot,\mu))_{\sharp}\mu.

(5.23)

Moreover,

{\boldsymbol{f}}_{t,i}(\cdot,\mu)
is Lipschitz
in
\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits(\mu)
and cyclically monotone
in
{\mathrm{H}}
,
the maps

\mathscr{F}_{t,i}:\mu\mapsto{\boldsymbol{f}}_{t,i}(\cdot,\mu)_{\sharp}\mu

are Lipshitz from

\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}_{t}
to
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

and cyclically monotone in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}).

3.

If
t\in(0,1)
and

\tilde{\mathtt{M}}
is a
t
-intermediate point between
{\mathtt{M}}_{0}
and
{\mathtt{M}}_{1}

then

\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits(\tilde{\mathtt{M}})\subset\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}_{t}

and
the formula

\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}=(\mathscr{G}_{t})_{\sharp}\tilde{\mathtt{M}}\quad\text{where}\quad\mathscr{G}_{t}(\mu):=({\boldsymbol{f}}_{t,0}(\cdot,\mu),{\boldsymbol{f}}_{t,1}(\cdot,\mu))_{\sharp}\mu

(5.24)

provides the unique

\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}\in\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}}_{0},{\mathtt{M}}_{1})

such that

\tilde{\mathtt{M}}=(\pi_{t}^{1\shortrightarrow 2})_{\sharp\sharp}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}

and correspondingly the unique geodesic

({\mathtt{M}}_{s})_{s\in[0,1]}

connecting
{\mathtt{M}}_{0}
to
{\mathtt{M}}_{1}

such that
{\mathtt{M}}_{t}=\tilde{\mathtt{M}}
.
Moreover

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{o}(\tilde{\mathtt{M}},{\mathtt{M}}_{i})
,

i=0,1
,
contains the unique element

\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}_{t,i}
given by

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}_{t,0}

\displaystyle=(\pi^{1},\pi^{1\shortrightarrow 2}_{t})_{\sharp\sharp}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}=(\mathscr{G}_{t,0})_{\sharp}\tilde{\mathtt{M}},\quad\mathscr{G}_{t,0}(\mu)=({\boldsymbol{f}}_{t,0}(\cdot,\mu),{\boldsymbol{i}})_{\sharp}\mu

(5.25)

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}_{t,1}

\displaystyle=(\pi^{1\shortrightarrow 2}_{t},\pi^{2})_{\sharp\sharp}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}=(\mathscr{G}_{t,1})_{\sharp}\tilde{\mathtt{M}},\quad\mathscr{G}_{t,1}(\mu)=({\boldsymbol{i}},{\boldsymbol{f}}_{t,1}(\cdot,\mu))_{\sharp}\mu

which is concentrated on
deterministic optimal couplings, and

\tilde{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}_{t,i}=(\pi^{1}_{\sharp},\pi^{2}_{\sharp})_{\sharp}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}_{t,i}=(\mathrm{Id}\times\mathscr{F}_{t,i})_{\sharp}\tilde{\mathtt{M}},\quad i=0,1,

(5.26)

is the unique optimal coupling
in
\Gamma_{o}(\tilde{\mathtt{M}},{\mathtt{M}}_{i})
.

4.

For every

t\in(0,1)
the conjugate
functions (recall the definition of Moreau-Yosida regularization
(
3.16
))

\phi^{t}:=\frac{1-t}{2}{\mathsf{m}}_{2}^{2}+t\phi,\quad(\phi^{t})^{\star}=t(\hat{\phi}^{\star})_{1/t-1}\circ{\mathfrak{d}}_{t^{-1}}

(5.27)

provide a pair of optimal Kantorovich potentials for
{\mathtt{M}}_{0}

and any
t
-intermediate point

\tilde{\mathtt{M}}
between
{\mathtt{M}}_{0}
and
{\mathtt{M}}_{1}.

Similarly

(1-t)\phi_{t/(1-t)}\circ{\mathfrak{d}}_{(1-t)^{-1}},\quad(1-t)\phi^{\star}+\frac{t}{2}{\mathsf{m}}_{2}^{2}

(5.28)

is a pair of optimal Kantorovich potentials for
\tilde{\mathtt{M}}

and
{\mathtt{M}}_{1}
.

Remark 5.8
.

The above Theorem recovers in a much more precise form
various results that
hold for
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{X}})

in suitable classes of
metric spaces
{\mathrm{X}}
, see

[
Vil09
, Chap. 7]
.
See in particular the
nonbranching property
(stated in locally compact spaces)

[
Vil09
, Corollary 7.32]

for Claim 2,
the
“interpolation of prices”

[
Vil09
, Theorem 7.36]

concerning Claim 3, and the related bibliographical notes.

Proof.

Claim 1.

For
0\leq s<t\leq 1

we define
the maps

\pi^{1\shortrightarrow 2}_{s,t}:{\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}}\to{\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}}

\pi^{1\shortrightarrow 2}_{s,t}(x_{0},x_{1}):=(\pi^{1\shortrightarrow 2}_{s}(x_{0},x_{1}),\pi^{1\shortrightarrow 2}_{t}(x_{0},x_{1}));

we observe that

|\pi^{1\shortrightarrow 2}_{s}(x_{0},x_{1})-\pi^{1\shortrightarrow 2}_{t}(x_{0},x_{1})|=|t-s|\cdot|x_{1}-x_{0}|

so that
the random coupling

{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}_{s,t}=(\pi^{1\shortrightarrow 2}_{s,t})_{\sharp\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}

belongs to
\mathrm{R}\Gamma({\mathtt{M}}_{s},{\mathtt{M}}_{t})

and thus yields

{\mathsf{W}}_{2}({\mathtt{M}}_{s},{\mathtt{M}}_{t})\leq|t-s|{\mathsf{W}}_{2}({\mathtt{M}}_{0},{\mathtt{M}}_{1})\quad\text{if }0\leq s<t\leq 1.

The triangle inequality then yields
(
5.19
).

Claim 2.

Let us consider the
Lagrangian lifting

\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}=\partial\hat{\phi}\subset{\mathcal{H}}\times{\mathcal{H}}

of
the total subdifferential

\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}=\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi
of
\phi
and let us set

I_{t}(X_{0},X_{1}):=(1-t)X_{0}+tX_{1},\quad\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}_{t}=I_{t}(\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}})=\Big\{\tilde{X}=(1-t)X_{0}+tX_{1}:(X_{0},X_{1})\in\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}\Big\}

which is clearly a set invariant by m.p.i.
By monotonicity, if

\tilde{X}=I_{t}(X_{0},X_{1}),

\tilde{X}^{\prime}=I_{t}(X_{0}^{\prime},X_{1}^{\prime})
for
(X_{0},X_{1}),(X_{0}^{\prime},X_{1}^{\prime})\in\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}

we have

|\tilde{X}-\tilde{X}^{\prime}|^{2}\geq(1-t)|X_{0}-X_{0}^{\prime}|^{2}+t|X_{1}-X_{1}^{\prime}|^{2},

(5.29)

which shows that
\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}_{t}
is closed
and there exist Lipschitz maps

F_{t,i}:\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}_{t}\to{\mathrm{H}}
such that

\tilde{X}=I_{t}(X_{0},X_{1}),\ (X_{0},X_{1})\in\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}\quad\Rightarrow\quad X_{i}=F_{t,i}(\tilde{X}).

(5.30)

Since
F_{t,i}
are also invariant by m.p.i.,
the general extension and representation Theorem
4.8 of
[
CSS25
]

shows that there is a unique pair of continuous maps

{\boldsymbol{f}}_{t,i}:{\mathcal{S}}({\mathrm{H}},\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}_{t})\to{\mathrm{H}}

representing
F_{t,i}
as

F_{t,i}[\tilde{X}](q)={\boldsymbol{f}}_{t,i}(\tilde{X}(q),\iota(\tilde{X}))

(5.31)

with the properties stated in Claim 2.
(
5.31
) clearly yields
(
5.23
) since

\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits(\mu)\subset\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}_{t}\subset\iota(\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}_{t})
.
The cyclical monotonity of
{\boldsymbol{f}}_{t,i}(\cdot,\mu)

and of
\mathscr{F}_{t,i}
follows from the corresponding
cyclical monotonicity of
the maps
F_{t,i}
in
{\mathcal{H}}
, which
in turn follows by the fact that
they are the inverse of
the cyclically monotone sets

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}_{t,0}:={}

\displaystyle(I_{0},I_{t})(\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}})=\Big\{(X_{0},(1-t)X_{0}+tX_{1}):(X_{0},X_{1})\in\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}\Big\},

(5.32)

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}_{t,1}:={}

\displaystyle(I_{1},I_{t})(\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}})=\Big\{(X_{1},(1-t)X_{0}+tX_{1})):(X_{0},X_{1})\in\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}\Big\}.

Claim 3.

Let
{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}_{0}\in\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}}_{0},\tilde{\mathtt{M}})

and
{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}_{1}\in\Gamma_{o}(\tilde{\mathtt{M}},{\mathtt{M}}_{1})
.
By the glueing Lemma
we find a tri-plan

{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}\in\Gamma({\mathtt{M}}_{0},\tilde{\mathtt{M}},{\mathtt{M}}_{1})

such that

\pi^{1,2}_{\sharp}{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}={\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}_{0}
,

\pi^{2,3}_{\sharp}{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}={\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}_{1}.

Consider now the closed set

{\mathcal{Q}}:=\Big\{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}}):\pi^{1,2}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\text{ and }\pi^{2,3}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\text{ belong to }{\mathcal{P}}_{2,o}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})\Big\}.

(5.33)

Since the map

(\pi^{1}_{\sharp}\times\pi^{2}_{\sharp}\times\pi^{3}_{\sharp}):{\mathcal{Q}}\to\big({\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\big)^{3}

is surjective, we can find

{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{Q}})\subset{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})

such that

(\pi^{1}_{\sharp}\times\pi^{2}_{\sharp}\times\pi^{3}_{\sharp})_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}={\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}
.

We have

\pi^{1,2}_{\sharp\sharp}({\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}})\in\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}}_{0},\tilde{\mathtt{M}}),\quad\pi^{2,3}_{\sharp\sharp}({\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}})\in\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{o}(\tilde{\mathtt{M}},{\mathtt{M}}_{1})

and by the elementary inequality

(a+b)^{2}\leq\frac{1}{t}a^{2}+\frac{1}{1-t}b^{2}

\displaystyle{\mathsf{W}}_{2}^{2}({\mathtt{M}}_{0},{\mathtt{M}}_{1})

\displaystyle\leq\int\int|x_{1}-x_{3}|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}({\boldsymbol{\gamma}})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{t}\int\int|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}({\boldsymbol{\gamma}})+\frac{1}{1-t}\int\int|x_{2}-x_{3}|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}({\boldsymbol{\gamma}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t}{\mathsf{W}}_{2}^{2}({\mathtt{M}}_{0},\tilde{\mathtt{M}})+\frac{1}{1-t}{\mathsf{W}}_{2}^{2}(\tilde{\mathtt{M}},{\mathtt{M}}_{1})={\mathsf{W}}_{2}^{2}({\mathtt{M}}_{0},{\mathtt{M}}_{1})

we deduce that

\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}:=\pi^{1,3}_{\sharp\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}\in\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}}_{0},{\mathtt{M}}_{1})

and

|x_{1}-x_{3}|^{2}=\frac{1}{t}|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2}+\frac{1}{1-t}|x_{2}-x_{3}|^{2}\text{ on }\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits({\boldsymbol{\gamma}})\quad\text{for ${\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}$-a.e.~${\boldsymbol{\gamma}}$},

so that

{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}
-a.e.
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
is supported in the set

{\mathrm{H}}^{3}_{t}:=\Big\{(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in{\mathrm{H}}^{3}:x_{2}=(1-t)x_{1}+tx_{3}\Big\}

(5.34)

and therefore

\tilde{\mathtt{M}}=(\pi^{1\shortrightarrow 2}_{t})_{\sharp\sharp}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}.

In order to show that
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}
is unique,
we observe that any random coupling
in
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}}_{0},{\mathtt{M}}_{1})

has support in
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}

so that
\tilde{\mathtt{M}}
has support
in
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}_{t}
and
we can then apply (
5.23
)
which yields (
5.24
).
The above discussion also shows that

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{o}(\tilde{\mathtt{M}},{\mathtt{M}}_{i})

and
\Gamma_{o}(\tilde{\mathtt{M}},{\mathtt{M}}_{i})

are uniqueley characterized by
(
5.25
) and (
5.26
)

Claim 4.

In order to check (
5.27
)
(the argument for (
5.28
) is similar)
we use the fact that

\tilde{\mathtt{M}}
is supported
in
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}_{t}=\iota(\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}_{t})
.
On the other hand,
the set

\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}_{t,0}
defined by
(
5.32
)
is the graph of the
subdifferential of
the Lagrangian lifting of
\phi^{t}

\hat{\phi}^{t}(X):=\frac{1-t}{2}\|X\|_{{\mathcal{H}}}^{2}+t\hat{\phi}(X)

whose Legendre-Fenchel transform
can be expressed in terms of
the Moreau-Yosida regularization of

\hat{\phi}^{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{$\ast$}}}}
(recall (
3.19
)
and (
3.22
))
and corresponds to the Lagrangian lifting
of the functional
(\phi^{t})^{\star}
given in
(
5.27
).
Since

\hat{\phi}^{t}(X)+(\hat{\phi}^{t})^{\mathop{\scalebox{1}{\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{$\ast$}}}}(Y)=\langle X,Y\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=[\iota(X),\iota(Y)]\quad\text{for every }(X,Y)\in\hat{\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}}_{t,0}

we get the proof of the claim.
∎

5.4
Lifting (laws of) random measures of
{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
to measures on Lagrangian maps in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{H}})
.

In the previous sections, we exploited the lifting
technique of Proposition
5.1

in order to describe optimal couplings in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}))

in terms of laws of random optimal couplings in
{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})
.

There is another lifting technique which is induced by
the
1
-Lipschitz and surjective law map
\iota:{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
.
The corresponding push-forward transformation
\iota_{\sharp}
still provides
a surjective map from
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{H}})

(the space of measures on Lagrangian maps of
{\mathcal{H}}
)
to
{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
(the space of (laws of) random measures), so that
it is natural to study the relations between optimal transport
problems
in
{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
and in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{H}})
.

First of all, since
\iota
is
1
-Lipschitz,
we observe that for every
{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{H}})
,
i=1,2
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathtt{M}}_{i}=\iota_{\sharp}{{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad{\mathsf{W}}_{2}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})\leq{\mathsf{W}}_{2,{\mathcal{H}}}({{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}},{{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}}).

(5.35)

Similarly, it is not difficult to check that given a coupling

{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{H}}\times{\mathcal{H}})
and setting

\iota_{i}:=\iota\circ\pi^{i}
,
we have

\displaystyle{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}=(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})_{\sharp}{{\mathfrak{p}}}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad\int{\mathsf{w}}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\,{\mathrm{d}}{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\leq\int\|X_{1}-X_{2}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}{{\mathfrak{p}}}(X_{1},X_{2}).

(5.36)

Eventually, still starting from

{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{H}}\times{\mathcal{H}})
and using

\iota^{2}(X,Y):=(X,Y)_{\sharp}\mathbb{M}
, we have

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}=\iota^{2}_{\sharp}{{\mathfrak{p}}}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad\int\int|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}({\boldsymbol{\gamma}})=\int\|X_{1}-X_{2}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}{{\mathfrak{p}}}(X_{1},X_{2}).

(5.37)

Since
\iota^{2}
is surjective from
{\mathcal{H}}\times{\mathcal{H}}
to

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})
,

\iota^{2}_{\sharp}
is surjective as well, so that given
{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}}))

it is always possible to find
a lifting
{\mathfrak{p}}^{\ell}
such that

{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}=\iota^{2}_{\sharp}{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\ell}}
.

If we want to
lift
\mathit{\Pi}
so that
(
5.36
) holds as an equality,
we can first select

{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{P}}_{2,o}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}}))

so that (
5.4
) holds: the lifting
{\mathfrak{p}}^{\ell}

satisfies the identity

\displaystyle{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}=(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{p}}^{\ell},\quad\int{\mathsf{w}}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\,{\mathrm{d}}{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\int\|X_{1}-X_{2}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathfrak{p}}^{\ell}(X_{1},X_{2}).

(5.38)

If moreover
{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}\in\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})

then
{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}\in\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})

and setting
{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}^{\ell}=\pi^{1}_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{p}}^{\ell},{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}^{\ell}=\pi^{2}_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{p}}^{\ell}
we get

\displaystyle{\mathtt{M}}_{i}=\iota_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}^{\ell},\quad{\mathsf{W}}_{2}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})={\mathsf{W}}_{2,{\mathcal{H}}}({\mathfrak{m}}_{1}^{\ell},{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}^{\ell}).

(5.39)

We recap the above argument in the next proposition.

Proposition 5.9

(From random OT to OT in
{\mathcal{H}}
)
.

For every pair
{\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
there exists a pair

{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}^{\ell},{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}^{\ell}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{H}})
such that (
5.39
) holds,
so that

{\mathsf{W}}_{2}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})=\min\Big\{{\mathsf{W}}_{2,{\mathcal{H}}}({\mathfrak{m}}_{1},{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}):{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{H}}),\ \iota_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}={\mathtt{M}}_{i}\Big\}.

(5.40)

If
{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}^{\ell},{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}^{\ell}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{H}})

are minimizers of (
5.40
) and
{\mathfrak{p}}^{\ell}\in\Gamma_{o}({\mathfrak{m}}_{1}^{\ell},{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}^{\ell})
in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{H}}\times{\mathcal{H}})

then

{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}=\iota^{2}_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{p}}^{\ell}\in\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})

and

\text{$\mathit{{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}}$}=(\pi^{1}_{\sharp},\pi^{2}_{\sharp}){\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}=(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{p}}^{\ell}\in\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})
.

We want to highlight that the optimal lifted measures

{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}^{\ell},{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}^{\ell}
given by the previous proposition
typically depend on both the measures
{\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2}

and in general we cannot fix an arbitrary
{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}^{\ell}
such that

\iota_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}^{\ell}={\mathtt{M}}_{1}
.
We want to find a sufficient condition on
{\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2}
for which
the following property holds:

\begin{gathered}\text{for every ${\mathfrak{m}}_{1}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{H}})$ such that\quad$\iota_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}={\mathtt{M}}_{1}$\quad there exists ${\mathfrak{m}}_{2}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{H}})$ with}\\
\iota_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}={\mathtt{M}}_{2},\quad{\mathsf{W}}_{2}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})={\mathsf{W}}_{2,{\mathcal{H}}}({{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}},{{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}}).\end{gathered}

(5.41)

A crucial role in this respect is played by
the set of “deterministic” couplings

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{\rm det}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})

which are concentrated on maps:

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm det}_{2}}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}}):={}

\displaystyle\Big\{({\boldsymbol{i}}\times{\boldsymbol{f}})_{\sharp}\mu:\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}),\ {\boldsymbol{f}}\in L^{2}({\mathrm{H}},\mu;{\mathrm{H}})\Big\},

(5.42)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}_{2,o}^{\rm det}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}}):={}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{\rm det}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})\cap{\mathcal{P}}_{2,o}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}}).

First of all, we will show a simple condition for which
there exists an optimal coupling
{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}\in\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})

which is concentrated on
{{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm det}_{2,o}}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})
.

Lemma 5.10
.

An optimal coupling
{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2,o}({\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}))
satisfies the property

\text{for ${\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}$-a.e.~$(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})$}\quad\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\cap{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{\rm det}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})\neq\emptyset

(5.43)

if and only if

\text{there exists
${\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{P}}_{2,o}^{\rm det}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}}))$ such that}\quad{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}=(\pi^{1}_{\sharp},\pi^{2}_{\sharp})_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}.

(5.44)

Proof.

Let us set
{\mathcal{O}}:=\Big\{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}):\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\cap{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{\rm det}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})\neq\emptyset\Big\}.

We can equivalently characterize
{\mathcal{O}}

as the image
of the Borel set
{\mathcal{P}}_{2,o}^{\rm det}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})
through the continuous map

\pi^{1}_{\sharp}\times\pi^{2}_{\sharp}
,
so that
{\mathcal{O}}
is a Souslin (and therefore universally measurable) set.

(
5.43
) just says that
\mathit{\Pi}
is concentrated on
{\mathcal{O}}
, and therefore its equivalence with (
5.44
) follows
by Theorem
2.1
.
∎

Theorem 5.11
.

Let
\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}\in\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})
satisfy (
5.43
),
let
{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}
as in (
5.44
),
and let
{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{H}})
such that

\iota_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}={\mathtt{M}}_{1}
.
Then there exists

{\mathfrak{p}}^{\ell}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2,o}({\mathcal{H}}\times{\mathcal{H}})
such that

\pi^{1}_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{p}}^{\ell}={\mathfrak{m}}_{1}
and

\iota^{2}_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{p}}^{\ell}={\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}
, so that, in particular,

(\iota_{1},\iota_{2})_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{p}}={\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}

and setting
{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}=\pi^{2}_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{p}}^{\ell}
we have

\iota_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}={\mathtt{M}}_{2},\quad{\mathsf{W}}_{2}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})={\mathsf{W}}_{2,{\mathcal{H}}}({\mathfrak{m}}_{1},{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}).

(5.45)

Proof.

Let us first consider the
closed subset
{\mathcal{A}}
of
{\mathcal{H}}\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})
defined by

{\mathcal{A}}:=\Big\{(X,\mu,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})\in{\mathcal{H}}\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}}):\iota(X)=\mu=\pi^{1}_{\sharp}({\boldsymbol{\gamma}})\Big\},

(5.46)

and the map
{\mathrm{A}}:{\mathcal{H}}\times{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{A}}

{\mathrm{A}}(X,Y):=(X,\iota(X),\iota^{2}(X,Y))=(X,X_{\sharp}\mathbb{M},(X,Y)_{\sharp}\mathbb{M}).

(5.47)

{\mathrm{A}}
is continuous but in general it is not surjective.
However, it is not difficult to check that the image of
{\mathrm{A}}

contains the Borel set

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}^{\rm det}:={}

\displaystyle\Big\{(X,\mu,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})\in{\mathcal{H}}\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\times{{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm det}_{2}}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}}):\iota(X)=\mu=\pi^{1}_{\sharp}({\boldsymbol{\gamma}})\Big\}

(5.48)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathcal{A}}\cap\Big({\mathcal{H}}\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\times{{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm det}_{2}}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})\Big).

In fact, if
(X,\mu,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\rm det}
then

\mu=\pi^{1}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}

and we can find a map
f_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in L^{2}({\mathrm{H}},\mu;{\mathrm{H}})
such that

{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=({\boldsymbol{i}}\times f_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})_{\sharp}\mu
.
Defining
Y:=f_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\circ X

we immediately see that
\iota^{2}(X,Y)={\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
, so that

{\mathrm{A}}(X,Y)=(X,\mu,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})
.

Let us now set

\bar{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}:=(\mathrm{Id}\times\iota)_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{H}}\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}))

and

\bar{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}:=(\pi^{1}_{\sharp}\times\mathrm{Id})_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\times{{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm det}_{2}}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}}))
.
By assumption

\pi^{2}_{\sharp}\bar{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}=\iota_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}={\mathtt{M}}_{1},\quad\pi^{1}_{\sharp}\bar{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}=(\pi^{1}_{\sharp})_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}={\mathtt{M}}_{1}

so that, by the gluing Lemma, we can find
a plan
{\boldsymbol{Q}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{H}}\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\times{{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm det}_{2}}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}}))

such that
\pi^{1\,2}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{Q}}=\bar{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}
and

\pi^{2\,3}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{Q}}=\bar{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}
.
By construction,
\bar{\mathfrak{m}}_{1}
is concentrated
on the set

\Big\{(X,\mu)\in{\mathcal{H}}\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}):\iota(X)=\mu\Big\}

and

\bar{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}
is concentrated on the set

\Big\{(\mu,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})\in{\mathcal{H}}\times{{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm det}_{2}}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}}):\mu=\pi^{1}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\Big\}

we deduce that
{\boldsymbol{Q}}
is concentrated on
{\mathcal{A}}^{\rm det}
.

By Theorem
2.1

we can find a probability measure

{\mathfrak{p}}^{\ell}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{H}}\times{\mathcal{H}})
such that

{\mathrm{A}}_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{p}}^{\ell}={\boldsymbol{Q}}
.
By the very definition of
{\mathrm{A}}
we get

\pi^{1}_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{p}}^{\ell}=(\pi^{1}\circ{\mathrm{A}})_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{p}}^{\ell}=\pi^{1}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{Q}}={\mathfrak{m}}_{1},\quad\iota^{2}_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{p}}^{\ell}=(\pi^{3}\circ{\mathrm{A}})_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}=\pi^{3}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{Q}}={\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}

and the thesis follows.
∎

Corollary 5.12
.

Let
{\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

and let us suppose that
{\mathtt{M}}_{1}
is concentrated on
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathrm{H}})
.
Then (
5.41
) holds.

6
Random Gaussian-null sets and strict Monge formulation of OT via nonlocal totally cyclically monotone fields

In this last section we want to address the uniqueness and the
Monge formulation of the
L^{2}
-OT problem in
{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
.
These questions can be settled at the usual level of
couplings of (laws of) random measures. We then seek for conditions
on
{\mathtt{M}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
ensuring that
the class of optimal coupling

\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})
contains a unique element
\mathit{\Pi}

which is concentrated on the graph of a Borel map

\mathscr{F}:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
, so that

{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}=(\mathrm{Id}\times\mathscr{F})_{\sharp}{\mathtt{M}}_{1},\quad{\mathtt{M}}_{2}=\mathscr{F}_{\sharp}{\mathtt{M}}_{1},\quad{\mathsf{W}}_{2}^{2}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})=\int_{{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})}{\mathsf{w}}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\mathscr{F}(\mu))\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathtt{M}}_{1}(\mu);

(6.1)

this property is equivalent to asking for

{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}\in{{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm det}_{2}}({\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}))
.

A second formulation involves random couplings and provides a more
refined description of
\mathscr{F}
: we look for conditions ensuring
that
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})
contains a unique element
{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}

that is concentrated on the graph of a deterministic
totally cyclically monotone field
{\boldsymbol{f}}:{\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to{\mathrm{H}}
.

6.1
The strict Monge formulation

In order to describe such a construction, we first observe that every

{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})
can be disintegrated with respect to

{\mathtt{M}}_{1}=\pi^{1}_{\sharp\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}
to obtain a
Borel family
{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}_{\mu}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})
indexed by
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
and
concentrated on the set of couplings

\Gamma(\mu):=\Big\{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}}):\pi^{1}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\mu\Big\}

for
{\mathtt{M}}_{1}
-a.e.
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
.
When
\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits({\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}})\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{2,o}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})

(as in the case of optimal couplings)
then

\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits({\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}_{\mu})\subset\Gamma_{o}(\mu):=\Gamma(\mu)\cap{\mathcal{P}}_{2,o}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})
.

If
{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}=(\pi^{1}_{\sharp},\pi^{2}_{\sharp})_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}

then
{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}_{\mu}

characterizes the
family of measures in

{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
arising from the disintegration

({\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}_{\mu})_{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})}

of
\mathit{\Pi}
with respect to its first marginal
through the formula

{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}_{\mu}=\pi^{2}_{\sharp\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}_{\mu}\quad\text{for ${\mathtt{M}}_{1}$-a.e.~$\mu$}.

(6.2)

If
\pi^{1}_{\sharp}
is essentially injective with respect to
{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}
then there is a Borel map

\mathscr{G}:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})
such that
for
{\mathtt{M}}_{1}
-a.e.
\mu

{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}_{\mu}
is concentrated on
a unique coupling
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\mathscr{G}(\mu)
with first marginal
\mu
, so
that
\mathit{\Pi}
is deterministic as in (
6.1
) and
we have

{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}_{\mu}=\delta_{\mathscr{G}(\mu)},\quad\mathscr{G}(\mu)\in\Gamma(\mu,\mathscr{F}(\mu)),\quad\mathscr{F}(\mu)=\pi^{2}_{\sharp}\mathscr{G}(\mu),\quad{\mathtt{M}}_{2}=(\pi^{2}_{\sharp}\circ\mathscr{G})_{\sharp}{\mathtt{M}}_{1}=\pi^{2}_{\sharp\sharp}\mathscr{G}_{\sharp}{\mathtt{M}}_{1}.

(6.3)

We can then
apply to
{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}_{\mu}

the disintegration map
{\mathcal{K}}
, obtaining the decomposition

\mathscr{G}(\mu)=\mu\otimes\kappa_{x,\mu}
,

\kappa_{x,\mu}={\mathcal{K}}(x,\mathscr{G}(\mu))
.
If
{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}
is concentrated on

{{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm det}_{2}}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})
then

{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}_{\mu}
is concentrated on
{{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm det}_{2}}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})
as well,
so that
\kappa_{x,\mu}=\delta_{{\boldsymbol{f}}(x,\mu)}
for some Borel map

{\boldsymbol{f}}:{\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to{\mathrm{H}}
.
We then obtain

\mathscr{G}(\mu)=({\boldsymbol{i}}\times{\boldsymbol{f}}(\cdot,\mu))_{\sharp}\mu,\quad\mathscr{F}(\mu)={\boldsymbol{f}}(\cdot,\mu)_{\sharp}\mu.

(6.4)

Since
\mathscr{F}(\mu)\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
and

{\mathtt{M}}_{2}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
we have

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathrm{H}}}|{\boldsymbol{f}}(x,\mu)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)

\displaystyle={\mathsf{m}}_{2}^{2}(\mathscr{F}(\mu))<\infty\quad\text{for ${\mathtt{M}}_{1}$-a.e.~$\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})$}

(6.5)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})}\Big(\int_{{\mathrm{H}}}|{\boldsymbol{f}}(x,\mu)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathtt{M}}_{1}(\mu)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})}{\mathsf{m}}_{2}^{2}(\mathscr{F}(\mu))\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathtt{M}}_{1}(\mu)={\mathsf{M}}_{2}^{2}({\mathtt{M}}_{2})<\infty.

It is then convenient to represent
{\boldsymbol{f}}
as a
{\mathrm{H}}
-valued
L^{2}
map
of the unfolded measure

\bar{\mathtt{M}}_{1}:=\int(\mu\otimes\delta_{\mu})\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathtt{M}}_{1}(\mu)\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})),

(6.6)

which satisfies
\pi^{2}_{\sharp}\bar{\mathtt{M}}_{1}={\mathtt{M}}_{1}
.
In fact if
{\boldsymbol{f}}\in L^{2}(\bar{\mathtt{M}}_{1};{\mathrm{H}})
then

\|{\boldsymbol{f}}\|_{L^{2}(\bar{\mathtt{M}}_{1};{\mathrm{H}})}^{2}=\int_{{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})}\int_{\mathrm{H}}|{\boldsymbol{f}}(x,\mu)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathtt{M}}_{1}(\mu)<\infty

(6.7)

and we can represent the
corresponding unfolded measure
\bar{\mathtt{M}}_{2}
as

\bar{\mathtt{M}}_{2}=({\boldsymbol{f}},\mathscr{F})_{\sharp}\bar{\mathtt{M}}_{1},\quad\bar{\mathtt{M}}_{i}=\int(\mu\otimes\delta_{\mu})\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathtt{M}}_{i}(\mu),

(6.8)

since

\displaystyle\int\xi(y,\nu)\,{\mathrm{d}}\bar{\mathtt{M}}_{2}(y,\nu)

\displaystyle=\int\Big(\int\xi(y,\nu)\,{\mathrm{d}}\nu(y)\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathtt{M}}_{2}(\nu)

\displaystyle=\int\Big(\int\xi(y,\mathscr{F}(\mu))\,{\mathrm{d}}\big(\mathscr{F}(\mu)\big)(y)\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathtt{M}}_{1}(\mu)

\displaystyle=\int\Big(\int\xi({\boldsymbol{f}}(x,\mu),\mathscr{F}(\mu))\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathtt{M}}_{1}(\mu)

\displaystyle=\int\xi({\boldsymbol{f}}(x,\mu),\mathscr{F}(\mu))\,{\mathrm{d}}\bar{\mathtt{M}}_{1}(x,\mu).

The above remarks justify the following definition.

Definition 6.1

(Fully deterministic random couplings)
.

We say that a random coupling law
{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})

is fully deterministic if

\pi^{1}_{\sharp}\text{ is ${\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}$-essentially injective and }{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}\text{ is concentrated on }{{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm det}_{2}}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}}).

(6.9)

We denote by
{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{\rm det}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})
the set of fully deterministic random couplings.

Lemma 6.2

(Representation of fully deterministic random couplings)
.

A random coupling law

{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})

with first random marginal
{\mathtt{M}}_{1}=\pi^{1}_{\sharp\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}

is fully deterministic
if and only if
there exists a Borel map
{\boldsymbol{f}}\in L^{2}(\bar{\mathtt{M}}_{1};{\mathrm{H}})
such that

{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}=\int\delta_{({\boldsymbol{i}}\times{\boldsymbol{f}}(\cdot,\mu))_{\sharp}\mu}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathtt{M}}_{1}(\mu)=\mathscr{G}_{\sharp}{\mathtt{M}}_{1},\quad\mathscr{G}(\mu):=({\boldsymbol{i}}\times{\boldsymbol{f}}(\cdot,\mu))_{\sharp}\mu.

(6.10)

In this case setting

\mathscr{F}(\mu):={\boldsymbol{f}}(\cdot,\mu)_{\sharp}\mu

we have

\begin{gathered}{\mathtt{M}}_{2}=\pi^{2}_{\sharp\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}=\mathscr{F}_{\sharp}{\mathtt{M}}_{1}\quad,\bar{\mathtt{M}}_{2}=({\boldsymbol{f}},\mathscr{F})_{\sharp}\bar{\mathtt{M}}_{1}\\
\int_{{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})}\Big(\int_{{\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}}}|x-y|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}({\boldsymbol{\gamma}})=\int_{{\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})}|{\boldsymbol{f}}(x,\mu)-x|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathtt{M}}_{1}(\mu).\end{gathered}

(6.11)

Using the unfolding
\bar{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}
of
{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}

we can also express (
6.11
) as

\bar{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}=({\boldsymbol{i}}\times{\boldsymbol{f}},\mathscr{G})_{\sharp}\bar{\mathtt{M}}_{1},\quad\int|x-y|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\bar{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}(x,y,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})=\int|{\boldsymbol{f}}(x,\mu)-x|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\bar{\mathtt{M}}_{1}(x,\mu).

(6.12)

Proof.

Because of the previous digression, we have just to show the converse direction: given a Borel map

{\boldsymbol{f}}\in L^{2}(\bar{\mathtt{M}}_{1};{\mathrm{H}})

the coupling
{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}
given by (
6.10
) is well defined,
i.e. the map
\mathscr{G}:{\mathcal{P}}({\mathrm{H}})\to{\mathcal{P}}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})

is Borel.
This property follows by standard argument, see e.g.

[
PS25
, Lemma D.2 and Corollary D.7]
; here is a self-contained discussion.

Let us first consider for a bounded Borel real function

z:{\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathcal{P}}({\mathrm{H}})\to\mathbb{R}

the functional
{\mathcal{Z}}:{\mathcal{P}}({\mathrm{H}})\to\mathbb{R}
,

{\mathcal{Z}}(\mu):=\int z(x,\mu)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\quad\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathrm{H}}),

(6.13)

and let us call
\mathscr{B}
the class of functions
z

for which
{\mathcal{Z}}
is Borel.

Clearly
\mathscr{B}
contains all the bounded and continuous functions
z\in{\mathrm{C}}_{b}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathcal{P}}({\mathrm{H}}))
(for which
{\mathcal{Z}}
is continuous).
It is also easy to check that
\mathscr{B}
is closed with respect to
uniform and monotone limits. By the functional monotone class
Theorem

[
Bog07
, Theorem 2.12.9]
we deduce that

\mathscr{B}
contains all the bounded Borel functions.

It follows that for every bounded continuous (or even Borel) map

\zeta:{\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}}\to\mathbb{R}
the map

\mu\mapsto\int_{{\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}}}\zeta\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathscr{G}(\mu)=\int\zeta(x,{\boldsymbol{f}}(x,\mu))\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu

(6.14)

is Borel, so the map
\mathscr{G}
is Borel as well, since
the functionals
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\mapsto\int\zeta,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
,

\zeta\in{\mathrm{C}}_{b}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})
generates
the weak (Polish) topology of
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})
.

Now, it is immediate to conclude that
{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}
is fully deterministic, since
\mathscr{G}
is injective and maps
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
to
{{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm det}_{2}}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})
.
∎

We end up with the following stronger Monge formulation of
OT problem between (laws of) random measures.

Problem 6.3

(Strict Monge formulation)
.

Given
{\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
find
a Borel map
{\boldsymbol{f}}\in L^{2}(\bar{\mathtt{M}}_{1};{\mathrm{H}})
such that
setting

\mathscr{F}(\mu):={\boldsymbol{f}}(\cdot,\mu)_{\sharp}\mu
we have

\mathscr{F}_{\sharp}{\mathtt{M}}_{1}={\mathtt{M}}_{2}\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathsf{W}}_{2}^{2}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})=\int_{{\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})}\big|{\boldsymbol{f}}(x,\mu)-x\big|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\bar{\mathtt{M}}_{1}(x,\mu).

(6.15)

Notice that the transformation

{\boldsymbol{f}}\times\mathscr{F}:(x,\mu)\to\big({\boldsymbol{f}}(x,\mu),{\boldsymbol{f}}(\cdot,\mu)_{\sharp}\mu\big)

maps
{\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
to itself and satisfies

({\boldsymbol{f}},\mathscr{F})_{\sharp}\bar{\mathtt{M}}_{1}=\bar{\mathtt{M}}_{2}

(6.16)

where, as usual,
\mathscr{F}(\mu)={\boldsymbol{f}}(\cdot,\mu)_{\sharp}\mu
and

\bar{\mathtt{M}}_{i}=\int\mu\otimes\delta_{\mu}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathtt{M}}_{i}(\mu)
.

It could seem that the strict Monge formulation is considerably more demanding than the usual Monge formulation expressed by (
6.1
). For example
in the extreme case when
{\mathtt{M}}_{i}=\delta_{\mu_{i}}
are Dirac masses
concentrated in two measures

\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

clearly there is just one
solution to the Monge formulation
(
6.1
) but
there could be many (or even no) solutions
to the strict Monge formulation, which
reduces to the usual
Optimal Transport problem
between
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
in

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}).

However, when we look for
conditions on
{\mathtt{M}}_{1}

which guarantee that
(
6.1
) is solvable

for every target measures
{\mathtt{M}}_{2}

then the two formulations are equivalent
and force uniqueness of solutions,
as the following result shows.

Theorem 6.4

(Monge vs strict Monge)
.

Given

{\mathtt{M}}_{1}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
,
the following two properties are equivalent:

1.

for every
{\mathtt{M}}_{2}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

there exists a
Borel map
\mathscr{F}=\mathscr{F}_{{\mathtt{M}}_{2}}:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
