(depending on
{\mathtt{M}}_{2}
) solving
the OT problem in Monge form
(
6.1
);

2.

for every
{\mathtt{M}}_{2}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

there exists a
Borel map
{\boldsymbol{f}}={\boldsymbol{f}}_{{\mathtt{M}}_{2}}\in L^{2}(\bar{\mathtt{M}}_{1};{\mathrm{H}})

(depending on
{\mathtt{M}}_{2}
)
solving
the OT problem in the strict Monge form
(
6.15
).

In both cases,
for every
{\mathtt{M}}_{2}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

the set of optimal couplings

\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})

contains the unique element

{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}={\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}_{{\mathtt{M}}_{2}}=(\mathrm{Id}\times\mathscr{F}_{{\mathtt{M}}_{2}})_{\sharp}{\mathtt{M}}_{1}

and, for
\mathit{\Pi}
-a.e.
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
, the set

\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})

contains the unique deterministic coupling

{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=({\boldsymbol{i}}\times{\boldsymbol{f}}_{{\mathtt{M}}_{2}}(\cdot,\mu_{1}))_{\sharp}\mu_{1}

so that
{\boldsymbol{f}}
corresponds to
the (unique) solution of the
strict Monge formulation
given in Problem
6.3
.
We also have
{\boldsymbol{f}}=\nabla_{W}\phi

for every optimal Kantorovich potential
\phi

(recall (
4.23
)).

Finally, every Lipschitz totally convex
function
\phi
is
W
-differentiable
at
{\mathtt{M}}_{1}
-a.e.
\mu
, according
to (
4.24
).

Proof.

Since 2.
\Rightarrow
1., it is sufficient to prove that 1. implies 2. and all the further
properties stated by the Theorem.

We thus fix
{\mathtt{M}}_{2}
,
an optimal Kantorovich potential
\phi

for the pair
{\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2}

with
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}=\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi

and we denote by
{\boldsymbol{f}}^{\circ}:{\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to{\mathrm{H}}
a Borel version of the minimal section
of
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}
(as in Proposition

4.2
)
and we
set

\mathscr{F}^{\circ}(\mu):={\boldsymbol{f}}^{\circ}(\cdot,\mu)_{\sharp}\mu
.

We then select
an optimal random coupling law

{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}\in\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})

with

{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}=(\pi^{1}_{\sharp},\pi^{2}_{\sharp})_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}\in\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})
;
since
{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}
is optimal,

\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}\subset\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}
.

By the minimality of
{\boldsymbol{f}}^{\circ}

we have

{\boldsymbol{f}}^{\circ}\in L^{2}(\bar{\mathtt{M}}_{1};{\mathrm{H}})
since

\displaystyle{\mathsf{W}}_{2}^{2}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})

\displaystyle=\int\int|x_{2}-x_{1}|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(x_{1},x_{2})\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}({\boldsymbol{\gamma}})\geq\int\int|{\boldsymbol{f}}^{\circ}(x_{1},\pi^{1}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})-x_{1}|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(x_{1},x_{2})\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}({\boldsymbol{\gamma}})

\displaystyle=\int\int|{\boldsymbol{f}}^{\circ}(x_{1},\mu)-x_{1}|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\bar{\mathtt{M}}_{1}(x,\mu)

We then introduce

{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}^{\prime}:=\frac{1}{2}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}+\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{G}^{\circ}_{\sharp}{\mathtt{M}}_{1},\quad\mathscr{G}^{\circ}(\mu):=({\boldsymbol{i}}\times{\boldsymbol{f}}^{\circ}(\cdot,\mu))_{\sharp}\mu,\quad{\mathtt{M}}_{2}^{\prime}:=\pi^{2}_{\sharp\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}^{\prime}.

Since
\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}^{\prime}\subset\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}

we have

{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2}^{\prime})

and
its disintegration with respect to
\pi^{1}_{\sharp}

can be expressed via the corresponding
disintegration

{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}_{\mu}
of
{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}
by

{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}_{\mu}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}_{\mu}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\mathscr{G}^{\circ}(\mu)}.

We then select the midpoint

\tilde{\mathtt{M}}:=(\pi^{1\shortrightarrow 2}_{t})_{\sharp\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}^{\prime}
between

{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{M}}}_{1}
and
{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{M}}}_{2}^{\prime}

induced by
{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}^{\prime}
corresponding to
t=1/2.

By Theorem

5.7

we know that

\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}:=(\pi^{1},\pi^{1\shortrightarrow 2}_{t})_{\sharp\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}^{\prime}
is the unique element
of
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},\tilde{\mathtt{M}})
,
and

\tilde{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}=(\pi^{1}_{\sharp},\pi^{2}_{\sharp})\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}

is the unique element
of
\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},\tilde{\mathtt{M}})

so that
by assumption

\tilde{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}=(\mathrm{Id}\times\tilde{\mathscr{F}})_{\sharp}{\mathtt{M}}_{1}

for
\tilde{\mathscr{F}}=\mathscr{F}_{\tilde{\mathtt{M}}}.

On the other hand
setting

{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}_{\mu,t}=(\pi^{1},\pi^{1\shortrightarrow 2}_{t})_{\sharp\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}_{\mu},\ {\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}_{\mu,t}=\pi^{2}_{\sharp\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}_{\mu,t},\ \mathscr{G}^{\circ}_{t}(\mu)=(\pi^{1},\pi^{1\shortrightarrow 2}_{t})_{\sharp}\mathscr{G}^{\circ}(\mu),\ \mathscr{F}_{t}^{\circ}(\mu)=(\pi^{1\shortrightarrow 2}_{t})_{\sharp}\mathscr{G}^{\circ}(\mu)=\pi^{2}_{\sharp}\mathscr{G}^{\circ}_{t}(\mu)

we have

\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}}_{\mu}=\frac{1}{2}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}_{\mu,t}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\mathscr{G}^{\circ}_{t}(\mu)},\quad\tilde{{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}}_{\mu}=\frac{1}{2}{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}_{\mu,t}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\mathscr{F}^{\circ}_{t}(\mu)}\quad\text{${\mathtt{M}}_{1}$-a.e.}

Since
\tilde{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}_{\mu}=\delta_{\tilde{\mathscr{F}}(\mu)}
,
we deduce that
{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}_{\mu,t}=\mathscr{F}^{\circ}_{t}(\mu)

for
{\mathtt{M}}_{1}
-a.e.
\mu
,
so that
\tilde{\mathtt{M}}
is the middle point
also between
{\mathtt{M}}_{1}
and
{\mathtt{M}}_{2}

and between
{\mathtt{M}}_{1}
and

{\mathtt{M}}_{2}^{\circ}=(\mathscr{F}^{\circ})_{\sharp}{\mathtt{M}}_{1}

(with respect to the same
optimal set
\boldsymbol{{\mathrm{F}}}
).
By the non-branching property
of Theorem
5.7

we deduce that
{\mathtt{M}}_{2}={\mathtt{M}}_{2}^{\circ}

and by the strict minimality of the section
{\boldsymbol{f}}^{\circ}

{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}=\mathscr{G}^{\circ}_{\sharp}{\mathtt{M}}_{1}

is a strict Monge solution
and is the unique element of
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})
;
similarly
{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}=(\mathrm{Id}\times\mathscr{F}^{\circ})_{\sharp}{\mathtt{M}}_{1}

is deterministic and is
the unique element of
\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2}).

Let us eventually check the last statement.
We take a Lipschitz totally convex function

\phi
: if there exists a Borel
set
B\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

where
\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi
is not a singleton
with
{\mathtt{M}}_{1}(B)>0
,
by a standard measurable selection
we can construct
an optimal random coupling law

{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}
with first random marginal
{\mathtt{M}}_{1}

and second random marginal
{\mathtt{M}}_{2}=\pi^{2}_{\sharp\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

(thanks to the fact that
\phi
is Lipschitz)
which is not concentrated on
the map
\nabla_{W}\phi
, contradicting the
above result.
∎

By the above result if we want to
solve the Monge problem
for arbitrary target
{\mathtt{M}}_{2}
it seems natural
to start from measures

{\mathtt{M}}_{1}
concentrated on

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathrm{H}})
.
The next simpler Proposition shows that
in this case there is also
a one-to-one correspondence between
optimal couplings of (laws of) random measures and
(laws of) random optimal couplings and
for every fixed target
{\mathtt{M}}_{2}

the Monge and the strict Monge formulations of the
L^{2}
-Optimal Transport problem are equivalent
as well.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
{\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
and let us assume that

{\mathtt{M}}_{1}
is concentrated on
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathrm{H}})
, i.e.
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathrm{H}})
for

{\mathtt{M}}_{1}
-a.e.
\mu
.
Then the restriction of the map
(\pi^{1}_{\sharp},\pi^{2}_{\sharp})_{\sharp}

on
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})
is injective and every

{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}\in\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})
is concentrated on

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{\rm det}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})
.
Moreover, if

{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}\in\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})
and

\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}=(\pi^{1}_{\sharp},\pi^{2}_{\sharp})_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}

is deterministic then
{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}
is fully deterministic.

Proof.

Since
{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}\in\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})
and

{\mathtt{M}}_{1}
is concentrated on
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathrm{H}})
, thanks to Theorem
2.5

we can find a Borel set
B\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})

of full
{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}
-measure
such that

B\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{2,o}^{\operatorname{det}}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})

and
\pi^{1}_{\sharp}(B)\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathrm{H}})

(when
{\mathrm{H}}
has finite dimension
we just take
B=(\pi^{1}_{\sharp})^{-1}\big({\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathrm{H}})\big)\cap{\mathcal{P}}_{2,o}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})
,
see Proposition
2.6
).

The restriction of
(\pi^{1}_{\sharp},\pi^{2}_{\sharp})
to
B

is injective, since,
given
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\in(\pi^{1}_{\sharp},\pi^{2}_{\sharp})(B)
, the set

\Gamma_{o}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
contains a unique element and it is deterministic.
It follows that
(\pi^{1}_{\sharp},\pi^{2}_{\sharp})_{\sharp}
is injective as
well
on
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}}_{1},{\mathtt{M}}_{2})
.
If moreover
\mathit{\Pi}
is deterministic and induced by the Borel map

\mathscr{F}
, we see that
for
{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}
-a.e.
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
,

{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in\Gamma_{o}(\pi^{1}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}},\mathscr{F}(\pi^{1}_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}))

so that
\pi^{1}_{\sharp}
is
{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}
-essentially injective.
∎

6.2
Regular and super-regular measures in
{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
and solution
to the Monge problem

We first observe that the definitions of
\sigma
-d.c. hypersurfaces and Gaussian null sets given in
Section
2.2
also apply to the infinite dimensional Hilbert
space
{\mathcal{H}}
;
we keep the notation
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathcal{H}}),{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{{gr}}({\mathcal{H}})

to denote the corresponding class of regular measures,
thus vanishing on all d.c. hypersurfaces and Gaussian-null Borel subsets of
{\mathcal{H}}
respectively.

Definition 6.6

(Random exceptional and Gaussian null sets, regular and super-regular measures)
.

-

We say that a Borel set
B\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

is a random exceptional (resp. G-null) set if

\iota^{-1}(B)
is
contained in a

\sigma
-d.c. hypersurface
of
{\mathcal{H}}
(resp. Gaussian-null in
{\mathcal{H}}
).

-

We denote by
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}))

(resp.
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{gr}({\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}))
) the set of regular (resp. G-regular) measures

{\mathtt{M}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

such that

{\mathtt{M}}(B)=0
for every random
exceptional (resp. G-null) Borel set

B\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
.

-

The set of super-regular measures

{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r\kern-1.0ptr}({\mathrm{H}})={\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathrm{H}}))

(resp. super-G-regular measures

{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{gr\kern-1.0ptr}({\mathrm{H}})={\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{gr}({\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathrm{H}}))
)
is the set of regular
(resp. of G-regular) measures
concentrated on
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathrm{H}})
.

It is clear that

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{gr}({\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}))\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}),\qquad{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{gr\kern-1.0ptr}({\mathrm{H}})\subset{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r\kern-1.0ptr}({\mathrm{H}}).

(6.17)

Let us make a few comments
on the previous definitions.

Remark 6.7

(LGGRM measures)
.

We can say that
a measure
{\mathtt{G}}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

is a Law of Gaussian Generated Random Measures (LGGRM) if

{\mathtt{G}}=\iota_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{g}}

for some nondegenerate Gaussian measure
{\mathfrak{g}}
in
{\mathcal{H}}
.
An equivalent way to say that
a Borel set in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

is a random G-null set is

{\mathtt{G}}(B)=0\quad\text{for every LGGRM ${\mathtt{G}}.$}

(6.18)

Remark 6.8

(Random exceptional and G-null sets
are independent of the choice of
({\mathrm{Q}},{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathrm{Q}},\mathbb{M})
)
.

It is not difficult to see that the above definitions of random exceptional and
G-null sets
(and the corresponding classes of regular and super-regular measures) are independent of the choice of
the nonatomic standard Borel space

({\mathrm{Q}},{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathrm{Q}},\mathbb{M})
.
In fact, if
({\mathrm{Q}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathrm{Q}}^{\prime},\mathbb{M}^{\prime})
is another standard Borel measure space
endowed with a nonatomic measure
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
, we can
find a measure preserving isomorphism

\mathtt{h}:{\mathrm{Q}}^{\prime}\to{\mathrm{Q}}
such that

\mathtt{h}_{\sharp}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}=\mathbb{M}
and
\mathtt{h}^{-1}_{\sharp}\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
.

\mathtt{h}
induces a linear isometry of

{\mathcal{H}}
onto
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}=L^{2}({\mathrm{Q}}^{\prime},\mathbb{M}^{\prime};{\mathrm{H}})

defined by
\mathtt{h}^{*}X:=X\circ\mathtt{h}
, with

\iota^{\prime}\circ\mathtt{h}^{*}=\iota
, since

X^{\prime}=X\circ\mathtt{h},\quad\iota^{\prime}(X^{\prime})=(X\circ\mathtt{h})_{\sharp}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}=X_{\sharp}\mathtt{h}_{\sharp}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}=X_{\sharp}\mathbb{M}=\iota(X).

(6.19)

Since the d.c. hypersurfaces are
preserved by isometric isomorphisms between Hilbert spaces, using
(
6.19
) it is immediate to check that a random exceptional set w.r.t.
{\mathrm{Q}}^{\prime}

is also exceptional w.r.t.
{\mathrm{Q}}
.

In a similar way, if
B^{\prime}
is a random Gaussian-null set
with respect to
{\mathrm{Q}}^{\prime},\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
,
i.e.
(\iota^{\prime})^{-1}(B^{\prime})
is Gaussian-null in
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}

and let
{\mathfrak{g}}
be an arbitrary nondegenerate Gaussian measure in

{\mathcal{H}}
.
We introduce
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}=(\mathtt{h}^{*})_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{g}}
and we observe that

{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}
is a nondegenerate Gaussian measure in
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}

(since
\mathtt{h}^{*}
is a linear surjective isometry).
By definition

{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}((\iota^{\prime})^{-1}B^{\prime})=0

and therefore

{\mathfrak{g}}(\iota^{-1}B)={\mathfrak{g}}\Big(\big(\iota^{\prime}\circ\mathtt{h}^{*}\big)^{-1}B\Big)={\mathfrak{g}}\Big((\mathtt{h}^{*})^{-1}(\iota^{\prime})^{-1}B\Big)={\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}\Big((\iota^{\prime})^{-1}B\Big)=0,

so that
\iota^{-1}B
is Gaussian null in
{\mathcal{H}}
.

Remark 6.9

(Stability in the class of mutually absolutely continuous measures)
.

The super-regularity condition is stable
with respect to multiplication by an integrable factor:

{\mathtt{M}}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r\kern-1.0ptr}({\mathrm{H}}),\quad{\mathtt{M}}^{\prime}\ll{\mathtt{M}}\quad\Rightarrow\quad{\mathtt{M}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r\kern-1.0ptr}({\mathrm{H}}).

(6.20)

Remark 6.10
.

If
{\mathtt{M}}
is concentrated
on
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathrm{H}})

then it is super-regular
if it vanishes on
all exceptional subsets of
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathrm{H}})
,
i.e. it is sufficient to check that

B\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathrm{H}}),\quad\iota^{-1}(B)\text{ exceptional in ${\mathcal{H}}$}\quad\Rightarrow\quad{\mathtt{M}}(B)=0.

(6.21)

Similarly, if

B\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{gr}({\mathrm{H}}),\quad\iota^{-1}(B)\text{ Gaussian null in ${\mathcal{H}}$}\quad\Rightarrow\quad{\mathtt{M}}(B)=0

(6.22)

then
{\mathtt{M}}
is super-G-regular.

There is a simple way to generate
super-regular measures.

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
{\mathfrak{m}}
be a regular measure
in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathcal{H}})

(respectively
G
-regular in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{gr}({\mathcal{H}})
)
such that

\iota(X)=X_{\sharp}\mathbb{M}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathrm{H}})\quad\text{for ${\mathfrak{m}}$-a.e.~}X\in{\mathcal{H}}.

(6.23)

Then
{\mathtt{M}}:=\iota_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{m}}
is a super-regular measure
in
{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r\kern-1.0ptr}({\mathrm{H}})

(resp. super-G-regular in
{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{gr\kern-1.0ptr}({\mathrm{H}})
).

Proof.

It is immediate to see that

{\mathtt{M}}:=\iota_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{m}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}))
is regular:
in fact, if
B
is a random
exceptional set

{\mathtt{M}}(B)={\mathfrak{m}}(\iota^{-1}B)=0

since
\iota^{-1}(B)
is a
\sigma
-d.c. hypersurface and
{\mathfrak{m}}
is regular.
Condition (
6.23
) also shows that
M

is concentrated on
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathrm{H}})
.
∎

It is also possible to change the reference measure
\mathbb{M}:
we show two simple cases.

Lemma 6.12
.

Let
\mathbb{M}^{\prime},\mathbb{M}^{\prime\prime}
be atomless
Borel probability measures on the
standard Borel space
({\mathrm{Q}},{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathrm{Q}})

with
\mathbb{M}^{\prime\prime}\leq a\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
for some
a>0

(so that
the corresponding
Hilbert spaces
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime\prime}

satisfy
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime\prime}

with continuous inclusion).
Denote by
\iota^{\prime}:X\to X_{\sharp}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
,

\iota^{\prime\prime}:X\to X_{\sharp}\mathbb{M}^{\prime\prime}
the corresponding
law maps.

If
{\mathfrak{m}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathcal{H}}^{\prime})

and
{\mathtt{M}}^{\prime}=\iota^{\prime}_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{m}}

is super-regular
then also
{\mathtt{M}}^{\prime\prime}=\iota^{\prime\prime}_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{m}}

is super-regular.

A similar result holds if

{\mathtt{M}}^{\prime\prime}\ll{\mathtt{M}}^{\prime}
,
and
{\mathfrak{m}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathcal{B}})
, for some separable Banach space
{\mathcal{B}}\subset{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}\cap{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime\prime}.

Proof.

Let
N\subset{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}

be a
{\mathfrak{m}}
-negligible Borel set
such that
\iota^{\prime}(X)\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathrm{H}})

for every
X\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}\setminus N.

Since
\iota^{\prime\prime}(X)\leq a\iota^{\prime}(X)

for every
X\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}

we deduce that

\iota^{\prime\prime}(X)\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathrm{H}})

for every
X\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}\setminus N

as well, so that
{\mathtt{M}}^{\prime\prime}

is concentrated in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathrm{H}}).

If
B
is a Borel exceptional set
of
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

then

(\iota^{\prime\prime})^{-1}(B)

is contained in a

\sigma
-d.c. hypersurface
S
of

{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime\prime}
; since
S\cap{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}

is a
\sigma
-d.c. hypersurface as well, we
deduce that

{\mathtt{M}}^{\prime\prime}(B)\leq{\mathfrak{m}}(S)={\mathfrak{m}}(S\cap{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime})=0

since
{\mathfrak{m}}
is regular. We conclude that

{\mathtt{M}}^{\prime\prime}
is super-regular.

A similar argument applies to the second statement.
∎

The relation between super-regular measures
and differentiability of
Lipschitz totally displacement convex functions
is clarified by the next two results.

Theorem 6.13
.

If
\phi:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to\mathbb{R}

is a totally displacement convex and Lipschitz function
then the singular set

\operatorname{Sing}^{r}(\phi):=\{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}^{r}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}):\#\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi[\mu]>1\}

of
regular
measures
where the total subdifferential of

\phi
is not reduced to
a singleton (the minimal section)
is exceptional.

Proof.

Since every measure

\mu\in\operatorname{Sing}^{r}(\phi)
is regular and

\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi[\mu]\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{2,o}({\mathrm{H}}\times{\mathrm{H}})
by Corollary
4.10
, all the elements of

\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi[\mu]
are
deterministic couplings.
If
\boldsymbol{\partial}\phi[\mu]

contains
at least two different elements,
they are
associated with
two different fields
{\boldsymbol{f}}_{1}(\cdot,\mu),{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2}(\cdot,\mu)

in
L^{2}({\mathrm{H}},\mu;{\mathrm{H}})
.

If
X\in\iota^{-1}(\mu)
, setting

Y_{i}={\boldsymbol{f}}_{i}(X,\mu)
we deduce that

(X,Y_{i})_{\sharp}\mathbb{M}\in\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi[\mu]

and therefore

Y_{i}\in\partial\hat{\phi}(X)
, where
\hat{\phi}=\phi\circ\iota
.
We conclude that
\partial\hat{\phi}(X)

contains two different elements and therefore

\hat{\phi}
is not Gateaux-differentiable at
X
. This arguments shows that

\iota^{-1}(\operatorname{Sing}^{r}(\phi))
is
a subset where
\hat{\phi}
is not Gateaux-differentiable
and therefore is a
\sigma
-d.c. hypersurface in
{\mathcal{H}}
since

\hat{\phi}
is a convex Lipschitz function.
∎

Combining Proposition
4.7
, Remark
6.10
and the above Theorem we immediately get:

Corollary 6.14
.

If
{\mathtt{M}}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r\kern-1.0ptr}({\mathrm{H}})

and
\phi:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\to\mathbb{R}

is a Lipschitz
totally displacement convex function,
then
for
{\mathtt{M}}
-a.e.
\mu
we have:

1.

\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}\phi[\mu]=\boldsymbol{\partial}_{{\mathrm{t}}}^{\circ}\phi[\mu]

is reduced to a single deterministic coupling in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2,o}^{\rm det}({\mathrm{H}}^{2})

of the form

({\boldsymbol{i}}\times\nabla_{W}\phi(\cdot,\mu))_{\sharp}\mu
;

2.

\partial^{-}\phi(\mu)=\nabla_{W}\phi(\cdot,\mu)_{\sharp}\mu
.

Theorem 6.15

(Solutions to the strict Monge problem for super-regular measures)
.

If
{\mathtt{M}}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r\kern-1.0ptr}({\mathrm{H}})
and

{\mathtt{N}}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
, then

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}},{\mathtt{N}})
and
\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}},{\mathtt{N}})

contain a unique element

{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}
and
{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}=(\pi^{1}_{\sharp},\pi^{2}_{\sharp})_{\sharp}{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}
respectively.

{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{P}}}
is fully deterministic and
there exists a unique
Borel map
{\boldsymbol{f}}\in L^{2}(\bar{\mathtt{M}};{\mathrm{H}})

solving the Monge OT problem
6.3
.

{\boldsymbol{f}}
is essentially totally cyclically monotone
and coincides with
the minimal section

\nabla_{W}\phi

of an optimal Kantorovich potential.

Proof.

By Proposition
6.5

and Theorem
6.4

it is sufficient to prove that

\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}},{\mathtt{N}})
contains a
deterministic coupling
\mathit{\Pi}
.

Let us first suppose that

{\mathtt{N}}
is concentrated on the closed ball

{\mathcal{B}}(R):=\Big\{\nu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}):{\mathsf{m}}_{2}(\nu)\leq R\Big\}.

(6.24)

In this case, by Corollary
5.6
there is an optimal Kantorovich potential

\phi
which is
a
R
-Lipschitz totally convex
function
\phi
with
S:=\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits({\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}})\subset\partial^{-}\phi

and Corollary
6.14

shows that there exists
a (unique) strictly Monge solution
which is given by

{\boldsymbol{f}}(\cdot,\mu)=\nabla_{W}\phi(x,\mu).

(6.25)

In the general case,
we argue as in the proof
of Theorem 6.2.10
[
AGS08
]
.
For every
R>0
we set

{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}(R):={\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\times{\mathcal{B}}(R)

and for an optimal coupling

{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}\in\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}},{\mathtt{N}})
,
we set for sufficiently large
n

Z_{n}:={\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}\big({\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}(n)\big),\quad{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}_{n}:=Z_{n}^{-1}{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}(n),\quad{\mathtt{M}}_{n}:=\pi^{1}_{\sharp}{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}_{n},\ {\mathtt{N}}_{n}:=\pi^{2}_{\sharp}{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}_{n}.

(6.26)

Since optimality is preserved by
restriction,

{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}_{n}\in\Gamma_{o}({\mathtt{M}}_{n},{\mathtt{N}}_{n})
;
since
{\mathtt{M}}_{n}\ll{\mathtt{M}}
and

{\mathtt{N}}_{n}
is concentrated on
{\mathcal{B}}(n)

the previous argument shows that
there exists a unique map

\mathscr{F}_{n}
such that

{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}_{n}=(\mathrm{Id}\times\mathscr{F}_{n})_{\sharp}{\mathtt{M}}_{n}.

Moreover, for
m>n
we easily get

\mathscr{F}_{m}=\mathscr{F}_{n}

{\mathtt{M}}_{n}
-a.e., so that
there exists a map
\mathscr{F}

such that
\mathscr{F}=\mathscr{F}_{n}

{\mathtt{M}}_{n}
-a.e. for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Passing to the limit in the identity

{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}_{n}=(\mathrm{Id}\times\mathscr{F})_{\sharp}{\mathtt{M}}_{n}

we obtain
{\text{$\mathit{\Pi}$}}=(\mathrm{Id}\times\mathscr{F})_{\sharp}{\mathtt{M}}.

It follows that
\mathit{\Pi}
is deterministic.
∎

Anticipating
some of the results of the next section, it is easy to see that

{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r\kern-1.0ptr}({\mathrm{H}})
is dense in
{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
.

Proposition 6.16
.

If
{\mathrm{H}}
has finite dimension then
{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{gr\kern-1.0ptr}({\mathrm{H}})
is dense in
{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
.
In particular, the class of initial measures for which the OT
problem
has a unique solution in (strict) Monge form is dense.

Proof.

Thanks to
(
6.20
),
if
{\mathtt{M}}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{gr\kern-1.0ptr}({\mathrm{H}})
and
{\mathtt{M}}^{\prime}\ll{\mathtt{M}}
then also

{\mathtt{M}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{gr\kern-1.0ptr}({\mathrm{H}})
.
If
{\mathrm{H}}
has finite dimension, we
will see in the next section
(see Theorems
6.19

and

6.25
)
that there exists a
reference measure
{\mathtt{G}}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{gr\kern-1.0ptr}({\mathrm{H}})
with full support.
It is then sufficient to observe that
the set

\Big\{{\mathtt{M}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}):{\mathtt{M}}^{\prime}\ll{\mathtt{G}}\Big\}\quad\text{is dense in }{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}}),

since its closure contains all the
finite combination of Dirac masses in
{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
.
In fact, if

{\mathtt{M}}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}a_{k}\delta_{\mu_{k}}

for distinct points
\mu_{1},\cdots,\mu_{n}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
,
we can choose
r>0
so small that
the balls
B_{r,k}=B_{r}(\mu_{k})
are disjoint.
Since
{\mathtt{G}}
has full support,

Z_{r,k}:=G(B_{r,k})>0
so that

{\mathtt{M}}_{r}:=\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{a_{k}}{Z_{r,k}}{\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{B_{r,k}}\Big)G\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r\kern-1.0ptr}({\mathrm{H}})

and
{\mathtt{M}}_{r}\to{\mathtt{M}}
in
{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})
as
r\downarrow 0.

∎

6.3
Examples of super-regular measures
for finite dimensional
{\mathrm{H}}

In this last section we will
exhibit many examples of super-regular measures.
We will focus on the relevant case of
measures induced by nondegenerate Gaussian measures
{\mathfrak{g}}
on
{\mathcal{H}}

as in Example
2.10
,
when
{\mathrm{H}}
has finite dimension: by using
Lemma
6.11
it will be sufficient
to check that (
6.23
) holds, i.e.
\iota_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{g}}({\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})\setminus{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathrm{H}}))=0.

We start from the
1
-dimensional case, where
we will prove a very general result.

The
1
-dimensional case
{\mathrm{H}}=\mathbb{R}

Example 6.17

(The random occupation measure
associated with Brownian motion in
[0,1{]}
)
.

Let
{\mathrm{Q}}=[0,1]
endowed with the usual Lebesgue measure
\mathbb{M}

and let
{\mathfrak{w}}
be the standard Wiener measure concentrated on

{\mathrm{C}}([0,1])
. We claim that

W:=\iota_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{w}}
is super-G-regular with full support.

We can apply Lemma
6.11
.
Since
{\mathfrak{w}}
is a Gaussian non-degenerate measure in
{\mathcal{H}}=L^{2}([0,1],\mathbb{M})
, it is clearly a regular measure
in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathcal{H}})
, so it is sufficient to check that

X_{\sharp}\mathbb{M}\text{ is nonatomic for ${\mathfrak{w}}$-a.e.~path $X\in{\mathcal{H}}$},

(6.27)

i.e.

\mathbb{M}\Big(\Big\{t\in[0,1]:X_{t}=y\Big\}\Big)=0\quad\text{for ${\mathfrak{w}}$-a.e.~$X\in{\mathrm{C}}([0,1])$}.

(6.28)

In fact we have the much stronger result that
the so-called occupation measure
\iota(X)=X_{\sharp}\mathbb{M}

is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure
in
\mathbb{R}
, see e.g.
[
MP10
, Theorem 3.26]
.

The previous example
is in fact a particular case of a general result: we will show that

any
measure
G=\iota_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{g}}

obtained as the push forward of
an arbitrary nondegenerate Gaussian
measure
{\mathfrak{g}}
in
{\mathcal{H}}

is super-G-regular.

Before discussing this result,
let us show a simple criterion
ensuring that a measure

{\mathtt{M}}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
is concentrated on
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}(\mathbb{R}).
We set

{\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{0}(r):=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }r=0,\\
0&\text{if }r\neq 0.\end{cases}

(6.29)

Lemma 6.18
.

Let
{\mathtt{M}}=\iota_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{m}}

for
{\mathfrak{m}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{H}})

and let us suppose
that
{\mathfrak{m}}

admits the representation
(
2.29
),
(
2.30
)
discussed in Section

2.4
.
If

\int_{\Omega}{\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{0}(\Xi(\omega,q_{1})-\Xi(\omega,q_{2}))\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega)=0\quad\text{for $\mathbb{M}\otimes\mathbb{M}$-a.e.~$(q_{1},q_{2})$
}

(6.30)

then
{\mathtt{M}}
is concentrated on
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}(\mathbb{R}).

Proof.

Let

D:=\{(x,x):x\in\mathbb{R}\}
be the diagonal
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, whose characteristic
function is given by

(x,y)\mapsto{\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{0}(x-y)
.
We recall that
a measure
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})

is atomless (and thus belongs to

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}(\mathbb{R})
) if and only if

\mu\otimes\mu(D)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}{\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{0}(x-y)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(y)=0.

(6.31)

Recalling the definition
(
2.34
) of
the
k
-projection of
{\mathtt{M}}
,
we deduce that
{\mathtt{M}}
is concentrated
on
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}(\mathbb{R})

if and only if

\operatorname{pr}^{2}[{\mathtt{M}}](D)=\int{\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{0}(x-y)\,{\mathrm{d}}\operatorname{pr}^{2}[{\mathtt{M}}](x,y)=0.

Applying formula
(
2.36
)
we thus
express the above integral as

\int_{\Omega}\Big(\int{\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{0}(\Xi(\omega,q_{1})-\Xi(\omega,q_{2}))\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2}(q_{1},q_{2})\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega)=0.

(6.32)

An application of Fubini’s Theorem
yields (
6.30
).
∎

As an application of the
above Lemma we have the following general result.

Theorem 6.19

(Push forward of
nondegenerate Gaussian measures are superregular)
.

If
{\mathrm{H}}=\mathbb{R}
and

{\mathfrak{g}}
is a nondegenerate
Gaussian measure in
{\mathcal{H}}
,
then
{\mathtt{G}}:=\iota_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{g}}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{gr\kern-1.0ptr}({\mathrm{H}})

is super-G-regular.

Proof.

Since
{\mathrm{Q}}
is a standard Borel space, we can find
a bounded metric
{\mathsf{d}}_{\mathrm{Q}}

in
{\mathrm{Q}}
such that

({\mathrm{Q}},{\mathsf{d}}_{\mathrm{Q}})
is
a complete and separable
metric space and

{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathrm{Q}}
coincides with the Borel
\sigma
-algebra induced by
{\mathsf{d}}_{\mathrm{Q}}.

We can also define

{\mathsf{d}}_{{\mathrm{Q}}^{2}}((q_{1},q_{2}),(q_{1}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime})):=\max[{\mathsf{d}}_{\mathrm{Q}}(q_{1},q_{1}^{\prime}),{\mathsf{d}}_{\mathrm{Q}}(q_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})]

and the swap isometric map

S:{\mathrm{Q}}^{2}\to{\mathrm{Q}}^{2}
,

S(q_{1},q_{2}):=(q_{2},q_{1}).

We adopt the notation of Example
(
2.10
). Since
{\mathfrak{g}}
is
nondegenerate, we can also assume that

\lambda_{n}>0
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}.

By Lemma

6.11
, it is sufficient to prove that
{\mathfrak{g}}
satisfies condition
(
6.23
).
Using the
representation
(
2.38
)
and (
2.39
), we can then apply Lemma
(
6.18
):
our thesis follows if we
prove (
6.30
).

Thanks to
(
2.39
),
we have

D(\omega;q_{1},q_{2}):=\Xi(\omega,q_{1})-\Xi(\omega,q_{2})=\sum_{n}\xi_{n}(\omega)\Big({\mathsf{E}}_{n}(q_{1})-{\mathsf{E}}_{n}(q_{2})\Big)

(6.33)

which is a series of independent random Gaussian variables.
We know that
for
\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2}
-a.e.
(q_{1},q_{2})

•

the series defining
D

converges in
L^{2}(\Omega,\mathbb{P})

and also
\mathbb{P}
-a.e.,

•

its law

\nu_{q_{1},q_{2}}:=D(\cdot;q_{1},q_{2})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}

is a Gaussian measure

•

\nu_{q_{1},q_{2}}=N(0,\lambda^{2}(q_{1},q_{2}))

where

\lambda^{2}(q_{1},q_{2})=\sum_{n}\lambda_{n}^{2}\Big({\mathsf{E}}_{n}(q_{1})-{\mathsf{E}}_{n}(q_{2})\Big)^{2}.

(6.34)

We can observe that
the integral in (
6.30
) is just

\nu_{q_{1},q_{2}}(\{0\})
,
so that
(
6.30
) holds
if
\nu_{q_{1},q_{2}}

is non-degenerate, i.e.

\lambda^{2}(q_{1},q_{2})>0\quad\text{
for $\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2}$-a.e.~$(q_{1},q_{2})$.
}

(6.35)

Let us denote by

A\subset{\mathrm{Q}}^{2}

the set
where
\lambda
vanishes
and let
D_{\mathrm{Q}}:=\{(q,q):q\in{\mathrm{Q}}\}
be the diagonal in

{\mathrm{Q}}^{2}.

Since
\mathbb{M}
is diffuse,

\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2}(D_{\mathrm{Q}})=0

so that
we have to prove that

\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2}(A^{\prime})=0

where
A^{\prime}:=A\setminus D_{\mathrm{Q}}
.
Since
\lambda_{n}>0
for every
n,

we immediately see that

(q_{1},q_{2})\in A\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad{\mathsf{E}}_{n}(q_{1})={\mathsf{E}}_{n}(q_{2})\quad\text{for every }n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}.

(6.36)

We argue by contradiction
and we suppose
that
\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2}(A^{\prime})>0
.
We can thus find
(\bar{q}_{1},\bar{q}_{2})\in A^{\prime}\cap\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits(\mathbb{M})

and a sufficiently small ball

B=B_{r}(\bar{q}_{1},\bar{q}_{2})

such that

S(B)\cap B=\emptyset
and
\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2}(A^{\prime}\cap B)>0
.
Since
A^{\prime}
is symmetric
and
S_{\sharp}\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2}=\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2}
,
we have

\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2}(A^{\prime}\cap B)=\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2}(A^{\prime}\cap S(B))>0
.

We set
B^{\prime}:=A^{\prime}\cap B

and we eventually consider the bounded Borel function

f(q_{1},q_{2}):={\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{B^{\prime}}(q_{1},q_{2})-{\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{S(B^{\prime})}(q_{1},q_{2})={\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{B^{\prime}}(q_{1},q_{2})-{\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{B^{\prime}}(q_{2},q_{1}).

We can
expand
f

as a orthogonal series
in
L^{2}({\mathrm{Q}}^{2},\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2})

with respect to the
complete
orthonormal system

{\mathsf{E}}_{m,n}(q_{1},q_{2}):={\mathsf{E}}_{m}(q_{1}){\mathsf{E}}_{n}(q_{2})

obtaining

f=\sum_{m,n}\hat{f}_{m,n}{\mathsf{E}}_{m,n}\quad\text{converging in }L^{2}({\mathrm{Q}}^{2},\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2})

(6.37)

where

\hat{f}_{m,n}:=\int_{{\mathrm{Q}}^{2}}f(q_{1},q_{2}){\mathsf{E}}_{m}(q_{1}){\mathsf{E}}_{n}(q_{2})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2}(q_{1},q_{2}).

(6.38)

Since
f\equiv 0
if
(q_{1},q_{2})\not\in A
,
the integral in (
6.38
)
can in fact be restricted to
A
.
Since (
6.36
) implies in particular

{\mathsf{E}}_{m,n}(q_{1},q_{2})={\mathsf{E}}_{m}(q_{1}){\mathsf{E}}_{n}(q_{2})={\mathsf{E}}_{n}(q_{1}){\mathsf{E}}_{m}(q_{2})={\mathsf{E}}_{n,m}(q_{1},q_{2})\quad\text{$\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2}$-a.e.~in $A$}

we immediately get

\hat{f}_{m,n}=\hat{f}_{n,m}.

On the other hand,
inverting the order of
q_{1},q_{2}
in (
6.38
),
using the invariance of

\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2}

and the anti-symmetry of
f
,
i.e.
f(q_{2},q_{1})=-f(q_{1},q_{2})

we also get

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{m,n}

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathrm{Q}}^{2}}f(q_{2},q_{1}){\mathsf{E}}_{m}(q_{2}){\mathsf{E}}_{n}(q_{1})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2}(q_{1},q_{2})

\displaystyle=-\int_{{\mathrm{Q}}^{2}}f(q_{1},q_{2}){\mathsf{E}}_{m}(q_{2}){\mathsf{E}}_{n}(q_{1})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2}(q_{1},q_{2})=-\hat{f}_{n,m}

We deduce that
\hat{f}_{m,n}=0

for every pair of indexes,
a contradiction since

f
is not identically
0.

∎

The range of application of the
previous Theorem can be considerably extended
thanks to the following
simple results.

Corollary 6.20
.

Let
{\mathcal{N}}:={\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\setminus{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}(\mathbb{R}).

Then the (Borel) set
\iota^{-1}({\mathcal{N}})

is Gaussian-null in
{\mathcal{H}}.

Proof.

Theorem

6.19

shows that

{\mathfrak{g}}(\iota^{-1}({\mathcal{N}}))=0

for every non-degenerate Gaussian
{\mathfrak{g}}
,
so that
\iota^{-1}({\mathcal{N}})

is Gaussian-null by definition.
∎

Corollary 6.21

(Push forward of
G-regular measures are super-G-regular)
.

If
{\mathrm{H}}=\mathbb{R}
and

{\mathfrak{r}}
is a G-regular
measure in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{gr}({\mathcal{H}})
,
then
{\mathtt{R}}:=\iota_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{r}}

is super-G-regular.

Proof.

Recall that as a G-regular measure in

{\mathcal{H}}

{\mathfrak{r}}
satisfies

{\mathfrak{r}}(B)=0\quad\text{for every
Gaussian null Borel set in ${\mathcal{H}}$},

(6.39)

in particular
{\boldsymbol{f}}(\iota^{-1}({\mathcal{N}}))=0,

so that

\iota(X)\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}(\mathbb{R})

for
{\mathfrak{r}}
-a.e.
X\in{\mathcal{H}}.

We concude by Lemma
6.11
.
∎

Let us see two simple examples.

Example 6.22

(Sum of Gaussians with random signs)
.

Let
{\mathrm{Q}}:=\{-1,+1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
be the Cantor set endowed with
the uniform product measure

\mathbb{M}:=(\frac{1}{2}\delta_{-1}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{1})^{\otimes\mathbb{N}}
.
Every element
q\in{\mathrm{Q}}

is a vector
(q_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}

of signs
\pm 1
indexed by
i\in\mathbb{N}.

We denote by
\varepsilon_{i}:{\mathrm{Q}}\to\mathbb{R}
the

i
-th coordinate,
and for every finite subset
I\subset\mathbb{N}
we consider the Walsh function

W_{I}(q):=\prod_{i\in I}\varepsilon_{i},\quad W_{\emptyset}\equiv 1.

(6.40)

If
\mathcal{I}
denotes the (countable) collection of all the finite parts of
\mathbb{N}
,
the Walsh system
(W_{I})_{I\in{\mathcal{I}}}

is a complete orthonormal system in
{\mathcal{H}}=L^{2}({\mathrm{Q}},\mathbb{M})
.

We select a family of independent Gaussian random variables
\xi_{I}\sim N(0,\lambda_{I}^{2})

indexed by
I\in{\mathcal{I}}

and coefficients

\lambda_{I}>0

for every
I\in{\mathcal{I}}

such that
\Lambda:=\sum_{I\in{\mathcal{I}}}\lambda_{I}^{2}<\infty
;
we form the random vector

{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=\sum_{I\in{\mathcal{I}}}\xi_{I}W_{I}\in{\mathcal{H}}
.
Denoting by
{\mathfrak{g}}_{W}
the law of
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
in
{\mathcal{H}}

we obtain
a nondegenerate Gaussian measure
{\mathfrak{g}}_{W}\in{\mathcal{P}}^{r}_{2}({\mathcal{H}})
.
Applying Theorem

6.19

we immediately get

G_{W}:=\iota_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{g}}_{W}

is super-regular.

Example 6.23

(The law of random Fourier series)
.

Let us now select

{\mathrm{Q}}:=(0,\pi)
with
the (normalized) Lebesgue measure
\mathbb{M}.

We consider the complete orthonormal system in
{\mathcal{H}}:=L^{2}(0,\pi)

given by the usual Fourier basis

S_{n}(q):=\sqrt{2}\sin(nq),\quad n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}.

(6.41)

As for the previous example,
we select a sequence of
independent Gaussian random variables

\xi_{n}\sim N(0,\lambda_{n}^{2})

and coefficients

\lambda_{n}>0
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}

with
\Lambda=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{n}^{2}<\infty.

Denoting by
{\mathfrak{g}}_{F}
the law of the random vector

{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\xi_{n}S_{n}

we obtain a nondegenerate Gaussian measure

{\mathfrak{g}}_{F}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}({\mathcal{H}}).

G_{F}:=\iota_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{g}}_{F}

is super-regular.

Notice that the case

\lambda_{n}:=\frac{1}{\pi n}

corresponds to the (centered) Brownian bridge.

The finite dimensional case
{\mathrm{H}}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
d>1

Let us now discuss
the case when
{\mathrm{H}}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
d>1
.
We will still focus on the construction
of suitable Gaussian measures
{\mathfrak{g}}
on
{\mathcal{H}}

such that
G=\iota_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{g}}
is super-regular
and we will consider two different approaches.
Unlike the previous
1
-d case, we will impose
further properties on
{\mathfrak{g}}
.

Example 6.24

(Gaussian measures concentrated on
{\mathrm{C}}^{1}
maps)
.

In this first example, we
select
{\mathrm{Q}}:=(0,1)^{d}

(or any smooth domain in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
)
endowed with the
d
-dimensional Lebesgue measure
\mathbb{M}
.

Theorem 6.25
.

If

{\mathfrak{g}}
is a non-degenerate Gaussian measure on
the Banach space
{\mathcal{B}}={\mathrm{C}}^{1}(\overline{{\mathrm{Q}}};\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset{\mathcal{H}}
,
then

G=\iota_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{g}}
is super-G-regular with full support.

Proof.

As before, we apply Lemma
6.11
.
Since
{\mathfrak{g}}
is a Gaussian non-degenerate measure in
{\mathcal{B}}
and
{\mathcal{B}}
is dense in

{\mathcal{H}}=L^{2}({\mathrm{Q}},\mathbb{M};\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

{\mathfrak{g}}
is clearly a Gaussian nondegenerate measure
in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{gr}({\mathcal{H}})
, so it is sufficient to check that

X_{\sharp}\mathbb{M}\text{ is absolutely continuous for ${\mathfrak{g}}$-a.e. $X\in{\mathcal{B}}$}.

(6.42)

By the area and co-area formulae
(see e.g.
[
GH80
, Thm. 2.3]
),
the push forward
\mu_{X}=X_{\sharp}\mathbb{M}

of a map
X\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}(\overline{{\mathrm{Q}}};\mathbb{R}^{d})

is absolutely continuous if

\mathbb{M}\Big(\big\{q\in{\mathrm{Q}}:\det DX(q)=0\big\}\Big)=0,

(6.43)

so that
(
6.42
) is true if we show that
(
6.43
) holds
for
{\mathfrak{g}}
-a.e.
X\in{\mathcal{B}}.

We can prove this property
by Fubini’s Theorem.
We consider the product measure

\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}:={\mathfrak{g}}\otimes\mathbb{M}
concentrated on

{\mathcal{B}}\times{\mathrm{Q}}
,
we denote by
{\mathsf{D}}:{\mathcal{B}}\times{\mathrm{Q}}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}

the “differential” evaluation map
{\mathsf{D}}(X,q):={\mathrm{D}}X(q)
,
and we introduce the closed set

A:=\Big\{(X,q)\in{\mathcal{B}}\times{\mathrm{Q}}:{\mathrm{D}}X(q)\in S\Big\},\quad S:=\Big\{D\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}:\det D=0\Big\}.

(6.44)

We know that for every
q\in{\mathrm{Q}}

the map
X\mapsto{\mathsf{D}}(X,q)

is linear, continuous, and surjective
from
{\mathcal{B}}
to
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
.
We thus deduce that

{\mathsf{D}}(\cdot,q)_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{g}}
is a nondegenerate Gaussian
measure in
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, so that

{\mathfrak{g}}\Big(\big\{X\in{\mathcal{B}}:{\mathsf{D}}(X,q)\in S\big\}\Big)=0\quad\text{for every $q\in{\mathrm{Q}}$}.

Integrating in
{\mathrm{Q}}
we get

\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}(A)=\int{\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\chi$}}_{A}(X,q)\,{\mathrm{d}}\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}=\int_{{\mathrm{Q}}}{\mathfrak{g}}\big(X\in{\mathcal{B}}:(X,q)\in A\big)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{M}(q)=0.

(6.45)

Applying Fubini’s Theorem we thus deduce that

\int_{{\mathcal{B}}}\mathbb{M}\Big(q\in{\mathrm{Q}}:(X,q)\in A\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathfrak{g}}(X)=0

which yields (
6.43
)
for
{\mathfrak{g}}
-a.e.
X\in{\mathcal{B}}
.
∎

The next example is a natural generalization of
Example
6.17
.

Example 6.26

(The occupation measure
of the fractional Brownian motion)
.

Let
{\mathrm{Q}}=[0,1]
endowed with
the Lebesgue measure; we fix
a Hurst parameter
H<1/d
and we consider
the
d
-dimensional fractional Brownian
motion
(\Xi^{H}_{t})_{t\in{\mathrm{Q}}}

[
Bia+08
]
.
Since
\Xi^{H}

has local time (or, equivalently, its
occupation measure is absolutely continuous
with square integrable density

[
Pit78
,
GH80
]
,

[
Bia+08
, Thm. 10.2.3]
),
its law

{\mathfrak{w}}^{H}
in
{\mathcal{B}}:={\mathrm{C}}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{d})

is a nondegenerate Gaussian measures
satisfying (
6.23
), so that

{\mathtt{W}}^{H}=\iota_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{w}}^{H}\in{\mathcal{P}}\kern-4.5pt{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathrm{H}})

is super-G-regular, according to Lemma

6.11
.

The above example is an application of a general technique due to Berman

[
Ber69
]

and involving the Fourier transform.
In order to explain the main idea in a general case,
let us denote by
\mu_{X}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

the law
\iota(X)=X_{\sharp}\mathbb{M}
of
a generic
element
X\in{\mathcal{H}}
.
The Fourier transform of
\mu_{X}

is the continuous function

\hat{\mu}_{X}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
defined by

\hat{\mu}_{X}(u):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathrm{e}}^{i\,u\cdot x}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{X}(x)=\int_{\mathrm{Q}}{\mathrm{e}}^{i\,u\cdot X(q)}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{M}(q)\quad u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(6.46)

Notice that
(X,u)\mapsto\hat{\mu}_{X}(u)

is jointly continuous in
{\mathcal{H}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
By Plancherel theorem,

\mu_{X}

is absolutely continuous w.r.t. the
d
-dimensional Lebesgue measure in
\mathbb{R}^{d}

with a density
\varrho_{X}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

if and only if

\hat{\mu}_{X}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and moreover

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{X}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\hat{\mu}_{X}(u)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}u

(6.47)

It follows that if
the measure
{\mathfrak{g}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathcal{H}})
satisfies

L^{2}:=\int_{\mathcal{H}}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\hat{\mu}_{X}(u)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}u\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathfrak{g}}(X)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big(\int_{\mathcal{H}}|\hat{\mu}_{X}(u)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathfrak{g}}(X)\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}u<\infty

(6.48)

we get
\mu_{X}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

for
{\mathfrak{g}}
-a.e.
X.

We can rewrite
(
6.48
)
by using the representation
(
2.29
) by the process
\Xi
,
so that
X(\cdot)=\Xi(\omega,\cdot)

and therefore
we can set
\mu_{\omega}=\mu_{\Xi(\omega,\cdot)}
.
We have

\displaystyle|\hat{\mu}_{\omega}(u)|^{2}

\displaystyle=\hat{\mu}_{\omega}(u)\cdot\overline{\hat{\mu}_{\omega}(u)}=\Big(\int_{\mathrm{Q}}{\mathrm{e}}^{i\,u\cdot\Xi(\omega,q_{1})}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{M}(q_{1})\Big)\Big(\int_{\mathrm{Q}}{\mathrm{e}}^{-i\,u\cdot X(\omega,q_{2})}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{M}(q_{2})\Big)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathrm{Q}}^{2}}{\mathrm{e}}^{i\,u\cdot\Xi(\omega,q_{1})}{\mathrm{e}}^{-i\,u\cdot\Xi(\omega,q_{2})}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2}(q_{1},q_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathrm{Q}}^{2}}{\mathrm{e}}^{i\,u\cdot(\Xi(\omega,q_{1})-\Xi(\omega,q_{2}))}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2}(q_{1},q_{2}).

(6.49)

Taking now the expectation
w.r.t.
\mathbb{P}
and integrating in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
w.r.t.
u

we end up with Berman condition

[
GH80
, Thm. 21.9]

L^{2}=\int_{{\mathrm{Q}}^{2}}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\Big[{\mathrm{e}}^{i\,u\cdot(\Xi(\omega,q_{1})-\Xi(\omega,q_{2}))}\Big]\,{\mathrm{d}}u\bigg)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2}(q_{1},q_{2})<\infty

(6.50)

In the particular case
when
\Xi=(\Xi^{1},\cdots,\Xi^{d})

is a Gaussian process
and
the determinant
\Delta(q_{1},q_{2})

of the covariance matrix
of
\Xi(\cdot,q_{1})-\Xi(\cdot,q_{2})
is positive
for a.e.
q_{1},q_{2}
, we end up
with the sufficient
condition for the validity
of (
6.50
)

[
GH80
, Thm. 22.1]

\int_{{\mathrm{Q}}^{2}}\frac{1}{\Delta(q_{1},q_{2})^{d}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2}(q_{1},q_{2})<\infty.

(6.51)

In the case of the fractional Brownian motion
of Example
6.26
we
thus recover the condition
Hd<1.

Example 6.27

(Berman condition for Karhunen-Loève expansions)
.

We slightly modify
the above argument,
by considering
an example inspired to the general framework discussed in Example
2.10
and
based on a complete orthonormal system

{\mathsf{E}}^{\prime}_{n}
of
the Hilbert space

{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}:=L^{2}({\mathrm{Q}},\mathbb{M};\mathbb{R})

of
scalar valued
square summable functions.
If
\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\cdots\boldsymbol{e}_{d}

is an orthogonal basis of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(e.g. the canonical one), we can then form
the complete orthonormal system

{\mathsf{E}}_{n,k}:=\boldsymbol{e}_{k}{\mathsf{E}}^{\prime}_{n}
of

{\mathcal{H}}:=L^{2}({\mathrm{Q}},\mathbb{M};\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

We assign
a sequence
\Sigma_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
of symmetric and positive definite matrices
in
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
satisfying
the boundedness and coercivity condition

0<\alpha_{n}^{2}\leq\Sigma_{n}{\boldsymbol{v}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{v}}\leq\beta_{n}^{2}\quad\text{for every }{\boldsymbol{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\ |{\boldsymbol{v}}|=1;\quad\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\beta_{n}^{2}<\infty.

(6.52)

We assign a sequence of centered
independent Gaussian
random variables in
\mathbb{R}^{d}

\xi_{n}\sim N(0,\Sigma_{n})
;
since
\sum_{n}\beta_{n}^{2}<\infty
,
we can form the random vector

{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=\sum_{n}\xi_{n}{\mathsf{E}}^{\prime}_{n}

(6.53)

corresponding to the
the measurable process

\Xi(\omega,q)=\sum_{n}\xi_{n}(\omega){\mathsf{E}}^{\prime}_{n}(q).

It is clear that
{\mathfrak{g}}={\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}

is a nondegenerate Gaussian in
{\mathcal{H}}
.
As in (
6.34
)
we consider the functions

\alpha^{2}(q_{1},q_{2})=\sum_{n}\alpha_{n}^{2}\Big({\mathsf{E}}^{\prime}_{n}(q_{1})-{\mathsf{E}}^{\prime}_{n}(q_{2})\Big)^{2},\quad\beta^{2}(q_{1},q_{2})=\sum_{n}\beta_{n}^{2}\Big({\mathsf{E}}^{\prime}_{n}(q_{1})-{\mathsf{E}}^{\prime}_{n}(q_{2})\Big)^{2}

(6.54)

formed with the orthonormal system
of the scalar-valued
L^{2}
- space

{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
.
We have already seen as a particular
consequence of the calculations of Theorem

6.19

that
\alpha^{2}(q_{1},q_{2})>0

a.e. if
q_{1}\neq q_{2}
.

Theorem 6.28
.

If

\int_{{\mathrm{Q}}^{2}}\frac{1}{\alpha^{d}(q_{1},q_{2})}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{M}(q_{1}){\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{M}(q_{2})<\infty

(6.55)

then
{\mathtt{G}}=\iota_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{g}}
is super-G-regular.

Notice that the integral in (
6.55
) can be restricted to the complement
of the diagonal
D_{\mathrm{Q}}
in
{\mathrm{Q}}^{2}
, since

\mathbb{M}
is atomless.

Proof.

We want to prove that
(
6.50
) holds.
We first integrate
(
6.49
)
with respect to
\mathbb{P},
obtaining

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}|\hat{\mu}_{\omega}(u)|^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\bigg(\int_{{\mathrm{Q}}^{2}}{\mathrm{e}}^{i\,u\cdot(\Xi(\omega,q_{1})-\Xi(\omega,q_{2}))}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2}(q_{1},q_{2})\bigg)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega)

(6.56)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathrm{Q}}^{2}}\bigg(\int_{\Omega}{\mathrm{e}}^{i\,u\cdot(\Xi(\omega,q_{1})-\Xi(\omega,q_{2}))}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega)\bigg)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2}(q_{1},q_{2})

(6.57)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathrm{Q}}^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\bigg[{\mathrm{e}}^{i\,u\cdot(\Xi(\cdot,q_{1})-\Xi(\cdot,q_{2}))}\bigg]\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2}(q_{1},q_{2}).

(6.58)

We now observe that
for
\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2}
-a.e.
(q_{1},q_{2})

\beta^{2}(q_{1},q_{2})
is finite so that
the expression

D(\omega;q_{1},q_{2}):=\Xi(\omega,q_{1})-\Xi(\omega,q_{2})=\sum_{n}\xi_{n}(\omega)[{\mathsf{E}}^{\prime}_{n}(q)-{\mathsf{E}}^{\prime}_{n}(q_{2})]

(6.59)

is a series of
independent Gaussian variables
pointwise converging
\mathbb{P}
-a.e.
Its sum is a Gaussian random variable
with covariance matrix

\Sigma(q_{1},q_{2}):=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\Sigma_{n}[{\mathsf{E}}^{\prime}_{n}(q)-{\mathsf{E}}^{\prime}_{n}(q_{2})]^{2},

(6.60)

satisfying

\alpha^{2}(q_{1},q_{2})\leq\Sigma(q_{1},q_{2}){\boldsymbol{v}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{v}}\leq\beta^{2}(q_{1},q_{2})\quad\text{for every }{\boldsymbol{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\ |{\boldsymbol{v}}|=1,

(6.61)

so that

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\bigg[{\mathrm{e}}^{i\,u\cdot(\Xi(\cdot,q_{1})-\Xi(\cdot,q_{2}))}\bigg]=\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma(q_{1},q_{2})u\cdot u\Big).

(6.62)

Combining (
6.62
)
with (
6.57
) we get

\int_{\mathcal{H}}|\hat{\mu}_{X}(u)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathfrak{g}}(X)=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}|\hat{\mu}_{\omega}(u)|^{2}=\int_{{\mathrm{Q}}^{2}}\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma(q_{1},q_{2})u\cdot u\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2}(q_{1},q_{2}).

(6.63)

We
can plug (
6.63
) in (
6.48
)
obtaining
after a further application of Fubini’s Theorem

\displaystyle L^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big(\int_{{\mathrm{Q}}^{2}}\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma(q_{1},q_{2})u\cdot u\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2}(q_{1},q_{2})\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}u

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathrm{Q}}^{2}}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma(q_{1},q_{2})u\cdot u\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}u\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2}(q_{1},q_{2})

Since the inner integral is

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma(q_{1},q_{2})u\cdot u\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}u\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}\alpha^{2}(q_{1},q_{2})|u|^{2}\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}u\leq\frac{(2\pi)^{d/2}}{\alpha^{d}(q_{1},q_{2})}

we conclude that

L^{2}\leq(2\pi)^{d/2}\int_{{\mathrm{Q}}^{2}}\frac{1}{\alpha^{d}(q_{1},q_{2})}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2}(q_{1},q_{2}),

so that
L^{2}
is finite
if and only if (
6.55
)
holds.
∎

We apply the above result
to the
d
-dimensional version of
Example (
6.22
).

Example 6.29

(Sum of
d
-dimensional Gaussians with random signs)
.

Let
{\mathrm{Q}},\mathbb{M},

and the Walsh system
W_{I}
as
in Example (
6.22
):
they form a complete orthonormal system
for the “scalar” Hilbert space

{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}=L^{2}({\mathrm{Q}},\mathbb{M};\mathbb{R})
.
We now select a family of independent
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-Gaussian random variables

\xi_{I}\sim N(0,\Sigma_{I})
as in the previous discussion with

0<\alpha^{2}_{I}\leq\Sigma_{I}{\boldsymbol{v}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{v}}\leq\beta_{I}^{2}\quad\text{for every }{\boldsymbol{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\quad B^{2}=\sum_{I}\beta_{I}^{2}<\infty.

(6.64)

As in (
6.53
)
we consider the random vector

{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=\sum_{I\in{\mathcal{I}}}\xi_{I}W_{I}\quad\text{with}\quad{\mathfrak{g}}={\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}.

(6.65)

We decompose
the set
{\mathcal{I}}
of finite parts of
\mathbb{N}_{+}

in the disjoint union of

{\mathcal{I}}_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
with

{\mathcal{I}}_{0}=\{\emptyset\},\quad{\mathcal{I}}_{n}:=\Big\{I\in{\mathcal{I}}:\max I=n\Big\},\quad n>0,

(6.66)

i.e.

{\mathcal{I}}_{1}=\big\{\{1\}\big\},\ {\mathcal{I}}_{2}=\big\{\{2\},\{1,2\}\big\},\ {\mathcal{I}}_{3}=\big\{\{3\},\{2,3\},\{1,3\},\{1,2,3\}\big\},\cdots

Notice that for every
I\in{\mathcal{I}}_{n}

the corresponding Walsh function
can be factorized as

W_{I}=\varepsilon_{n}W_{I^{\prime}}\quad\text{for some $I^{\prime}\subset\{1,\cdots,n-1\}.$}

(6.67)

For each
{\mathcal{I}}_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we compute the
contribution of
\alpha_{I}^{2}

to the total sum

A^{2}_{n}:=\sum_{I\in{\mathcal{I}}_{n}}\alpha_{I}^{2},\quad\text{so that}\quad A^{2}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}A_{n}^{2}=\sum_{I}\alpha_{I}^{2}\leq B^{2}.

(6.68)

The next result shows that

G=\iota_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{g}}

is super-G-regular if
A_{n}
does not decay
too fast.

Theorem 6.30
.

If

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{n}A_{n}^{d}}<\infty

(6.69)

then
{\mathtt{G}}=\iota_{\sharp}{\mathfrak{g}}

is super-G-regular.

Notice that
in the simplest
case when
\Sigma_{I}=\lambda_{I}{\mathsf{I}}_{d\times d}

and
\alpha_{I}=\beta_{I}=\lambda_{I}
, asymptotic behaviours as

A_{n}\sim n^{-\theta}
with
\theta>1/2

or
A_{n}\sim a^{-n}

with
1<a<2^{1/d}

comply with (
6.69
)
and the summability of
n\mapsto A_{n}^{2}

(corresponding to
\sum_{I}\beta_{I}^{2}<\infty
).

Proof.

We are in the setting of Theorem
(
6.28
),
so it is sufficient to check
that (
6.55
) holds,
where in our case

\alpha^{2}(q_{1},q_{2})=\sum_{I\in{\mathcal{I}}}\alpha_{I}^{2}\Big(W_{I}(q_{1})-W_{I}(q_{2})\Big)^{2}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\sum_{I\in{\mathcal{I}}_{n}}\alpha_{I}^{2}\Big(W_{I}(q_{1})-W_{I}(q_{2})\Big)^{2}

(6.70)

For every pair
(q_{1},q_{2})\in{\mathrm{Q}}^{2}
with
q_{1}\neq q_{2}

let us denote by

N(q_{1},q_{2})

the first integer
n\in N_{+}

such that
\varepsilon_{n}(q_{1})\neq\varepsilon_{n}(q_{2})
:

N(q_{1},q_{2}):=\min\Big\{n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}:\varepsilon_{n}(q_{1})\neq\varepsilon_{n}(q_{2})\Big\}.

(6.71)

Since
q_{1}\neq q_{2}

the set in (
6.71
)
is not empty, so that
N(q_{1},q_{2})

is well defined.
Since
\varepsilon_{k}(q_{1})=\varepsilon_{k}(q_{2})

for every
k<N(q_{1},q_{2})

and therefore

W_{I^{\prime}}(q_{1})=W_{I^{\prime}}(q_{2})

for every
I^{\prime}\subset\{1,\cdots,N(q_{1},q_{2})-1\}
.
Therefore the factorization
(
6.67
)
shows that

N=N(q_{1},q_{2}),\quad I\in{\mathcal{I}}_{N}\quad\Rightarrow\quad W_{I}(q_{1})=\varepsilon_{N}(q_{1})W_{I^{\prime}(q_{1})}\neq W_{I}(q_{2})=\varepsilon_{N}(q_{2})W_{I^{\prime}(q_{1})}

(6.72)

so that

\alpha^{2}(q_{1},q_{2})\geq\sum_{I\in{\mathcal{I}}_{N}}\alpha_{I}^{2}\Big(W_{I}(q_{1})-W_{I}(q_{2})\Big)^{2}=4\sum_{I\in{\mathcal{I}}_{N}}\alpha_{I}^{2}=4A^{2}_{N}

(6.73)

so that

\int_{{\mathrm{Q}}^{2}}\frac{1}{\alpha^{d}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2}\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{A_{n}^{d}}\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2}\Big[\{(q_{1},q_{2})\in{\mathrm{Q}}^{2}:N(q_{1},q_{2})=n\}\Big].

(6.74)

Recall now that

\varepsilon_{n}
are independent
and

\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2}[\varepsilon_{k}(q_{1})\neq\varepsilon_{k}(q_{2})]=\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2}[\varepsilon_{k}(q_{1})=\varepsilon_{k}(q_{2})]=1/2
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
.
We thus obtain

\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2}\Big[\{(q_{1},q_{2})\in{\mathrm{Q}}^{2}:N(q_{1},q_{2})=n\}\Big]=\frac{1}{2^{n}}

and inserting this expression in (
6.74
)
we eventually get

\int_{{\mathrm{Q}}^{2}}\frac{1}{\alpha^{d}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{M}^{\otimes 2}\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{n}A_{n}^{d}}<+\infty

thanks to (
6.69
).
∎
